COMMENTS OF SIERRA CLUB AND EARTHJUSTICE 
 
I. 
INTRODUCTION 
More than a decade after the statutory deadline, EPA has issued a proposed review and 
revision of its air toxics emission standards for primary copper smelters under Clean Air 
Act § 112(d)(6) and § 112(f)(2). 
There are currently three primary copper smelters operating in the United States. Of 
these, two are by far the worst polluters. Both are located in close proximity to the San 
Carlos Apache Reservation. The Asarco smelter, owned by Grupo Mexico, is located in 
Hayden, Arizona, less than 5 miles from the Reservation. Freeport’s Miami, Arizona 
smelter is located less than 8 miles from the Reservation. 
In its Proposed Residual Risk Assessment (RRA) for copper smelters, EPA states that 
these two smelters alone emit “approximately 21 tons per year” of hazardous air 
pollutants.1 The bulk of these emissions, according to EPA, are toxic metals.2 The 21 ton 
per year (tpy) total includes 13 tpy of lead and 2 tpy of arsenic, according to EPA.3 
At these levels, the Freeport and Asarco smelters are among the worst lead emitters in 
the United States. To put their emissions into perspective, secondary lead smelters – long 
recognized as one of the worst lead emission sources – emit less than one half of one ton 
per year.4 By EPA’s estimates, the Freeport and Asarco smelters collectively emit more 
lead than 24 secondary lead smelters. 
Reports to EPA’s Toxics Release Inventory by the smelters’ owners indicate their 
 
1 EPA, Residual Risk Assessment for the Primary Copper Smelting Source Category in 
Support of the 2021 Risk and Technology Review Proposed Rule (February 2021), EPA-
HQ-OAR-2020-0430-0051 (RRA) at 5. 
2  Id. 
3 Id. at 38 (Table 3.1-1), Appendix 1, Emissions Data Used for Primary Copper Smelting 
Risk and Technology Review (RTR) Modeling Files, at A-4 – A-5. 
4 77 Fed. Reg. 563, 575 (January 5, 2012). 

2 
 
emissions are even higher. In 2020, for example, the Freeport smelter alone reported 
emitting more than 14 tons of lead.5 In 2019, it reported emitting 21 tons of lead and, in 
2018, it reported emitting more than 29 tons of lead – more than twice the amount EPA 
estimates for all copper smelters put together.6 Moreover, there is strong reason to believe 
that these smelters’ future emissions will be higher than their present or past emissions. 
Smelters’ emissions are largely a function of their production levels, and copper production 
levels are certain to increase significantly. Copper is critical to our clean energy pathway, 
and its use in electric cars, new transmission lines, wind energy generation, and solar 
energy generation will grow as the U.S. builds toward a clean energy economy.  “An 
energy system powered by clean energy technologies differs profoundly from one fueled 
by traditional hydrocarbon resources. Solar photovoltaic (PV) plants, wind farms and 
electric vehicles (EVs) generally require more minerals to build than their fossil fuel-based 
counterparts. A typical electric car requires six times the mineral inputs of a conventional 
car and an onshore wind plant requires nine times more mineral resources than a gas-fired 
plant. Since 2010 the average amount of minerals needed for a new unit of power 
generation capacity has increased by 50% as the share of renewables in new investment has 
risen.”7 Copper smelters’ production will increase and, as it does, their toxic pollution will 
increase as well unless EPA acts now to require far better pollution controls      
EPA has known for decades that the metals emitted by copper smelters are highly 
toxic. The agency recognized in its original 2002 rule for copper smelters that arsenic and 
nickel are human carcinogens and that lead, cadmium, and beryllium are probable human 
carcinogens.8 “Exposure of humans to arsenic by inhalation or by ingestion has been 
shown to be associated with forms of lung, bladder, liver, and other cancers. Brain damage, 
kidney damage, and gastrointestinal distress may occur from acute exposure to high levels 
of lead in humans. Chronic exposure to lead by humans results in effects on the central 
nervous system, blood, blood pressure, and kidneys.”9 
Subsequent information shows that the health impacts are even worse than EPA knew 
in 2002. EPA has stated that “[o]nce taken into the body, lead distributes throughout the 
body in the blood and is accumulated in the bones. Depending on the level of exposure, 
lead can adversely affect the nervous system, kidney function, immune system, 
reproductive and developmental systems and the cardiovascular system. Lead exposure 
also affects the oxygen carrying capacity of the blood. The lead effects most likely to be 
encountered in current populations are neurological effects in children. Infants and young 
children are especially sensitive to lead exposures, which may contribute to behavioral 
problems, learning deficits and lowered IQ.”10

The World Health Organization has found that even “[a]t lower levels of exposure that 
cause no obvious symptoms, lead is now known to produce a spectrum of injury across 
multiple body systems.”11 “In particular, lead can affect children’s brain development, 
resulting in reduced intelligence quotient (IQ), behavioural changes such as reduced 
attention span and increased antisocial behaviour, and reduced educational attainment. 
Lead exposure also causes anaemia, hypertension, renal impairment, immunotoxicity and 
toxicity to the reproductive organs. The neurological and behavioural effects of lead are 
believed to be irreversible.”12 
Emissions of lead, arsenic, and other hazardous air pollutants from the Asarco and 
Freeport smelters put people in nearby communities – and especially children – at risk of 
serious health and developmental harm. The threat is particularly great on the San Carlos 
Apache Reservation because these smelters have been operating, and emitting toxic 
pollution, in the same locations – just a few miles from the Reservation – for more than a 
century. Many of the pollutants they emit, including lead, arsenic, and dioxins, are both 
persistent in the environment and bioaccumulative, and have built up on the Reservation 
for more than a century.  
 
The exposure of people in nearby communities to the smelters’ current emissions in the 
air is exacerbated by exposure to previous years and decades of pollution that contaminate 
water, soil, playgrounds, gardens, and food. Not only are the Tribe and surrounding 
communities affected by both the fugitive and stack emissions from these two smelters, but 
they are also affected by resuspension of the legacy lead which has already been deposited 
in the environment. Fully half of the tribal members are under the age of twenty, when 
exposures to these heavy metals can do the most long-term damage to their 
health.  Moreover, a significant percentage of the tribal households live below the poverty 
line; this is an environmental justice community who lands and future are being damaged 
by these toxic emissions. EPA must take strong actions to reduce this pollution if it it to 
avoid irreparably harming the Tribe’s lands, people, and future. 
Regrettably, EPA’s proposed RTR will do little to reduce these exposures. EPA 
states that its proposed rule would reduce copper smelters’ toxic emissions by 4.26 tpy, or 
approximately 20 percent.13 By EPA’s estimates it will allow copper smelters to continue 
emitting approximately 17 tons of hazardous air pollutants, including almost 11 tons of 
lead and almost 1.8 tons of arsenic each year. As before, these emissions will be emitted 
almost entirely from two smelters that are both located in close proximity to the San Carlos 
Apache Reservation. 
 
Based on the emission estimates that Asarco and Freeport, continuing toxic 
emissions will be even higher. The lead emissions Asarco reported to TRI from 2015-2017 
(before it shut down for most of 2018), averaged 6506 pounds per year.14 The average lead 
emissions from the Freeport-McMoran smelter from 2018-2020 were 43,033 pounds. Thus, 
 
11 Fact Sheet, Lead Poisoning, WHO (Oct. 11, 2021), https://www.who.int/news-
room/fact-sheets/detail/lead-poisoning-and-health .  
12 Id. 
13 87 Fed. Reg. 1616, 1650 (January 11, 2022). 
14 TRI Spreadsheets showing Freeport’s and Asarco’s reported emissions of lead 
compounds, Ex. A hereto. 

4 
 
although the incomplete data for the Asarco smelter make a direct year-by-year comparison 
impossible, the average combined lead emissions from these two smelters in recent years is 
approximately 25 tons per year. Reducing these emissions by 20 percent would allow them 
to continue to emitting 20 tons of lead each year. 
Needless to say, if copper smelters’ production increases in the next years as 
expected, their emissions of lead, arsenic and other toxics will be even higher. To allow the 
continued emissions of 11 tons, 20 tons, or even more lead into the San Carlos Apache 
Reservation is an environmental injustice. These emissions exceed, by orders of 
magnitude, lead emissions almost anywhere else in the country. According to its TRI 
reports, the Freeport smelter is the single worst lead emitter of any kind in the entire 
country.15 
It is long past time to finally require copper smelters to reduce their toxic emissions 
by the maximum achievable degree. Far greater reductions than EPA proposes are both 
technologically and economically feasible. To provide just one example, EPA states that 
the Freeport smelter could reduce its emissions of metals by 7.6 tons by installing a wet 
electrostatic precipitator (“wet ESP”) on its main stack.16 This single reduction measure at 
an emissions point EPA describes as “contributing significantly to risk at Freeport” would 
eliminate more than one third of the entire category’s total hazardous air pollutant 
emissions and substantially reduce the accumulation of lead and other highly toxic 
pollution in the San Carlos Apache Reservation. There is no dispute that the use of a wet 
ESP is technically achievable, and the agency has found that it would cost, on an 
annualized basis, only $4.8 million, less than one tenth of one percent of Freeport’s annual 
revenues.17 
Finally, it bears emphasis that by not requiring achievable reductions in lead and 
other metal emissions from copper smelters, EPA would leave the United States in 
violation of the Heavy Metals Protocol of the Convention on Long-Range Transboundary 
Air Pollution, to which the United States is a signatory.18 
II. 
EPA’s Proposed Technology Review 
Clean Air Act § 112(d)(6) requires EPA to review its air toxics standards every 
eight years and revise them “as necessary (taking into account developments in practices, 
processes and control technologies). The purpose of this provision is to ensure “EPA 
maintains source standards compliant with the law and on pace with emerging 
developments that create opportunities to do even better.”19 Although the D.C. Circuit has 
 
15 TRI Spreadsheet showing emissions of 100 worst lead emitters. Ex. A hereto. 
16 87 Fed. Reg. at 1643. 
17 EPA, Evaluation of Beyond-the-floor and Ample Margin of Safety Control Options and 
Costs for Process Fugitive Emissions from Smelting Furnaces and Converters, and for 
Point Source Emissions from Anode Refining Furnaces and for the Combined Emissions 
Stream Emitted from the Freeport Aisle Scrubber (November 16, 2020), EPA-HQ-OAR-
2020-0430-0061 (“AMOS Memo”), at 6-7. See also 87 Fed. Reg. at 1635 (showing that 
$1,558,000 is just 0.016 percent of the annual revenue of Freeport’s ultimate parent 
company). 
18 https://unece.org/sites/default/files/2021-10/1998.Heavy_.Metals.e.pdf 
19 Louisiana Environmental Action Network v. EPA, 955 F.3d 1088, 1093 (D.C. Cir. 2020) 

5 
 
held that § 112(d)(6) does not require EPA to recalculate the statutory minimum 
stringencies (“floors”) of its emission standards every eight years, the mandate to revise 
them “as necessary” does require EPA “to ‘revise’ an emission standard “as necessary” to 
bring it into compliance with the Act’s own definition in section 112(d) (1)-(3) of a 
minimally adequate ‘emission standard.’”20 The Act creates a clear statutory obligation for 
standards that reduce each listed hazardous air pollutant by the maximum degree that is 
achievable.21 Thus, in its § 112(d)(6) rulemakings, EPA must, at a minimum, ensure that 
its standards for a source category include limits for each hazardous air pollutant that 
category emits and that these limits require the “maximum” degree of reduction in 
emissions of these hazardous air pollutants that is “achievable” considering cost.22  
EPA does not propose to strengthen the standards in its § 112(d)(6) review all. The 
agency’s stated rationale is that “[w]ith regard to the emissions sources at major primary 
copper smelting facilities, including sources of fugitive dust emissions, the Agency did 
not identify any developments in practices, processes, or control technologies beyond those 
described under the ample margin of safety analysis above.”23 EPA also states there have 
been “no developments in practices, processes, or control technologies were identified that 
would reduce HAP emissions from copper concentrate dryers, smelting furnaces, slag 
cleaning vessels, matte drying and grinding, copper converting, secondary gas systems, and 
fugitive emission sources.”24 
EPA’s failure to strengthen its standards under § 112(d)(6) would be unlawful and 
arbitrary for at least two reasons. First, there are “developments in practices and processes 
and control technologies” as EPA itself defines the term. Second, EPA’s professed belief 
that there are no developments, even if correct, would not excuse the agency from bringing 
its emission limits into compliance with the Clean Air Act. 
EPA takes the position that the following things are considered “developments”: 
 
• 
Any add-on control technology or other equipment that was not identified and 
considered during development of the original MACT standards;  
• 
Any improvements in add-on control technology or other equipment (that were 
identified and considered during development of the original MACT standards) that 
could result in significant additional emissions reduction; 
• 
Any work practice or operational procedure that was not identified or considered 
during development of the original MACT standards;  
• 
Any process changes or pollution prevention alternatives that could be broadly 
applied to the industry and that were not identified or considered during 
development of the original MACT standards; and  
 
(“LEAN”). 
20 Id. at 1098. 
21 42 U.S.C. § 7412(d)(1)-(3). 
22 42 U.S.C. § 7412(d)(1)-(2); LEAN, 55 F.3d at 1089 (citing National Lime Ass’n v. EPA, 
233 F.3d 625, 634 (D.C. Cir. 2000). 
23 87 Fed. Reg. at 1645. 
24 EPA, Technology Review for the Primary Copper Smelting Source Category (January 
20, 2021), EPA-HQ-OAR-2020-0430-0033 (“Tech Review Memo”) at 21. 

6 
 
• 
Any significant changes in the cost (including cost effectiveness) of applying add-
on control technology or other equipment to affected sources (including controls 
the EPA considered during the development of the original MACT standards).25 
 
Because the Clean Air Act directs EPA to take account of practices, processes and control 
technologies without limitation, EPA’s attempt to narrow the universe of developments it 
must take account of to those it lists is unlawful. 
 
Even accepting EPA’s definition of developments, however, there are important 
developments that would significantly reduce copper smelters’ emissions of hazardous air 
pollutants. 
 
As EPA explains the Kennecott smelter in Utah uses a Outotec smelting and 
converting process that is both continuous and closed.26 EPA states “molten copper matte 
tapped from the Outotec smelting furnace is not transferred as molten material directly to 
the converting vessel as is performed at the two major source smelters. Instead, the matte is 
first quenched with water to form solid granules of copper matte. … Because there are no 
transfers of molten material between the smelting furnace and the continuous copper 
converter, this technology has inherently lower potential HAP emissions than a smelter 
using batch copper converting technology.”27 “Due to the Outotec closed process and lack of 
molten material transfer in open ladles between the smelting, converting, and anode refining 
steps, most process fugitive emissions are eliminated.”28 Of the continuous smelting and 
converting process, EPA states “[t]he design reduces the process fugitive emissions when 
compared to batch converters. With continuous converting there are no transfers of molten 
material via open ladles between smelting, converting, and the anode furnace departments. In 
addition, there are no fugitive emissions associated with the repeated rolling-out of converters 
for charging, skimming, and pouring.”29  
 
The Kennecott smelter then routes emissions from its continuous process to a series 
of control devices, a waste heat boiler (WHB), an electrostatic precipitator (ESP), a wet 
scrubber, a wet ESP, and then the acid plant.30  
 
The Kennecott smelter’s combination of a far superior smelting process and control 
technologies constitute “developments” as EPA itself defines the term. EPA defines 
developments to include both “[a]ny add-on control technology or other equipment that 
was not identified and considered during development of the original MACT standards”  
and “[a]ny improvements in add-on control technology or other equipment (that were 
identified and considered during development of the original MACT standards) that could 
result in significant additional emissions reduction.”31 The Kennecott smelter’s system 
meets both of these tests. EPA itself describes this combination as a “Potentially New or 
 
25 Id. at 1, 19-20. 
26 Tech Review Memo at 17-18. 
27 87 Fed. Reg. at 1620. 
28 Tech Review Memo at 5. 
29 Id. at 7. 
30 Tech Review Memo at 5, 7, 17. 
31 Id. at 5, 19-20. 

7 
 
Improved Control Technology.”32 It was not “identified and considered” as a control 
technology “during development of the original MACT standards.” EPA excluded the 
Kennecott smelter from its floor analysis under § 112(d)(3) and did not consider the 
Outotec continuous system in its beyond-the-floor analysis under § 112(d)(2).33 
 
Further, even if EPA had considered the Kennecott smelter’s system in 2002, which 
it did not, this technology undisputedly could “result in significant emissions reduction.” In 
particular, it “has inherently lower potential HAP emissions than a smelter using batch 
copper converting technology,”34 eliminates most process fugitive emissions.35 
 
Kennecott’s closed and continuous smelting and converting process is also a 
“process change[] or pollution prevention alternative[] that could be broadly applied to the 
industry and that were not identified or considered during development of the original 
MACT standards.”36 EPA does not claim otherwise. 
 
EPA suggests the Kennecott’s process and control technology combination does not 
constitute a development but relies on arguments that are irrelevant under its own definition of 
the term development. Specifically, EPA states “[a] BACT analysis was prepared in 2017 for 
the Kennecott Utah facility as input to Utah’s State Implementation Plan (SIP) and did not 
identify any new major developments in technologies or costs have occurred after 
promulgation of the MACT standards.” EPA then states 
 
The primary copper smelting area source MACT standard (Subpart EEEEEE) 
specifically identifies the Smelter main stack emission performance as MACT for 
copper smelters (existing sources not using batch copper converters). Smelter process 
and emission controlling technologies that contributed to EPA’s designation of the 
modernized Smelter as a separate MACT category for HAP emissions, including off-
gases from furnaces, also contribute to the control of fine particulate and precursor 
emissions. No new major developments in technologies or costs have occurred after 
promulgation of the MACT standards.” (UDAQ, 2018a).37 
 
As EPA states, the 2017 analysis it cites is a “BACT” analysis – i.e., not a MACT 
analysis done for the purpose of § 112 standards. Section 112 MACT standards apply to a 
completely different set of pollutants (the hazardous air pollutants listed in § 112(b)) and 
provide different requirements (the reduction of each listed hazardous air pollutant by the 
“maximum” degree that is “achievable”). EPA does not and cannot claim that the set of 
developments for the purpose of a 2007 BACT standard necessarily encompass all 
developments for the purpose of a 2002 MACT standard, and the record provides no basis for 
any such assumption. 
 
Further, the BACT analysis is of the “area source” (Subpart EEEEE) standards that 
EPA promulgated in 2007, not the major source (Subpart QQQ). EPA’s conclusions about 
 
32 Id. at 16-17. 
33 67 Fed. Reg. 40,478, 40,486 (June 12, 2002).  
34 87 Fed. Reg. at 1620. 
35 Tech Review Memo at 5. 
36 Id. 
37 Id. at 17 (emphasis added). 

8 
 
what constitutes a development for the purpose of the 2007 area source standards do not even 
purport to apply the 2002 major source standards. The latter were promulgated 5 years earlier 
and EPA’s rationale for them is completely different than the rationale if proffered for the 2007 
area source standards. 
 
Assuming for discussion purposes that the Kennecott smelter’s system was evaluated 
for the 2007 area source MACT rule, it was not identified and considered as a means for 
reduction in the 2002 major source rule. Even if it had been, EPA acknowledges it is an 
improvement over the systems used by Asarco and Freeport and could result in significant 
emission reductions.    
 
The individual elements of the Kennecott smelter’s system are also developments 
as EPA defines the term, for all the reasons given above. 
 
The Outotec continuous smelting and conversion process, in particular, is a 
development. It is a technology identified by EPA in the Tech Review Memo and, although 
it was installed before EPA promulgated the 2002 standards, EPA did not identify or 
consider it as a control measure during development of the original MACT standards.38 
Even if EPA had identified and considered the Outotec system as a control measure 
“during development of the original MACT standards,” it undisputedly is an improvement 
over the systems currently used by Asarco and Freeport that could “result in significant 
emissions reduction.” 
 
A wet ESP is also a development within EPA’s definition of the term. EPA did not 
consider them as a control measure in promulgating the 2002 standards. Even if EPA had 
considered wet ESPs as a control measure in promulgating its 2002 standards, modern wet 
ESPs are an improvement in add-on control technology that could result in significant 
additional emissions reduction. As EPA itself points out in its discussion of the ample 
margin of safety under § 112(f)(2), a wet ESP would essentially eliminate emissions of 
metals from the main emission point at the Freeport, the Aisle Scrubber, reducing the 
plants emissions of these pollutants by 7.6 tons each year.39 
 
EPA claims wet ESP “were identified,” is not a “new” technology, and was “in use in 
the industry during development the NESHAP for both major sources and minor sources.”40 
EPA does not specify where wet ESPs are identified in the 2002 rule but, even it had been 
identified there, EPA’s claim that wet ESPs are not a “new” technology is irrelevant under 
EPA’s definition of the term. What matters is whether wet ESP were identified and 
considered during development of the original MACT standards. EPA does not claim wet 
ESPs were “considered” in the development of the 2002 rule, and does not specify any part 
of the 2002 rule in which wet ESP were even “identified.” 
 
In its ample margin of safety analysis under § 112(f)(2), EPA rejects wet ESP based 
on arguments about costs. EPA does not offer any cost rationale for rejecting wet ESP 
 
38 Id. at 16-17, 1, 19-20. 
39 87 Fed. Reg. at 1643; AMOS Memo at 6-7. 
40 Tech Review Memo at 21. 

9 
 
under § 112(d)(6), and the cost arguments it makes under § 112(f)(2) do not apply under 
§ 112(d)(6). 
 
Assuming arguendo that the arguments EPA advances for rejecting a wet ESP in its 
ample margin of safety analysis could be applied to its proposed actions under 112(d)(6), 
those arguments are without merit. Not only are wet ESPs a development under EPA’s 
own definition of the term, standards reflecting the use of wet ESP would be “achievable” 
within the meaning of the Clean Air Act and under EPA’s own approach to implementing 
§ 112(d)(6). 
 
First, there is no dispute that wet ESP are technically achievable; they are already in 
use at copper smelters. Second, the cost of wet ESP does not prevent them from being 
achievable. EPA states that the capital cost of a wet ESP is $36 million, the annualized cost 
is $4.8 million. Elsewhere in the proposal, EPA states that the purported annualized cost of 
the rule for Freeport, $2.3 million, would reflect just “0.016 percent” of the annual revenue 
of the Freeport smelter’s ultimate parent company.41 If $2.3 million is just 0.016 percent of 
it, Freeport’s annual revenue is $14,475,000,000 -- approximately $14.48 billion. The $4.8 
million annualized cost of a wet ESP would reflect only 0.033 percent of Freeport’s 
annualized revenue, and Freeport could easily afford it. Thus it is achievable economically 
as well. 
 
EPA also states that that the cost of installing a wet ESP on Freeport’s Aisle 
Scrubber per ton of metal hazardous air pollution removed is $632,000.42 That number is 
based on dividing EPA’s estimate of the annualized cost, $4.8 million by the EPA’s 
estimate the resulting annual reductions in metal hazardous air pollutants, 7.6 tons.43 
Although the cost per ton of a emission limit is not relevant to whether that limit is 
“achievable” – let alone whether it reflects the “maximum” degree of reduction that is 
achievable – EPA has regularly used cost per ton as a cost measure in determining whether 
to set beyond the floor standards. In the past, EPA has found far higher cost per ton to be 
acceptable for metal hazardous air pollutant reductions. Specifically, as EPA notes in the 
proposal, it has found that a cost per ton of $1.3 million (in 2009 dollars) is acceptable for 
metal hazardous air pollutants (mostly lead, just as here).44 EPA does not claim, let alone 
explain, why it would not regard $632,000 per ton acceptable given that it has already 
found that $1.3 million – more than twice as much – for reducing metal hazardous air 
pollutants. 
 
Instead, EPA relies on inaccurate characterizations of the capital cost, annual cost, 
and cost-per-ton of a wet ESP in rejecting limits based on this technology in it ample 
margin of safety analysis. Specifically, EPA claims there that the capital cost of a wet ESP 
would be $50 million, the annualized costs would be $13 million, and the “cost-
effectiveness” would be $1.7 million per ton of metal hazardous air pollutants reduced.45 
Those claims are inconsistent with – and far higher than – EPA’s estimate of the cost of 
 
41 87 Fed. Reg. at 1651. 
42 AMOS Memo at 6-7. 
43 Id. 
44 87 Fed. Reg. at 1635. 
45 Id. 

10 
 
installing a wet ESP on the Freeport smelter elsewhere in the AMOS Memo, where EPA 
states the capital cost of a wet ESP is $36 million, the annualized costs would be only $4.8 
million, and the cost per ton would be $632,000 per ton of metal hazardous air pollutants 
reduced.46 Nowhere does EPA explain why it is providing inconsistent and far higher cost 
numbers in the proposal than it provides in the AMOS memo. Nor does it provide any 
support whatsoever for the higher cost numbers in its proposal. 
 
The claims EPA makes in its proposal are not only inconsistent with those in the 
AMOS memo, but also with EPA’s claims about the cost of wet ESPs elsewhere in the 
proposal. In discussing beyond the floor standards for anode refining furnaces, EPA uses 
the same $36 million capital cost and $4.8 million annualized cost numbers that it provides 
in the AMOS memo.47 There, EPA asserts the capital cost of installing two wet ESPS 
would be $72 million and that the annualized cost would be $9.6 million.48 Both numbers 
are exactly twice the cost EPA estimates for installing a single wet ESP, $36 million in 
capital costs and $4.8 million annualized costs. 
 
 
Further, even assuming arguendo that the capital cost of a wet ESP is $50 million 
instead of $36 million as EPA found in the AMOS memo, the agency’s claim that the 
annualized costs are $13 million instead of $4.8 million would be unexplained and 
irrational. The $4.8 million annualized cost EPA provides in the AMOS Memo and in its 
discussion of beyond the floor standards for anode refining is approximately 13 percent of 
the $36 million capital cost EPA provides there. The $13 million annualized cost EPA 
claims in rejecting wet ESPs in its ample margin of safety analysis, however, is 26 percent 
of the $50 million capital cost there. Nowhere does EPA explain why it assumes that 
annualized cost are twice as high, as a percentage of capital costs, than it found in the 
AMOS Memo and in its discussion of beyond the floor standards for anode refining. 
 
 
Moreover, even if the $50 million capital cost and $13 million annualized cost 
claims EPA makes in the proposal were supported by record evidence and not directly 
undermined by EPA’s statements elsewhere in the record, but see supra, even an 
annualized cost of $13 million would reflect less than 0.09 percent – less than one tenth of 
one percent – of Freeport’s $14.8 billion annual revenues. Thus, it would still be 
economically achievable.    
 
Even if there had been no developments in practices, processes and control 
technologies, but see supra, § 112(d)(6) requires EPA to “to ‘revise’ an emission standard 
“as necessary” to bring it into compliance with the Act’s own definition in section 112(d) 
(1)-(3) of a minimally adequate ‘emission standard.’”49 These requirements include 
reducing each of the listed hazardous air pollutants that a source category emits by the 
maximum achievable degree. 
 
EPA’s proposed revisions to its copper smelters standards do not purport to ensure 
the maximum achievable degree of reduction in emissions of lead, arsenic and the other 
 
46 Id. 
47 87 Fed. Reg. at 1633. 
48 Id. 
49 LEAN, 955 F.3d at 1098. 

11 
 
hazardous air pollutants for which EPA claims PM is a surrogate. Indeed, EPA did not 
even attempt to determine whether they require the maximum achievable degree of 
reduction, relying instead on its unlawful and arbitrary claim that there are no 
developments.  
 
EPA’s standards do not require the “maximum” degree of reduction in lead, 
arsenic, and the other hazardous air pollutants for which EPA uses PM as a surrogate 
because far greater reductions can be achieved through the use of the Kennecott smelter’s 
Outotec combined smelting and converting system and through the use of wet ESPs. EPA 
does not claim the use of these control measures is not achievable and provides no basis for 
rejecting them in its § 112(d)(6) other than its unlawful and arbitrary claims that they are 
not developments. 
 
As the D.C. Circuit has made plain and as EPA has recognized, § 112(d)(6) 
requires EPA to promulgate limits for any hazardous air pollutants that are emitted by a 
source category and not already subject to limits.50 EPA’s proposal violates this 
requirement. 
 
EPA proposes new limits for mercury and certain previously unregulated emission 
points at copper smelters.51 Commenters support this aspect of this proposal but note that 
EPA’s approach to standard-setting leaves many of the new limits meaningless. 
 
EPA proposes a beyond the floor standard for mercury based on the emission 
reductions that can be achieved with activated carbon injection. EPA estimates this limit 
would result in a 90 percent reduction of the Freeport’s acid plant, which accounts for 64 
percent of the category’s total mercury emissions.52 If these estimates are correct, EPA’s 
new limit will reduce the category’s total emissions by about 58 percent. Commenters 
support this reduction. However, EPA must set mercury limits for all the emission points 
that emit mercury. Because Freeport’s acid plant accounts for only 64 percent of the 
category’s total mercury emissions, there are plainly other emission points that account for 
the other 36 percent. EPA must set mercury emission limits for these emission points as 
well. 
 
EPA also proposes a beyond the floor limit for fugitive particulate matter (PM) 
emissions from the anode refining process.53 EPA estimates this limit will reduce fugitive 
emissions of lead and arsenic from the Freeport smelter by 4.25 tpy, or approximately 81 
percent.54 Although 4.25 tons of lead and arsenic is only a relatively small portion of the 
toxic pollution emitted by Freeport in particular and the two smelters located near the San 
Carlos Apache Reservation more generally, eliminating these emissions is an important 
step in the right direction. Commenters support this reduction. 
 
 
50 Id. 955 F.3d 1089, 1098; 87 Fed. Reg. at 1620 (citing LEAN). 
51 87 Fed. Reg. at 1632. 
52 Id. at 1636. 
53 Id. at 1634-1635. 
54 Id. 

12 
 
EPA also proposes PM limits for point source emissions from anode refining, 
fugitive emissions from converters and smelters, and point source emissions from new 
converters. Because of EPA’s standard setting approach, these standards will achieve no 
reductions at all. EPA confirms this point by estimating that its rule, as a whole, will 
achieve reductions of 4.26 tpy – i.e., no more than is achieved by its limit for fugitive 
emissions from anode furnaces and its mercury limit.55 
 
One reason that the PM limits for point source emissions from anode refining, 
fugitive emissions from converters and smelters, and point source emissions from new 
converters achieve no reductions standards is that EPA set them at the statutory minimum 
stringency required by § 112(d)(3). This provision requires floors to be no less stringent 
than the average emission level achieved by the best performing five sources, and EPA 
considered emissions only from the two major sources, Freeport and Asarco.56  
 
Compounding the problem, EPA did not even gather emissions data for both major 
sources. In each instance where it set limits at the floor, EPA says it has information for 
only one source, the Freeport plant.57 EPA’s failure to gather information for both of the 
two major sources contravenes the Clean Air Act, is arbitrary, and shows contempt for the 
statute and the people Congress enacted it to protect.  
 
When EPA sets existing source standards for categories with fewer than 30 sources, 
it must base limits on “the average emission limitation achieved by the best performing 5 
sources (for which the Administrator has or could reasonably obtain emissions 
information).”58 Although EPA claims it has emissions information for only one smelter, 
the agency does not claim it cannot reasonably obtain emissions information for the other. 
Indeed, any such claim would be implausible. EPA would need to gather data for only one 
other smelter (or two, if the agency included the Kennecott smelter in its floor 
calculations). The agency has ample authority to gather these data.59 The simple and 
regrettable reality is that, after delaying its residual risk and technology review for copper 
smelters for more than a decade after the statutory deadline, EPA did not even bother to 
require emissions testing from any smelter. 
 
EPA’s floors are unlawful because they are not based on the average emission 
limitation achieved by the best performing 5 sources for which the Administrator has or 
could reasonably obtain emissions information. They are also arbitrary because EPA does 
not even consider its statutory obligation makes no effort whatsoever to square its failure to 
obtain information with § 112(d)(3)(B). 
 
 
55 Id. at 1650. 
56 42 U.S.C. § 7412(d)(3); EPA, MACT Floor Analysis for the Primary Copper Smelting 
Source Category (November 27, 2020), EPA-HQ-OAR-2020-0430-0055. 
57 87 Fed. Reg. at 1632-1636. 
58 42 U.S.C. § 7412(d)(3)(B) (emphasis added). 
59 42 U.S.C. § 7414. See U.S. Sugar v. EPA, 830 F.3d 579, 644 (D.C. Cir. 2016). 

13 
 
EPA’s new source floors are unlawful as well. Section 112(d)(3) requires new 
source floors to reflect the emission level achieved by the single best performing source.60 
EPA has emissions information for only one of the two major source copper smelters and 
no idea whether that source is the “single best performing” one. EPA does not even claim 
that the source for which it has emissions information, the Freeport smelter, is the single 
best performing source, and it offers no record basis to support any such claim. 
 
If anything, the record indicates that the source for which EPA does not have 
emissions information, the Asarco smelter, is the better performing source with at least 
some of the emission points for which EPA is setting new limits. Asarco’s self-reported 
total emissions of lead to EPA’s Toxics Release Inventory (TRI) are far lower than 
Freeport’s, the source for which EPA says it has emissions information.61  
 
EPA’s failure to base its new source standards on the emission level actually 
achieved by the single best performing source is flatly unlawful. Further, EPA’s failure to 
confront an important aspect of the problem – that it does not actually know whether the 
source for which it happens to have emissions information is the best performer – is 
arbitrary. And, although EPA does not claim that source on which it based its new floors is 
the single best performer, any such claim would be arbitrary because it is completely 
unsupported by record evidence and refuted by the sources’ TRI reports. 
 
EPA must use its § 114 authority to gather all the emissions data it needs to set 
lawful non-arbitrary limits for the emission points it previously failed to regulate. In doing 
so, EPA should be cognizant that its failure to set limits for these emission points was 
unlawful when EPA first promulgated emission standards for copper smelters in 2002 and 
that the agency has now deprived people of legally mandated protection from toxic air 
pollution for more than 20 years. EPA needs to remedy this situation, and setting do-
nothing limits now adds insult to injury. 
 
Beyond EPA’s arbitrary and unlawful floor approach, it should be plain to the 
agency that Congress did not intend it to set meaningless standards whenever it regulates a 
source category with only a few sources. EPA could prevent this outcome by not using its 
“upper prediction limit” (“UPL”), which produces a number that is worse than the worst 
measured emission level at any source. EPA could also set standards that truly reflect that 
“maximum” degree of reduction that is “achievable” considering cost, as required by 
§ 112(d)(2)(B). By failing to do either of these things, however, EPA produces limits that 
require no reductions at all. 
 
 
60 42 U.S.C. § 7412(d)(3); U.S. Sugar, 830 F.3d at 594 (“For new sources—those built 
after promulgation of a HAPs limit, see 42 U.S.C. § 7412(a)(4)—the MACT floor cannot 
be less stringent than the emissions levels achieved by the best performing similar 
source.”).  
61 TRI Spreadsheets showing Freeport’s and Asarco’s reported emissions of lead 
compounds, Ex. A hereto. 

14 
 
EPA rejects beyond the floor standards for point source emissions from anode 
refining based on its claim that costs of installing a wet ESP would be “relatively high” and 
that the cost-effectiveness would be “poor.”62  
 
The question EPA must answer under Clean Air Act § 112(d)(2) is not whether it 
thinks the costs of a limit are “relatively high” or the cost-effectiveness “poor,” but 
whether it reflects the “maximum” degree of reduction that is “achievable” considering 
costs.63 EPA does not even attempt to answer this question and does not even claim that its 
proposed limit for point source emissions from anode refining reflects the maximum 
achievable degree of reduction. For this reason alone, its proposed limit is unlawful and 
arbitrary. 
 
Had EPA determined the maximum degree of reduction that is “achievable,” rather 
than a level at which EPA thinks the cost per ton is “relatively high” or “poor,” it is 
difficult to imagine that the agency would have rejected a beyond-the-floor limit on cost 
grounds. Elsewhere in the proposal EPA states that the purported annualized cost of the 
rule for Freeport, $2.3 million, would reflect just “0.016 percent” of the annual revenue of 
the Freeport smelter’s ultimate parent company.64 If $2.3 million is just 0.016 percent of it, 
Freeport’s annual revenue is $14,475,000,000 -- approximately $14.48 billion. The $4.8 
million annualized cost of a wet ESP would reflect only 0.033 percent of Freeport’s 
annualized revenue, and Freeport could easily afford it. EPA provides no revenue estimate 
for Asarco and, thus, no reason to believe it is not also achievable for Asarco. 
 
EPA’s rejection of a beyond the floor standard based on its claim that cost-
effectiveness is poor is arbitrary for at least two reasons. First, EPA admits it does not 
know how much a wet ESP would reduce emissions from anode refining. To rejecting this 
measure on cost-effectiveness grounds is nothing more than speculation. 
 
Second, EPA’s rejection of wet ESP technology rests on its assumption that the 
smelters would install separate wet ESPs on each different emission point – i.e. one to 
control point source emissions from anode refining operations and another one to control 
point source emissions from converters. EPA itself states that both the Freeport and Asarco 
smelters already “have many combined gas streams that vent to a common control system 
and/or stack” and that “at Asarco, the exhaust gases from the dryers, smelting furnace, 
converters, anode furnaces and acid plant are all ultimately vented to the main stack.”65 
Record evidence that multiple emission sources can be – and are – routed to the same 
control system and stack, it makes no sense to assume that the cost of a wet ESP should be 
measured against emission reductions from just one emission source. Assuming that cost 
per ton is a relevant metric for beyond the floor standards, EPA needs to compare the cost 
of a wet ESP against the total reduction if all emission sources were routed through it. The 
agency offers no reason to believe that the smelters could not and would not install a single 
wet ESP to control point source emissions from more than one emission point, as they 
already have done with other control technology. If EPA had considered this solution, it 
 
62 87 Fed. Reg. at 1633. 
63 42 U.S.C. § 7412(d)(2). 
64 87 Fed. Reg. at 1651. 
65 RRA, Appendix 1 at A-3. 

15 
 
would have recognized that the emission reductions achieved through wet ESPs would be a 
combined reduction of emissions from multiple points. 
 
EPA identifies several pollutants for which its current standards lack emission 
limits, including hexane, formaldehyde, toluene, benzene, p-dichlorobenzene, and 
naphthalene.66 The agency states that although emissions of these pollutants have been 
reported in the National Emissions Inventory (NEI), it does not actually know whether 
copper smelters emit them or in what quantity they may be emitted.67 EPA apparently 
made no independent effort to disprove the NEI data indicating that copper smelters do 
emit these pollutants. Nonetheless, EPA does not propose limits for any of them. 
 
The Clean Air Act unambiguously requires EPA to set limits for each hazardous air 
pollutant that a source category emits.68 It does not excuse EPA from meeting this 
obligation just because the agency has chosen not to test for a pollutant and, therefore, does 
not know for certain whether it is emitted. It is EPA’s obligation to gather the data it needs 
to set lawful standards.69 That obligation includes gathering emissions data to determine 
which pollutants a category emits and to set limits for them. 
 
EPA’s failure to meet its obligation to set limits for listed hazardous air pollutants 
that copper smelters emits is egregious with respect to dioxins. EPA’s proposal neither sets 
limits for dioxins nor even mentions them, leaving the public to believe that copper 
smelters do not emit them. Yet Freeport has been reporting significant emissions of dioxins 
to EPA’s Toxics Release Inventory (TRI) every year for more than 20 years.70 Moreover, 
the European Environment Agency has found that copper smelters emit dioxins in 
significant quantities, 0.01 micrograms (TEQ) per metric ton of copper produced, as well 
as 3.7 micrograms of PCBs per metric ton of copper produced.71 In 2018, primary copper 
smelters in the U.S. produced 536,000 metric tons of copper.72 At this rate copper smelters  
emitted 5,360 micrograms TEQ of dioxins and 1,983,200 micrograms of PCBs. Not only 
did EPA not test copper smelters for dioxin emissions, it did not even consult its own TRI 
database or consider other evidence, such as the European Environment Agency’s report, 
showing that they do emit dioxins.. 
 
EPA has a clear statutory obligation to set limits for coppers smelters emissions of, 
at a minimum, dioxins, hexane, formaldehyde, toluene, benzene, p-dichlorobenzene, and

obligation, EPA must promptly use its § 114 authority to require the emissions testing it 
needs to set all missing limits. 
III. 
EPA’s Proposed Residual Risk Analysis 
A. EPA Underestimates Copper Smelters’ Emissions. 
 
Because EPA failed to require emissions testing, it does not have actual emissions 
data in sufficient quantities to accurately determine copper smelters’ toxic emissions.  
 
For the Freeport smelter, EPA states that it relies on a small set of emission test 
results provided by the Arizona Department of Environmental Quality (ADEQ).73 EPA 
states it supplemented these data with select data that Asarco and Freeport provided after 
they were invited to comment on the data that were allegedly posted on a website “for 
public review.”74 EPA did not publish notice of the data or otherwise notify commenters of 
the website or the availability of data for comment.75 Only Asarco and Freeport 
commented on the data.76 EPA’s decision to allow only two companies with a financial 
interest in the outcome of the rule to participate in its collection and selection of the data on 
which its proposed rule is based raises serious questions about the integrity and reliability 
of the data and the fairness rulemaking process.  
 
EPA does not claim that the set of data it used for Freeport include data for all the 
emission points at Freeport or all the hazardous air pollutants they emit, and it appears that 
some emission points and pollutants are not included. 
 
Although EPA claims that the emissions data it used for both Freeport and Asarco 
are in the docket,77 most of the relevant data appear to be missing from the docket. 
Commenters were not able to locate either the test results supplied by ADEQ or the test 
results supplied by Freeport in the docket, despite diligent searching and repeated requests 
to EPA for assistance.78 Instead, EPA appears to have provided only its own estimates of 
Freeport’s yearly emissions of certain pollutants from certain emission points based on 
those data.79 Exacerbating the problem, EPA does not say how it derived the yearly 
emission numbers from the data, indicating only that it used “engineering judgment.”80 
Thus, EPA asks commenters to accept its claims about yearly emissions at face value

without being able to see either the data they are purportedly based on or how EPA used

The Clean Air Act provides:   
 
In the case of any rule to which this subsection applies, notice of proposed 
rulemaking shall be published in the Federal Register, as provided under section 
553(b) of title 5, shall be accompanied by a statement of its basis and purpose and 
shall specify the period available for public comment (hereinafter referred to as the 
“comment period”). The notice of proposed rulemaking shall also state the docket 
number, the location or locations of the docket, and the times it will be open to 
public inspection. The statement of basis and purpose shall include a summary of— 
 
(A)  the factual data on which the proposed rule is based; 
(B)  the methodology used in obtaining the data and in analyzing the data; and 
(C)  the major legal interpretations and policy considerations underlying the 
proposed rule. 
 
The statement shall also set forth or summarize and provide a reference to any 
pertinent findings, recommendations, and comments by the Scientific Review 
Committee established under section 7409(d) of this title and the National 
Academy of Sciences, and, if the proposal differs in any important respect from any 
of these recommendations, an explanation of the reasons for such differences. All 
data, information, and documents referred to in this paragraph on which the 
proposed rule relies shall be included in the docket on the date of publication of the 
proposed rule.81 
 
EPA’s failure to include data on which it claims to rely on the date of publication of the 
proposed copper smelters RTR violates the Clean Air Act.82 Likewise, EPA’s failure to 
show the “methodology” it used to “analyz[e] the data” violates the Act.83 As a practical 
matter, it prevents commenters from evaluating EPA’s claims about copper smelters’ 
yearly emissions and providing fully informed comments on both EPA’s claims about 
copper smelters’ yearly emissions and the risk analysis based on those claims. EPA must 
immediately post in the docket the missing emissions information and an explanation of 
the methodology it used to derive its emission numbers from these data and must reopen 
the comment period for at least 30 days to allow the public to comment on them.  
 
For the Asarco smelter, EPA states that it relies primarily on estimates from the 
National Emissions Inventory (NEI) for 2017, supplemented by some 2019 emissions test 
data that Asarco chose to submit.84 
 
As EPA’s own Office of the Inspector General (“OIG”) warned the agency long 
ago, “[t]he heavy use of emission factors in the [national emissions inventory] makes the 
 
81 42 U.S.C. § 7607(d)(3) (emphasis added). 
82 Id. 
83 Id. 
84 87 Fed. Reg. at 1621; RRA, Appendix 1 at A-2. 

18 
 
reliability of the data highly uncertain. Emission factors can result in emissions data of 
questionable reliability… .”85 EPA’s reliance on NEI data and its failure to even 
acknowledge the flaws in such an approach even though they were clearly identified more 
than a decade ago, is arbitrary.  
 
One concern about EPA’s use of emission factors in the NEI is that the tests used to 
develop emission factors are intentionally conducted on new equipment operating under 
normal conditions because emission factors are formulas that attempt to estimate long-term 
average emissions.86 EPA itself notes that “[p]arameters that can cause short-term 
fluctuations in emissions are generally avoided in testing and not taken into account in test 
evaluation.”87 Further, “[s]ources often are tested more frequently when they are new and 
when they are believed to be operating properly, and either situation may bias the 
results.”88 
 
Here, it is not even clear how emissions of copper smelters’ hazardous air pollutant 
emissions could be generated for NEI, let alone how such estimates could possibly be 
regarded as reliable for the purposes of estimating risk. As noted in the Inspector General 
report, the purported basis for NEI estimates is testing of equipment. But, with the 
exception of the few test results provided by ADEQ, Freeport and Asarco, copper smelters 
have not been tested for hazardous air pollutant emissions. Therefore there are no test data 
upon which NEI estimates could be based with any credibility. 
 
Although EPA claims to have relied on the NEI estimates for Asarco, it did not 
place these estimates in the docket. Further, EPA does not explain how it combines NEI 
estimates for Asarco with the Asarco-supplied emissions data to reach its claims about 
Asarco’s yearly emissions. As explained above with regard to the ADEQ data and the 2019 
emissions data supplied by Freeport, EPA’s failure the post the NEI estimates and the 2019 
emissions data supplied by Asarco in the docket at the time it issued the proposed RTR 
violates Clean Air Act § 307(d)(3).89 Likewise, EPA does not provide the methodology by 
which it used the NEI emission estimates and the Asarco-supplied data to reach its claims 
about Asarco’s yearly emissions. EPA must immediately post in the docket the missing 
NEI emission estimates, the missing emissions data, and an explanation of the 
methodology EPA used to derive yearly emission numbers from those data. EPA also must 
reopen the comment period for at least 30 days to allow the public to comment on them. 
 
For both Freeport and Asarco, the agency’s acceptance and use of limited data these 
companies selected and then chose to submit to the record is troubling, given EPA’s failure 
to require any mandatory emissions testing and its failure to include the public in its review

of the data. EPA does not provide any basis to believe the data supplied by these 
companies is representative and not cherry-picked to create the impression that their 
emissions are lower than they actually are.  
 
EPA states that it chose to use so-called “actual” emissions data rather than 
“allowable” emissions data to estimate copper smelters’ emissions.90 Much of the “data” 
EPA describes as “actual” emissions data, however, are mostly not actual emissions data at 
all, but NEI estimates with all the flaws described above. Given the scarcity of truly 
“actual” emissions data for copper smelters and the established unreliability of NEI data, 
EPA should use “allowable” data – as it agrees it can and has in other rulemakings.91 
Nowhere does EPA explain its choice to use the flawed set of so-called “actual” data in this 
rulemaking instead of allowable data. 
 
Underscoring the flaws in EPA’s emissions estimates is the gap between them and 
the emissions that both Asarco and Freeport submitted to TRI. For example, EPA estimates 
that both facilities together emit 13.47 tpy of lead compounds. In 2018, however, Freeport 
alone reported emitting 29 tons of lead compounds. Asarco reported emitting more than 1.8 
tons of lead, even though it operated for only part of the year. Thus, the companies’ own 
reports indicate they emitted more than 30 tons of lead compounds that year – i.e., 
substantially more than twice the amount EPA estimates.92 EPA does not even 
acknowledge the TRI reports, let alone explain why it uses emission estimates that are so 
much lower than the emissions reported by the facilities. 
 
Another troublesome inconsistency stems from the relatively low level of arsenic 
EPA assumes is in smelters’ emissions. Whereas EPA assumes that smelters emit 13 tons 
of lead and 2 tons of arsenic each year for a ratio of 2/13 or 15.4 percent, the European 
Environment Agency has found that the ratio is 4/19, or 21.1 percent.93  
 
Given all of these flaws, EPA’s low emission estimates are arbitrary and capricious. 
B. EPA Underestimates Exposures to Copper Smelters’ Toxic Emissions. 
 EPA’s RTR only analyzed lead exposures through inhalation, yet it is clear that 
when lead is deposited onto soils and then retrained into the environment those emissions 
result in contamination of the food chain, surface waters, residential interiors via track-in 
and children through hand-to-mouth activities of young children who ingest dust particles. 
By EPA only calculating inhalation risk, EPA grossly underestimates the exposures to lead 
in the communities hosting these copper smelters and the San Carlos Reservation.94 
 
90 RRA, Appendix 1 at A-10 – A-11. 
91 Id. 
92 TRI Spreadsheets showing Freeport’s and Asarco’s emissions of lead compounds, Ex. A 
hereto.

10-11.

USEPA itself developed the IEUBK model specifically to estimate the exposures to 
lead from the multi-pathways it takes in the environment as it is deposited on soil.  It is 
shocking that for this risk assessment EPA is refusing to use it and therefore grossly 
underestimating risk by only looking at the inhalation pathway.95 
There is a lack of adequate ambient air monitors in this airshed; there are no air 
monitors located on the San Carlos Apache Reservation, yet they are directly downwind of 
two huge air pollution sources, despite a duty in the Clean Air Act to monitor near the 
highest-emitting sources. This lack of adequate monitoring hampers both the agency’s and 
the public’s ability to quantify the risk from these facilities in the deposition zone for lead 
and arsenic emissions from all three of these copper smelters. 
There are questionable lead NAAQS monitoring data for the Freeport smelter. For 
example, EPA assumes the Freeport smelter is in compliance with the lead NAAQS even 
though its TRI reports indicate that it’s emitting much more lead than the Asarco smelter, 
which is violating both the lead NAAQS and the SOX NAAQS.96 EPA’s assumption that 
the Freeport analysis is in compliance with the lead NAAQS and failure to consider this 
assumption in light of Freeport’s TRI reports is arbitrary. 
Also arbitrary is EPA’s failure to explain why the modeled levels of lead are so far 
below the actual monitored levels.97 EPA’s assumption that its modeled levels are correct 
even though they are so far below monitored levels is arbitrary. 
EPA acknowledges that Freeport’s lead emissions are very high when it recycles 
acid plant sludge and/or uses the anode utility vessel.98 EPA, “assum[es],” however, these 
practices occur only 7 weeks out of a 104 week period.99 The agency then proceeds to 
“weight” its estimate of Freeport’s total emissions based on that assumption. Nowhere in 
the record does EPA indicate how it knows that Freeport does not recycle acid plant sludge 
or use the anode utility vessel more often or provide a record basis for making that 
assumption, which substantially reduces its emission estimate. EPA’s weighting approach, 
and its resulting emissions and risk calculations are arbitrary for these reason as well. 
EPA states that to determine exposure it combined monitoring data from 2019 with 
meteorological data from 2012-2013.100 The weather prevailing in 2019 may have driven 
far greater exposures as a result of the 2019 emissions than the weather in 2012-2013 
would have driven. In particular EPA does not explain why it used data from the wrong 
year or what effect this had. EPA does not claim, and could not claim, that getting 
meteorological data from the correct year was impossible; there is a weather station in

Globe, AZ. EPA’s use of weather data from a time when the monitored emissions were not 
actually happening is arbitrary, as is EPA’s total failure to explain its use of those data 
instead of data for the correct time period. 
EPA never explains, much less examines, what conclusions can be drawn about the 
behavior of lead from copper smelters from ambient monitored lead emissions near Hayden 
during the Asarco shutdown. When this facility is shut down, it will still be emitting lead 
from fugitive emissions. For example, the retired Exide lead smelters in Vernon, CA has 
fenceline monitors for lead surrounding it, and is still emitting lead.  EPA could get a very 
good idea how much lead is being emitted from production activities by examining how 
much lead is being emitted during the shutdown. Subtracting the “background lead 
emissions” produced while the facility was non-operational would allow EPA to develop 
an emissions factor for fugitives when the facility is operational. EPA’s failure to 
determine the background levels of lead in the air is arbitrary and renders its conclusions 
about ambient lead levels, exposure levels, and risk arbitrary. 
C. EPA’s Assumption That Risks from Lead Exposure Are Acceptable If 
They Do Not Exceed the Lead NAAQS Is Unlawful and Arbitrary. 
All of the metal hazardous air pollutants that copper smelters emit are persistent in 
the environment. So too are dioxins, PAHs, POMs, Lead, arsenic, cadmium, dioxins and 
other hazardous air pollutants that copper smelters emitted. All these long-lived, persistent 
pollutants emitted years and even decades ago, persist in water and soil even now. 
The Freeport and Asarco smelters have both been emitting these persistent 
hazardous air pollutants from sites bordering the San Carlos Apache reservation for more 
than a century. During that time lead, arsenic and other persistent poisons have built up in 
the soil of the San Carlos Apache reservation. It is well known that the soil in Hayden is 
badly contaminated with lead.101 Some of the Lead emissions from the Hayden and Miami 
Arizona smelters are lofted into the air by their tall stacks and then deposited on nearby 
communities such as the San Carlos Apache Reservation.102 There is strong reason, 
therefore, to believe that, after more than a century of emissions from the Asarco and 
Freeport smelters, lead, arsenic, cadmium, dioxins and other persistent pollutants have 
accumulated both on the San Carlos Apache Reservation and in other areas near the these 
smelters.  
One of the main pathways by which children are exposed to lead, in particular, is 
through contact with lead-contaminated soil. EPA did not even attempt to evaluate the risks 
resulting from exposure to lead through drinking water, eating food, and contact with soil. 
EPA states “[i]n evaluating the potential multipathway risk from emissions of lead

maximum estimated chronic inhalation exposure concentrations to the level of the current 
National Ambient Air Quality Standard (NAAQS) for lead. Values above the level of the 
primary (health-based) lead NAAQS are considered to have a potential for multipathway 
risk.103 For this source category modeled max 3-month concentration were just above the 
NAAQS standard for lead as discussed above.”104   
“[C]hronic inhalation exposures” do not begin to fully reflect the exposure or the 
risks faced by people who live near the Asarco and Freeport smelters. EPA’s blanket 
assumption that ambient air levels below the lead NAAQS are safe is unlawful and 
arbitrary.  It is especially galling to note that these smelters have operated now for over a 
century, and have emitted hundreds of tons into the surrounding environment that also 
contribute to the exposure exposome of the nearby towns and the San Carlos Apache tribal 
members.  Lead laden dust from historic deposition can become resuspended in the 
ambient air during wind episodes and contribute to the further exposure of the surrounding 
residents.  This resuspension of lead dust is a known and well studies phenomenon that 
contributes to childhood lead poisoning in our nation’s inner cities each summer.105  
Indeed, in Flint, Michigan the children are not only affected by the lead in drinking 
water, but also by resuspended lead from lead-contaminated soils from old industrial 
sources and other urban lead sources such as the use of leaded gasoline, leaded paint, and 
other emissions. 106 
There is a direct correlation between lead in topsoil and blood lead levels in 
children.  A notable study in New Orleans followed the decline in lead in topsoils in New 
Orleans and the corresponding decline in blood lead levels (BLLs) in children post 
Hurricane Katrina.   
“Lead (Pb) is extremely toxic and a major cause of chronic diseases worldwide. Pb 
is associated with health disparities, particularly within low-income populations. In 
biological systems, Pb mimics calcium and, among other effects, interrupts cell signaling. 
Furthermore, Pb exposure results in epigenetic changes that affect multigenerational gene 
expression. Exposure to Pb has decreased through primary prevention, including removal 
of Pb solder from canned food, regulating lead-based paint, and especially eliminating Pb 
additives in gasoline. While researchers observe a continuous decline in children’s blood 
lead (BPb), reservoirs of exposure persist in topsoil, which stores the legacy dust from 
leaded gasoline and other sources. Our surveys of metropolitan New Orleans reveal that 
median topsoil Pb in communities (n = 274) decreased 44% from 99 mg/kg to 54 mg/kg 
(P value of 2.09 × 10−08), with a median depletion rate of ∼2.4 mg⋅kg⋅y−1 over 15 y. From

in children: A review and new directions,

2000 through 2005 to 2011 through 2016, children’s BPb declined from 3.6 μg/dL to 1.2

12 y[ears].”107 
Moreover, a recent study at a former lead smelter in Omaha, NE found a significant 
correlation between the remediation of lead-contaminated soils and declines in childhood 
blood lead levels as well.   
“Even low-level lead exposures are associated with decrements in cognitive 
function in young children; specifically, effects on cognition are observed in populations 
with group mean BLLs <5μg/dL. Although contemporary BLLs in the United States are 
lower on average than they were in the past, lead from anthropogenic sources persists in the 
environment, and children are potentially exposed from legacy sources. No threshold for 
lead-related cognitive effects in children has been identified.”108 
The Omaha Lead Superfund Site (OLSS) was contaminated by emissions from a 
lead smelter and refining facility that operated for approximately 125 years until its closure 
in 1997. Other sources of lead contamination at the site include a former paint 
manufacturer, lead battery plant, and lead-based paint. The Agency for Toxic Substances 
and Disease Registry (ATSDR) conducted a public health assessment from July 2000 
through August 2002 and found that 9.7% of the children 0–6 y old in Omaha had BLLs 
greater than 10μg/dL10μg/dL109 “whereas this proportion was 3.1% in U.S. children overall 
at the time. In 2003, the OLSS was officially designated a Superfund site under the 
Comprehensive Environmental Response Compensation and Liability Act and was 
included on the National Priorities List of contaminated sites.”110 
This conclusion is very important to note, because the San Carlos Apache nation 
and the residents of the towns near these three copper smelters have already been exposed 
to a century of lead emissions as host to one of world’s greatest regional lead hotspots.  The 
reservoir of lead-contaminated soils around both of these smelters creates the background 
lead exposome to which the new lead emissions from these smelters will be added.   
The declines of blood lead levels in children witnessed in New Orleans post Katrina 
are very illustrative of the public health benefits of lead exposure reduction.  To be pointed, 
since 3.9 points (60%) of the IQ loss in children occurs between 2.4-10 
micrograms/deciliter of blood these reductions in BLL documented in New Orleans are 
extremely significant and have a large material positive effect on the affected populations.

As well, at federal Superfund sites (like the Asarco smelter) the Integrated Exposure 
Uptake BioKenetic Model (IEUBK)is used to estimate lead exposure to children.  The 
model is an EPA model used to estimate lead exposure in children using different exposure 
scenarios that are supposed to mimic the lead exposome of a given population.  This model 
clearly uses many different inputs to estimate lead exposures: food intake, drinking water 
consumption, maternal blood lead concentrations using NHANES  data, soil/dust inhalation 
parameters, and inhalation. 112 If EPA is serious about evaluating the risks to people who 
live near copper smelters, it needs to measure their actual exposure through all pathways 
and determine they levels to which they are suffering from lead poisoning already.113 
Again, compared to the IEUBK model, which EPA uses extensively to model risk 
from lead exposure, the inhalation-only risk assessment done for this rule is wholly 
inadequate especially in light of the decades of lead and arsenic emissions that have already 
emanated from these two sources. 
To satisfy Clean Air Act § 112(f)(2), EPA must determine whether “standards 
promulgated pursuant to subsection (d) and applicable to a category or subcategory of 
sources emitting a pollutant (or pollutants) classified as a known, probable or possible 
human carcinogen do not reduce lifetime excess cancer risks to the individual most 
exposed to emissions from a source in the category or subcategory to less than one in one 
million.”114 In addition, EPA must “promulgate standards for such category or subcategory 
if promulgation of such standards is required in order to provide an ample margin of safety 
to protect public health in accordance with this section (as in effect before November 15, 
1990) or to prevent, taking into consideration costs, energy, safety, and other relevant 
factors, an adverse environmental effect. Emission standards promulgated under this 
subsection shall provide an ample margin of safety to protect public health in accordance 
with this section (as in effect before November 15, 1990), unless the Administrator 
determines that a more stringent standard is necessary to prevent, taking into consideration 
costs, energy, safety, and other relevant factors, an adverse environmental effect.”115 
EPA cannot possibly satisfy these requirements where, as here, it makes a blanket 
assumption that health risks are acceptable so long as concentrations of lead in the ambient 
air are below the lead NAAQS. Specifically, EPA cannot possibly show that the health 
risks (including the risk of cancer) are acceptable in a community where lead has been 
deposited for more than a century, based solely on the lead levels in the air. Exposures to 
any new emissions of lead at all add to people’s exposures to lead that has accumulated 
from previous years’ emissions. Concentrations of accumulated lead in the soil alone may 
make health risks unacceptable and may mean that ambient concentrations of lead below 
the lead NAAQS are necessary for risks to be acceptable. EPA’s approach to lead risks 
unlawfully rewrites § 112(f)(2) to allow EPA to determine whether lead levels in the air are 
below the lead NAAQS rather than ensuring that risks – including risks from exposure to 
lead – are acceptable. 
Historic lead deposition from these smelters is the gift that keeps on giving because

EPA seems to be systematically ignoring the impact which re-suspension of 
entrained metals have on their risk findings and contributes to continuing adverse effects on 
environmental justice communities and subsistence fisher people. A recent paper called 
EPA out on this exact same shortcoming with their estimates on mercury emissions from 
coal fired power plants. 
“Deposition from legacy mercury: EPA did not consider the long lifetime of utility- 
derived mercury in the environment. After mercury from coal-fired EGUs is deposited to 
ecosystems, some is reemitted and continues to cycle in the global environment where it 
can accumulate in the commercial seafood that people eat. In our analysis, we estimate the 
global legacy of cumulative U.S. utility-derived mercury emissions from Streets et al. 
(2019) using a global biogeochemical box model (Amos et al., 2013, 2014). We used the 
model to quantify the contribution to global deposition from cumulative U.S. EGU 
emissions in 2010 (1.0%) and 2020 (0.89%) that is still actively cycling in the biosphere

and thus contributes to background deposition from all sources. This contribution to 
background deposition is an important contributor to mercury exposures from global ocean 
seafood harvests (Figure 3).”118 
Ironically, USEPA has known about the substantial contribution of the re-
suspension of lead from copper smelters for decades.119 For example, in 1998, USEPA 
published an extensive report on lead emissions from a number of source categories, 
including copper smelters.  They specifically included estimates (based on lead monitoring) 
from resuspension of lead from roadways near major lead sources.120 
To the extent EPA claims to know that just because lead levels are below the lead 
NAAQS, people living on the San Carlos Apache Reservation, in Hayden, Winkleman, or 
in Miami have acceptable health risks and are protected by an ample margin of safety, its 
claim is arbitrary. Because EPA does not and cannot claim to know how much lead people 
in these communities are exposed to, it cannot rationally claim that their risks are 
acceptable and that they are protected with an ample margin of safety by its copper smelter 
standards. Nor can EPA claim that its standards suffice to prevent adverse environmental 
effects in an environment that has already been contaminated by lead emissions from 
copper smelters for more than a century. 
 
EPA further claims: 
 
the primary lead NAAQS is a reasonable measure of determining risk acceptability 
(i.e., the first step of the Benzene NESHAP analysis) since it is designed to protect the

That argument lacks merit for several two reasons. First, contrary to EPA’s claim, the lead 
NAAQS is not designed to protect people exposed to emissions from a category of major 
sources of hazardous air pollutants from all risks of lead exposure, but only from the risks 
of inhalation exposure to lead. 
 
Second, EPA’s belief that applying the lead NAAQS at the acceptability step does 
not make the agency’s use of the lead NAAQS in place of an actual determination of the 
risks actually are for people exposed to lead emissions from copper smelters any less 
unlawful or arbitrary. As the D.C. Circuit has explained in the closely analogous context of 
§ 112(d)(4), EPA cannot avoid meeting specific statutory requirements for health-based 
decision-making merely by stating its approach is “conservative.”122 
 
 Third, the Children’s Health Protection Advisory Committee has advised EPA that 
it should strengthen the Lead NAAQS by an order of magnitude (to 0.02 μg/m3 or below, 
require better monitoring, and base the measurements on a one-month period) because it 
“is insufficient to protect children’s health.”123 The NAAQS in place now only seeks to 
avoid an air-related population mean IQ loss in excess of 2 points – recognizing that on 
average that, or in some communities even higher neurological harm is occurring under the 
2008 Lead NAAQS.124 There can be no doubt that risks and likely harm occur below the 
level of the 2008 NAAQS.  
D. EPA Correctly Finds that Risks from Copper Smelters Are Unacceptable. 
For several reasons, EPA correctly proposes to find that the health risks from 
copper smelters is unacceptable.125 EPA finds that, for the individual most exposed to 
allowable emissions from copper smelters, the inhalation cancer risk alone is 90-in-one-
million.126 
Further, ambient lead concentrations exceed the lead NAAQS, using both EPA’s 
“allowable” and so-called “actual” lead emissions estimates. “Lead concentrations above 
the NAAQS increase the risk of developmental effects for children.”127 Although lead 
levels below the NAAQS do not mean that risk levels are acceptable, risk levels above the 
NAAQS are plainly unacceptable. In addition, copper smelter pollutants other than lead,
