Appendix G 
MACT Compliance Monitoring, Enforcement, 
and Compliance Assistance Activities 
Our evaluation objectives included the following questions: 
• Is there a relationship between the quantity and quality of compliance monitoring and 
enforcement and the effectiveness of MACT standards in achieving the desired emissions 
reductions? 
• Is there a relationship between the adequacy of compliance assistance tools and the 
effectiveness of MACT standards in achieving the desired emissions reductions?   
Limitations to the amount and quality of data regarding compliance monitoring and compliance 
assistance activities prevented us from quantitatively assessing the impact of these activities on 
MACT effectiveness.  Instead we reviewed results from previous studies and initiatives, and 
discussed the impact of these activities on MACT effectiveness with representatives from 
selected facilities and States.  The sections below discuss the results of our review. 
. 
Results of Statistically Valid Compliance Rate Studies  
We identified two statistically valid compliance rate studies that have been conducted for MACT 
industries, one by EPA and the other by the California Air Resources Board.  Both of these 
studies found high noncompliance rates for their respective industries.   
In Fiscal Year 2002, EPA conducted a statistically valid study of compliance with the Ethylene 
Oxide Sterilizer MACT. This is the only statistically valid study EPA has conducted for the 
MACT standards. Generally, sources that operate ethylene oxide sterilizers are smaller 
stationary sources that sterilize or fumigate medical supplies, pharmaceuticals, and/or spices.  
EPA randomly selected 70 facilities to inspect out of a universe of 222 facilities.  The study 
found that 50.8 percent of the selected facilities were out of compliance with the MACT 
standard. 
In a statistically valid study conducted in 1999 and 2000, the California Air Resources Board 
found significant noncompliance with California’s Chrome Plating Airborne Toxic Control 
Measure, the State’s equivalent to the Federal Chrome Electroplating MACT.  Inspectors in five 
air districts conducted on-site inspections of 188 chrome platers and found 73 percent of them 
had some type of violation.  The majority of the violations pertained to monitoring, 
recordkeeping, and reporting: 68 percent of the facilities had a non-emission-related violation; 
39 percent had an emission-related violation (a violation that could potentially result in an 
emission exceedance); and 8 percent had a direct excess emission violation (an emission 
exceedance witnessed by the inspector).   
In a followup to the Chrome Plating compliance rate study, the California Air Resources Board 
and the individual air districts conducted a number of targeted compliance-related activities for 
57 

the chrome platers in the study.  After conducting these permitting, training, enforcement, and 
outreach activities, the facilities were inspected again in 2003.  The California Air Resources 
Board found that the noncompliance rate at these facilities had decreased from 78 percent in 
1999-2000 to 14 percent in 2003. 
EPA’s Targeted MACT Initiatives Have Found Noncompliance with MACT 
Standards 
OECA has included air toxics as a National Enforcement Priority since Fiscal Year 2000.  For 
the planning cycle Fiscal Years 2005-2007, OECA implemented an air toxics initiative in which 
each region selects MACT source categories for targeted compliance investigations and 
enforcement.  EPA’s original goal under this initiative was to achieve air toxics emissions 
reductions of 12,000 pounds each year from these targeted MACTs, for a total reduction of 
36,000 pounds by the end of Fiscal Year 2007. However, reported emissions reductions for 
Fiscal Years 2005 and 2006 under the initiative have greatly exceeded this goal.  In Fiscal Year 
2005, total reported air toxics emission reductions from regionally targeted cases were 332,000 
pounds; for Fiscal Year 2006, they were 356,000 pounds. Most of the violations and resulting 
emissions reductions were from facilities subject to the Secondary Aluminum MACT, the 
Hazardous Organic NESHAP, and the Pharmaceutical MACT. 
Based on its enforcement activity in Fiscal Years 2004-2005 related to the national air toxics 
strategy, EPA found significant noncompliance with the LDAR requirements of the MACT 
standards. Twenty-two MACTs, including the Petroleum Refining MACT and the Hazardous 
Organic NESHAP, have LDAR requirements.  In response to this noncompliance, OECA is 
conducting a national targeted initiative in Fiscal Year 2006-2007 to focus on MACT equipment 
leak requirements. 
Impact of Self-Monitoring and Reporting Requirements on Compliance and 
Emissions Reductions 
Representatives from five of the eight sampled facilities we interviewed told us that self-
monitoring requirements were important to achieving compliance with MACT requirements and 
gaining emissions reductions.  In general, the facility representatives we spoke with told us that 
internal compliance monitoring, rather than State-conducted on-site inspections, is what drives 
compliance and emissions reductions.  They told us that the self-monitoring required by the 
MACT standard can help a facility identify a problem before it becomes a violation, and can help 
ensure that the facility fixes problems more quickly.   
The only MACT violations at the nine sample facilities we reviewed were discovered through 
internal facility monitoring and self-reported by the facilities to State agencies.  All three of the 
primary aluminum facilities have reported violations to the State, two for emissions exceedances 
and one for failure to record startup and shutdown events.  One of the petroleum refineries also 
self-reported violations pertaining to startup and shutdown maintenance.   
Industry representatives told us that self-reporting requirements enable regulatory agencies to 
become aware of MACT violations that they otherwise may not detect.  For example, one 
58 

refinery representative told us that finding violations would require review of the facility's 
extensive records, noting that there are about 250,000 components in the LDAR database alone.  
He said the State does not have the time or expertise to inspect at that degree of specificity.  
Compliance and enforcement staff from Kansas told us that self-reporting is very important 
because the State does not have the number of staff nor expertise to find every violation at 
petroleum refineries.  They said the refineries have more knowledge and expertise about their 
operations than Kansas’ inspectors, and this makes it difficult for them to determine if the facility 
is in compliance.  According to one Kansas staff member, the State has to have a lot of trust in 
the facility.  If a facility reports that it is in compliance, the State has to accept that unless their 
inspectors find otherwise. 
State and Industry Representatives Generally Find Compliance Assistance Useful  
EPA developed several compliance assistance tools for each of the three MACTs we selected for 
in-depth analysis. These tools24 include such documents as plain language guides, applicability 
flowcharts, and Questions and Answers documents. Six of the seven States and six of the eight 
facilities we spoke with had used one or more of EPA’s compliance assistance tools in 
implementing the three MACTs in our sample.  Representatives from the six States that used the 
tools, and from five of the six facilities that used the tools, said that they were useful for 
implementing the MACT.  The petroleum refinery representatives we spoke with said they found 
EPA’s Questions and Answers document particularly helpful.  
The results of EPA-conducted online surveys on the usefulness of its compliance assistance tools 
generally showed good results. In 2004 and 2005, EPA surveyed industry and regulators on the 
usefulness and effectiveness of EPA’s compliance assistance tools for four MACT standards: the 
Aerospace MACT, Plywood MACT, Reciprocating Internal Combustion Engines MACT, and 
Industrial Boiler MACT. In general, the surveys found that the tools were obtained and used by 
a large portion of the respondents.  Most of the tools were rated as "good" or "very good" in 
terms of usefulness by a majority of respondents.   
State agency staff and industry managers told us that compliance assistance tools could be 
improved in some areas.  For example, EPA did not issue some of the tools until after the 
compliance date of the MACT.  Representatives from three States told us that some of EPA's 
inspection checklists are of limited value because they cover too much information or are too 
complicated to use.  In addition, an official from one of the refineries told us that many of the 
tools are labeled "Draft" or included a disclaimer.  Thus the guidance may not be very helpful 
when it comes to inspections or enforcement.  For example, the Petroleum Refining MACT 
Standard Guidance contains a disclaimer that the document is only guidance and does not create 
any enforceable rights. 
24 A complete list of the tools developed by EPA for these three MACTs is located in Appendix G. 
59 

State Representatives Indicated that Compliance Assistance Is More Important 
for Smaller Facilities 
In general, State agency staff told us compliance assistance is most important and useful for 
smaller facilities.  They explained that smaller sources may not be accustomed to being 
regulated, and that compliance assistance can be critical for effective implementation of the 
standards at these facilities.  In contrast, larger facilities often have environmental staff to 
implement the regulations and are often members of industry associations that provide 
environmental compliance training. 
60

Appendix I 
Agency Response to the Draft Report 
OCT 23 2007 
MEMORANDUM 
SUBJECT: Comments of OIG Evaluation Report: “Improvements in Air Toxics 
Emissions Data Needed to Conduct Residual Risk Assessments” (OIG- 
 
Assignment No. 2005-001117) 
FROM: 
Robert J. Meyers 
Principal Deputy Assistant Administrator 
for Air and Radiation (6101A) 
TO: 
Wade T. Najjum 
Assistant Inspector General for Program Evaluation (2460T) 
We appreciate the opportunity to review and comment on the final Office of 
Inspector General (OIG) report entitled “Improvements in Air Toxics Emissions Data 
Needed to Conduct Residual Risk Assessments” (OIG-Assignment No. 2005-001117). 
The Office of Air and Radiation (OAR) disagrees with several conclusions reached by 
OIG as well as with OIG’s recommendations contained in the final report. Below, we 
explain our disagreements along with the activities we have underway to implement the 
recommendations. 
(1) 
3-1) We recommend that the Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) 
develop data quality objectives for using NEI data in conducting residual 
 
risk assessments. 
Response: It is OAR’s view that quantifiable Data Quality Objectives (DQOs) or 
acceptance criteria for use of data within the National Emissions Inventory are not 
practicable or appropriate within the current assembly and use of the National 
Emissions lnventory (NEI).  To fulfill Agency mission goals and objectives using 
data of known quality, EPA programs develop and implement supporting quality 
systems.  The Office of Air Quality Planning and Standards (OAQPS) uses an 
applied Quality System, implemented through a formal Quality Management Plan 
(QMP), to manage the quality of its environmental data collection, generation, and 
use. To expedite these concepts, OAQPS’ QMP requires that projects meeting 
certain criteria are required to create Quality Assurance Project Plans (QAPP). 
63 

For the NEI, EPA uses a Quality System that is based on an acceptance process to 
determine if the inventory is suitable for the purpose(s) for which it is to be used.         
This process includes numerous quality reviews by both EPA and our State/local            
partners. These reviews are both automated (e.g., State IT system checks) and 
professionally evaluated by experts (e.g., EPA and State staff).  The review 
process generally incorporates multiple elements such as 1) data/information flow 
monitoring and checks; 2) clear decision points in the data flow; 3) decisions by 
professional experts in assessing data elements through mass balance calculations, 
statistical determinations, and other tools; and 4) data acceptance determinations regarding 
the usability of the data/information for the intended purpose. 
These reviews are documented in the plans for inventory development which are 
updated with each Inventory Cycle (e.g., 1999, 2002, and 2005).  For the NEI, 
project specific plans, similar to a QAPP, have been prepared at the beginning of 
each 3-year project cycle. Project specific documentation has served as an 
updated plan by specifying differences from the original plan for each finalized 
version of a project. The 2005 NET data plan development is located at 
http://www.epa.gov/ttn/chief/net/2005dataplan.html. 
Also, on the EPA CHIEF website 
(http://www.epa.gov/ttn/chief/net/2002inventory.html) are the other elements of the 
DQOs and QAPPs such as the Input formats, the Consolidated Emissions Reporting 
Rule, and Documentation of the 2002 NEI: 
In the future, as resources permit, EPA can work with our partners, EPA 
researchers, and the broader scientific community to identify and incorporate new,  
and possibly quantifiable, quality measures and acceptance criteria. At this time, 
quantifiable acceptance criteria for NEI data national data elements are not 
meaningful or useful.  EPA continues to improve the NEI and is currently 
developing a new data base to improve the timeliness and quality of data and 
reports from the NEI. As we work with our partners on improving the quality and 
consistency of data provided, we will continue to consider new Data Quality 
Objectives appropriate for the use of NEI data and information. 
(2)  
3-2) We also recommend that EPA establish requirements for State reporting 
of air toxics emissions data and compliance monitoring information. 
Response: While we agree that establishing requirements for reporting air toxics 
emissions data to the EPA could improve the quality of such data, we do not 
believe such requirements are appropriate at this time.  We arc currently revising 
the requirements for State reporting of criteria pollutant emissions to EPA in the  
Air Emissions Reporting Requirements rule.  These changes were proposed last 
year and we are currently considering public comments, including a comment on 
this issue. As this is a policy issue under consideration, it is premature to convey our 
program’s position on this issue. 
64 

We note that our current, evolving HAP emission inventory efforts should 
continue to improve progress in improving HAP emissions inventory data quality. 
Working with our State and local partners and industry, we believe these efforts 
can achieve the desired data quality results. The EPA has developed national 
level inventories of HAP emissions on a 3 year cycle since 1990. The success of 
EPA’s efforts rests in large measure on the cooperation of states in submitting 
HAP data to EPA to support the development of national HAP emission 
inventories. Each emission inventory cycle has benefited from increased state 
agency participation in submitting HAP data. We believe it will he possible to 
continue developing and improving national level HAP inventories using the 
cooperative approach employed to date but intend to closely monitor the 
participation of State agencies and industry in this effort. We also expect EPA’s 
new Emissions Inventory System (EIS), when completed, to improve HAP 
inventory quality. The EIS, by further automating data handling and analysis, and 
providing inventory transparency, will facilitate identification and resolution of 
both HAP and criteria pollutant inventory data issues. We understand and 
appreciate the objectives inherit in your recommendation regarding the need for a 
HAP data reporting rule. We may revisit this recommendation in the future 
should our voluntary efforts to improve this data not meet our program needs. 
     In closing, I would like to thank the Office of the Inspector General for working 
with us in developing the final report and offering its recommendations. If you have 
additional questions after reviewing this response and the documents cited therein, please 
do not hesitate to request a briefing. Thank you for the opportunity to formally respond 
to the recommendations contained in the Office of Inspector General (OIG) report 
entitled “Improvements in Air Toxics Emissions Data Needed to conduct Residual 
Risk Assessments. 
Attachment 
cc: 
 Pete Cosier, OAR Audit Follow-up Coordinator 
Michael Boucher, OAQPS Audit Follow-up Coordinator 
Rick Beusse, Director for Program Evaluation, Air Issues, OlG 
65

Re: National Emission Standards for Hazardous Air Pollutants: Primary Copper
Smelting Residual Risk and Technology Review and Primary Copper Smelting Area
Source Technology Review (Docket ID EPA-HQ-OAR-2020-0430)
Dear Administrator Regan:
On January 11, 2022, the Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) published the
preamble to a proposed rule addressing the Residual Risk and Technology Review (RTR)
for the National Emission Standards for Hazardous Air Pollutants (NESHAP)for the major
source Primary Copper Smelters (87 Fed. Reg. 1616; Docket ID EPA-HQ-OAR-2020-
0430). As described in this letter, Freeport-McMoRan Miami Inc. (FMMI) makes two
specific requests in connection with this proposedrule:
e
FMMIrequests that EPA extend the commentperiod for this proposed rule by 45 days.
e
The preamble published in the Federal Register does not contain the proposed rule
language, and the draft rule language contained in the docket does not match the
substantive changes described in the preamble--creating significant uncertainty as to
EPA’s proposed action. Accordingly, FMMIrequests that EPA provide the proposed
rule language as the agency proposesit will appear in the Code of Federal Regulations.
The proposed Primary Copper Smelter RTR rule is of immense importance to
FMMI. The proposedrule, if implemented as proposed, will have enormous impact on the
continued operation ofFMMI’s Miamismelter potentially impacting the smelter’s viability
to operate. The proposed rule is very complex and raises numeroustechnical feasibility
issues. FMMI’sreview will require analysis by experts in several disciplines including:(1)
engineers and smelter experts to evaluate the feasibility, design and cost of the new and
novel air emission control systems; (2) environmental andstatistical experts to evaluate
EPA’s representative emission rates based on an “upper prediction limit” and stack test
data; (3) air modelers and risk assessors to evaluate conclusions based on the EPA’s
residual risk analysis; (4) smelter experts to evaluate EPA’s proposed work practice
standards; and (5) stack testing and smelter experts to evaluate EPA’s proposed compliance
demonstration requirements. All of this work takes time, and it cannot reasonably be done

during the existing commentperiod. In particular, engineers cannot assess the feasibility
and cost of the novel control technologies proposed by EPA (e.g., the injection of carbon
into the acid plant vent) in the limited time offered by the current commentperiod. As such,
an extension of the public commentperiod is warranted to provide for full and meaningful
public participation.
In addition, the proposed Primary Copper Smelter RTRrule, as published, does not
give FMMIadequate notice and the opportunity to comment on the proposed changes to
the Code of Federal Regulations. The January 11, 2022 publication in the Federal Register
does not contain the proposed changesto the text of the relevant regulations. The docket
contains a draft redline of those proposed changes dated November 4, 2021 (“Proposed
Regulation Edits for 40 CFR Part 63, Subpart QQQ: Primary Copper Smelting NESHAP
Risk and Technology Review Proposal” EPA-HQ-OAR-2020-0430-0064),
but
this
proposed rule language contains numerous substantive differences from the proposal
outlined in the preamble text. For example, the mercury emission requirements set out in
proposed 40 C.F.R. § 63.1444(j) in the November 4th draft included in the docket are
completely different from the discussion in the published preamble at 87 Fed. Reg. 1636.
Similarly, the compliance periods discussed in proposed 40 C.F.R. § 63.1443(a)(2) in the
November4th draft are not the same as the compliance periods discussed in the preamble
at 87 Fed. Reg. 1650. These inconsistencies create substantial uncertainty with regard to
the actions that EPA is proposing and impair FMMI’s ability to review EPA’s proposed
action and provide meaningful comments. As a result, FMMIrespectfully requests that
EPA provide the proposed language associated with this rulemaking as the agency
proposes will appear in the Code of Federal Regulations, and to provide a suitable
extension of the commentperiod to allow for review of the proposed rule language.
Consistent with
5 U.S.C.
§
553(c),
the regulated community must have a
meaningful opportunity to comment on proposed regulations. In general, a meaningful
opportunity includes a commentperiod ofnot less than 60 days. Exec. Order No. 12,866 §
6(a).
In this case, EPA has not provided the proposed rule language that matches the
preamble and has only provided 45 days for comments. FMMIrespectfully requests that
the agency provide the proposed rule language and extend the current commentperiod for
another 45 days.
Weappreciate your consideration of these requests.
If you have any questions,
please contact Todd Weaverat 602-509-0884 or Bryce Maresat 928-473-7060.

Re:  
Kennecott Utah Copper LLC’s Comments on the NESHAP for 
Primary Copper Smelting Residual Risk and Primary Copper 
Smelting Area Source Technology Review 
 
Administrator Regan, 
 
Kennecott Utah Copper LLC (“KUC”) submits the following comments regarding the U.S. 
Environmental Protection Agency’s (“EPA”) proposed revisions to the National Emission 
Standards for Hazardous Air Pollutants: Primary Copper Smelting Residual Risk and 
Technology Review and Primary Copper Smelting Area Source Technology Review.1 The 
proposed rule revision proposes changes to 40 CFR part 63, subpart QQQ, Primary Copper 
Smelting Major Sources, and subpart EEEEEE, Primary Copper Smelting Area Sources.  
As EPA acknowledged in the preamble to the proposed rule, KUC owns and operates the 
only area source primary copper smelter located in the United States and, consequently, is 
the only source subject to subpart EEEEEE.2 As such, KUC has unique expertise regarding 
the source category and the feasibility of controls required under the National Emission 
Standards for Hazardous Air Pollutants (“NESHAP”). The comments presented in this letter 
are specific to EPA’s solicitation for comment, clarification to the preamble, as well as 
proposed minor changes to subpart EEEEEE. 
 
                                                   
1 See Docket ID No. EPA-HQ-OAR-2020-0430; 87 Fed. Reg. 1616, 1616/1 (Jan. 11, 2022); see also 87 
Fed. Reg. 1616, January 11, 2022.  
2 87 Fed. Reg. 1620/3.  

 
 
Page 2 of 5 
 
Comment No. 1 – Correction to EPA’s Statement that Implies All Area Sources Must 
Conduct Stack Tests Every 2.5 years 
In the preamble to the proposed rule revisions, EPA states, 
The NESHAP for area sources (40 CFR part 63, subpart EEEEEE) 
established GACT [Generally Available Control Technology] standards for 
primary copper smelting areas sources and was proposed on October 6, 
2006 (71 FR 59302), and finalized on January 23, 2007 (72 FR 2930). The 
affected sources (i.e., copper concentrate dryers, smelting vessels, 
converting vessels, matter drying and grinding plants, secondary gas systems 
and anode refining departments) are subject to PM [particulate matter] limits 
as surrogate for HAP metals. Compliance must be demonstrated by 
performance tests every 2.5 years.3 
The statement is incorrect to the extent that EPA implies that all sources subject to subpart 
EEEEEE are required to stack test every 2.5 years.  
Subpart EEEEEE segregates existing sources into two categories: (1) sources using batch 
copper converters; and (2) sources not using batch copper converters.4  Under section 
63.11147, sources not using batch copper converters are required to install and operate a 
continuous particulate matter monitor and are not required to conduct stack testing.5 In 
contrast, existing sources using batch copper converters are required to conduct 
performance tests under section 63.11148(e)(2) every 2.5 years. 
As a source not using a batch copper converter, KUC is regulated under section 63.11147 
and is not required by the NESHAP to conduct a performance test every 2.5 years. KUC 
requests that EPA clarify the statement that the requirement to conduct a performance test 
every 2.5 years only applies to existing sources using batch copper converters subject to 
section 63.11148.  
Comment No. 2 – KUC Requests Clarification that Lead and Arsenic Emissions 
Calculations are Derived from Measured Emissions 
In the preamble to the proposed rule revisions, EPA states that estimates associated with 
KUC’s lead and arsenic emissions are “uncertain” because EPA does not have emissions 
                                                   
3 87 Fed. Reg. 1621/1 (emphasis added).  
4 Compare 40 CFR 63.11147 (standards and compliance requirements for existing sources not using batch 
copper converters) with 40 CFR 63.11148 (standards and compliance requirements for existing sources 
using batch copper converters). 
5 40 CFR 63.11147(b) (For purposes of determining compliance with the emissions limit in paragraph (a)(1) 
of this section . . . (1) you must calibrate, maintain and operate a system to continuously measure emissions 
of particulate matter (PM) from the smelter’s main stack.”); see also id. § 63.11149(b) (requiring a PM 
CEMS for new sources).  

 
 
Page 3 of 5 
 
test data from KUC for these pollutants.6 Furthermore, EPA specifically solicited comments, 
data, and information regarding KUC’s lead and arsenic emissions. 
While KUC does not conduct emissions stack testing for lead or arsenic, it is not accurate to 
characterize these emissions as uncertain emission estimates. Rather, than being emission 
estimates, KUC measures the overwhelming majority of its HAP metals emissions from the 
smelter main stack. As contemplated by section 63.11147(b), KUC has installed a 
continuous stack particulate sampler on its smelter main stack. While the NESHAP 
contemplates that the sampler will be used to measure compliance with the PM10 limitation 
found in section 63.11147(a), each daily PM10 sample is analyzed for the primary HAP 
constituents including lead and arsenic, and the fractions of each constituent are reported, 
as required, in the HAP section of the air emission inventory. Indeed, under KUC’s Title V 
Permit, KUC is required to measure both PM10 and Lead emissions from the main stack.7  
Approximately 85% of the emissions from KUC’s copper smelter are routed through the 
main stack, meaning that the overwhelming majority of the lead and arsenic emissions that 
are reported as part of KUC’s emission inventory are based on actual measured emissions. 
Given this foundation, KUC requests that EPA clarify its statement that lead and arsenic 
emissions are uncertain and are, in fact, overwhelmingly based on measured emissions.  
Comment No. 3 – KUC Requests the Removal or Adjustment of the Requirement to 
Submit a Monthly Report Summarizing the 24-hour Average Mass PM10 Emission 
Rates 
Section 63.11147(b)(6) requires existing area sources not operating batch converters to 
“submit to the permitting authority by the 20th day of each month a report summarizing the 
24-hour average mass PM10 emissions rates for the previous month.” In the proposed 
revision, EPA proposes to add text to this provision that requires the monthly submission to 
be made through EPA’s Compliance and Emissions Data Reporting Interface (“CEDRI”).  
KUC requests that, rather than adding the text requiring reporting through CEDRI, EPA 
strike the reporting obligation entirely (i.e., strike section 63.11147(b)(6)). 
In the alternative, KUC requests that EPA adjust the report frequency to make the report 
due either quarterly or semi-annually. KUC understands that the monthly reporting 
requirement originated as a permit condition imposed by Utah regulators following the 
modernization of KUC’s copper smelter in the late 1990s. At the time, monthly reporting was 
reasonable as regulators wanted to ensure that KUC was in continuous compliance with the 
PM10 emission limitations imposed as part of the smelter modernization.  
                                                   
6 87 Fed. Reg. 1624/1.  
7 KUC Title V Operating Permit, Permit No. 3500030004, Conditions II.B.24.a (PM10 emission limitations 
and monitoring requirements) & II.B.24.d (Lead emission limitations and monitoring requirements).  

 
 
Page 4 of 5 
 
However, with more than 25 years of operations since the smelter modernization project, 
reporting this data on a monthly basis is no longer needed to ensure continuous 
compliance. KUC has discussed this issue with the Utah Division of Air Quality (“UDAQ”) 
staff. It is KUC’s understanding that UDAQ agrees that the monthly submission of this 
information is no longer necessary.  
To be clear, KUC is only requesting relief from the obligation to report the information to the 
permitting authority be removed. KUC is not requesting that any other provisions of section 
63.11147(b) be removed or altered, meaning that sources will continue to be required to 
operate a continuous stack particulate sampler, calculation of compliance with the emission 
limitation on an average mass PM10 emission rate for each 24-hour period, and retention of 
both the monitoring records and calculated average mass PM10 emission rate for 5 years. 
As such, sources will continue to collect and calculate the emissions data for compliance 
and this information will be available for review during any inspection or whenever 
requested by the permitting authority. Furthermore, all sources subject to the compliance 
requirements of section 63.11147(b) are also subject to an affirmative duty to report 
deviations to the permitting authority.8 
In the event that EPA declines to remove the reporting obligation, KUC alternatively 
requests that EPA modify the frequency in which the report is to be submitted. As opposed 
to making the report due on the 20th day of each month, KUC requests that report be made 
due either the quarterly or with the source’s semi-annual monitoring reporting.  
Comment No. 4 – With the Removal of SSM Conditions, KUC Requests that EPA 
Remove the Conditions related to Emergency Situations 
KUC supports EPA’s proposal to remove the startup, shutdown, and malfunction (“SSM”) 
provisions from subpart EEEEEE. Additionally, KUC requests that EPA remove the 
corresponding provisions governing emergencies found in section 63.11147(c)(4) and (c)(5) 
and section 63.11148(f)(4) and (f)(5). 
KUC questions the utility of continuing to define an emergency and dictate responses to a 
qualified emergency in the text of the NESHAP given that the regulations already impose an 
obligation to maintain and operate the source consistent with the general duty to minimize 
emissions, including during SSM periods.9 Furthermore, if emergency provisions were 
                                                   
8 See 40 CFR 63.11147(d) (“Deviations. You must submit written notification to the permitting authority of

of the deviation). As a Title V source, KUC’s smelter is also subject to deviation reporting within 14 days of 
the deviation and also must list all deviations in its semi-annual monitoring report. KUC Title V Operating 
Permit, Permit No. 3500030004, Conditions I.S.2.a & I.S.2.c.    
9 See 40 CFR 63.11147(c) & 63.11147(f). Additionally, for sources not using batch copper converters, the 
retention of the emergency conditions is particularly unnecessary given that the obligations in section 
63.11147(c)(4) are largely duplicated by the subsequent provisions governing deviations found in section 
63.11147(d). 

 
 
Page 5 of 5 
 
critical to the NESHAP, KUC would anticipate that EPA would include such provisions in 
subpart QQQ as well as subpart EEEEEE. 
Closing 
KUC appreciates this opportunity to comment on the National Emission Standards for 
Hazardous Air Pollutants: Primary Copper Smelting Residual Risk and Technology Review 
and Primary Copper Smelting Area Source Technology Review. Should you or EPA staff 
have any questions about this request, please contact Jenny Esker, KUC Principal Advisor, 
Air Quality, at (801) 569-6494, or Jenny.Esker@riotinto.com.

Re: 
ASARCO LLC’S COMMENTS 
“National Emission Standards for Hazardous Air Pollutants: Primary 
Copper Smelting Residual Risk and Technology Review and Primary 
Copper Smelting Area Source Technology Review,” 87 Fed. Reg. 1616 
 
Dear U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (“EPA”): 
 
ASARCO LLC (“Asarco”), through the undersigned counsel, makes the following 
comments on the above-referenced proposed rulemaking: 
 
A. 
Mercury Test Methods 
 
All aspects of the proposal that entail the option or requirement to use Method 30A, 
Method 30B, or Hg CEMS should not be included in the final revised Subpart QQQ rules. 
 
1. 
There is no discussion, in the proposed rulemaking preamble or supporting 
materials in the rulemaking docket, of any justification for the proposed rules’ imposition of the 
option or requirement to use Method 30A, Method 30B, or Hg CEMS to demonstrate compliance 
with the proposed rules’ numeric Hg emissions limits, or any consideration of the practicability 
of doing so.  The only reference within the entire rulemaking docket to Method 30A and Method 
30B is in the portion of the preamble which states EPA identified no applicable voluntary 
consensus standard for the methods.  87 Fed. Reg. at 1653/3.  This is a failure of EPA’s 
obligation to “examine the relevant data and articulate a satisfactory explanation for its action 
including a ‘rational connection between the facts found and the choices made.’”  Motor Vehicle 
Mfrs. Ass’n v. State Farm Mut. Auto. Inc. Co., 463 U.S. 29, 43 (1983) (quoting Burlington Truck 
Lines, Inc. v. United States, 371 U.S. 156, 168 (1962)).  Therefore, EPA should either: (a) 
exclude from the final rules the option or requirement to use Method 30A, Method 30B, or Hg 
CEMS; or (b) issue a supplemental notice of proposed rulemaking that (i) states EPA’s 
justification for the proposed rules’ imposition of the option or requirement to use Method 30A, 

  
 
 
 
Docket ID No. EPA-HQ-OAR-2020-0430 
April 26, 2022 
Page 2 of 15 
 
George A. Tsiolis 
Attorney at Law 
Method 30B, or Hg CEMS to demonstrate compliance with the proposed rules’ numeric Hg 
emissions limits, (ii) examines the practicability of doing so, and (iii) invites the public to 
comment accordingly. 
 
2. 
According to the rulemaking preamble and MACT floor analysis in the 
rulemaking docket: (a) the proposed existing-sources Hg emissions limit of 0.0043 lbs/hr 
“reflects a 90 percent reduction from the acid plant portion of the UPL MACT floor level of 0.01 
lbs/hr”; (b) the MACT floor of 0.01 lbs/hr is derived using a 99% UPL of 14.6 μg/dscm; (c) the 
99% UPL of 14.6 μg/dscm was used because it was greater than 3 times an RDL of 1.044 
μg/dscm; and (d) the RDL of 1.044 μg/dscm was derived solely using Method 29 test data.  87 
Fed. Reg. at 1636/1-2; MACT Floor Analysis, Doc. 55.  This means the proposed 0.0043 lbs/hr 
limit is a function of emissions data gathered solely using Method 29.  As such, it would be 
inappropriate to require or authorize the use of Hg CEMS, or any method other than Method 29, 
to demonstrate compliance with the proposed 0.0043 lbs/hr limit.  See 42 U.S.C. § 7412(d)(3)(B) 
(“Emission standards promulgated under this subsection for existing sources in a category or 
subcategory . . . shall not be less stringent, and may be more stringent than . . . the average 
emission limitation achieved by the best performing 5 sources (for which the Administrator has 
or could reasonably obtain emissions information) in the category or subcategory for categories 
or subcategories with fewer than 30 sources.”);  Sierra Club v. EPA, 353 F.3d 976, 984 (D.C. 
Cir. 2004) (“EPA complied with Section 7412(d)(3) by setting emission limits on the basis of the 
[pollutant] control that the best sources actually achieved.”). 
 
 
B. 
Mercury CEMS Data Averaging Period 
 
EPA should reconsider the 2-hour rolling-average provision in proposed § 63.1453(g). 
 
1. 
There is nothing in the rulemaking docket that justifies a 2-hour rolling-
average basis; or that explains why rolling averages specifically on a 2-hour basis, as opposed to 
a 4-hour, 8-hour, 24-hour, 30-day, 180-day, 365-day, or other basis, are appropriate.  This is a 
failure of EPA’s obligation to “examine the relevant data and articulate a satisfactory explanation 
for its action including a ‘rational connection between the facts found and the choices made.’” 
Motor Vehicle Mfrs. Ass’n, 463 U.S. at 43. 
 
2. 
As stated above, the proposed 0.0043 lbs/hr limit “reflects a 90 percent 
reduction from the acid plant portion of the UPL MACT floor level of 0.01 lbs/hr.”  87 Fed. Reg. 
at 1636/2.  This means that the proposed 0.0043 lbs/hr limit is a function ultimately of 99% UPL 
statistical analysis of Method 29 test data—for which reason it would be appropriate to require 
the use of Method 29 test data to demonstrate compliance with 0.0043 lbs/hr limit.  See id.  
Conversely, it would be inappropriate for EPA to include within Subpart QQQ a requirement or 
authorization to use 2-hour rolling averages of Hg CEMS data to demonstrate compliance with a 
numeric Hg emissions limit, unless the numeric limit is a function of 99% UPL statistical 

  
 
 
 
Docket ID No. EPA-HQ-OAR-2020-0430 
April 26, 2022 
Page 3 of 15 
 
George A. Tsiolis 
Attorney at Law 
analysis of 2-hour rolling averages of relevant Hg CEMS data.  See 42 U.S.C. § 7412(d)(3)(B); 
Sierra Club v. EPA, 353 F.3d at 984. 
 
3. 
The consent decree that was entered in 2015 and currently governs 
Asarco’s operation of the Hayden smelter employs an aggressive 8-hour rolling average limit for 
the PM CEMS data employed to demonstrate compliance with Subpart QQQ’s current process-
stack numeric PM emissions limits.  Doc. 18, ¶¶ 16.a, 17.a, 18.a, 19.a, 20.a.  Moreover, the 
consent decree recognizes the potential that “inherent process variability precludes compliance 
with a PM emission limit set forth in Paragraphs 16-20 on an 8-hour rolling average basis at one 
or more of the exhaust streams,” for which reasons the decree gives Asarco the option to request, 
and EPA to grant, “a longer averaging period, not to exceed 24 hours.”  Doc. 18, ¶ 21.  There is 
nothing in the rulemaking preamble or supporting materials in the rulemaking docket that 
indicates why the 2-hour rolling-average period for Hg CEMS data in proposed § 63.1453(g) 
would be appropriate when no lesser than an 8-hour rolling average period was deemed 
appropriate for PM CEMS data, for the same exhaust streams.  Therefore, if, comments A.1 and 
A.2 (above) notwithstanding, EPA determines to include in a final rulemaking the option or 
requirement to use Hg CEMS to demonstrate compliance with the proposed rules’ numeric Hg 
emissions limits, then the averaging period in proposed § 63.1453(g (1)(iv) should be raised to 
24 hours or greater;1 and included in the final rulemaking should be a fulsome explanation of the 
scientific justification of the averaging period, in keeping with the obligation stated in 42 U.S.C. 
§ 7412(d)(3)(B). 
 
C. 
Mercury Site-Specific Operating Limit 
 
The proposed rules’ provisions on site-specific operating limits and operating-parameter 
alternatives for demonstrating initial and continuous compliance with the proposed numeric Hg 
emissions limit are materially unclear and need substantial clarification as to EPA’s intent. 
 
 
1. 
Proposed § 63.1451(j) states initial compliance has been demonstrated if, 
inter alia, a “site-specific operating limit for a parameter” has been established “based on values 
measured during the performance test you conducted to demonstrate initial compliance.”  
Similarly, proposed § 63.1453(g)(2) provides the option of demonstrating continuous compliance 
by monitoring “the operating parameter established during the [initial] performance tests.”  
However, the only provisions of the rule that would seem to correspond to proposed §§ 
63.1451(j) and 63.1453(g)(2) concern the development of operating limits and appropriate 
parameters for control devices other than a baghouse or venturi wet scrubber—for example, 
proposed §§ 63.1450(d)(3) and 63.1452(d).  Whereas, for situations in which the control device 
 
 
1 The imposition of the 8- to 24-hour averaging periods in the consent decree reflected EPA’s muscular 
exercise of the court’s equity jurisdiction. 

  
 
 
 
Docket ID No. EPA-HQ-OAR-2020-0430 
April 26, 2022 
Page 4 of 15 
 
George A. Tsiolis 
Attorney at Law 
is a baghouse or venturi wet scrubber, the proposed rules are unclear how initial compliance 
would be demonstrated, unless the intent of proposed § 63.1451(j) is that the conditions in either 
paragraph (j)(1) or paragraph (j)(2) must be met; and unclear how continuous compliance would 
be demonstrated, unless the intent of proposed § 63.1453(g) is that the requirements in 
paragraphs (g)(1), or (2), or (3) must be met. 
 
2. 
Overall, it is unclear what EPA’s basic intent is, on how the operating-
parameter alternatives for demonstrating initial and continuous compliance with the Hg 
emissions limit in proposed § 63.1444 would work.  EPA should issue a supplemental notice of 
proposed rulemaking that explains the operating-parameter alternatives methodology—
including, without limitation, what kinds of representative parameters are envisioned—and invite 
the public to comment accordingly; otherwise, EPA should restrict the method of demonstrating 
initial and continuous compliance to performance testing using Method 29. 
 
3. 
The parameters of pressure drop and flow rate, notably, correlate to 
particulate matter; whereas, much of the Hg emitted at the smelters is in vapor form, for which 
reason PM does not serve as a surrogate.  87 Fed. Reg. at 1636/1.  In addition, unlike PM 
emissions, Hg emissions are highly dependent on the composition of the concentrate feed to the 
smelter.  Therefore, it would seem impossible to establish a PM-based parametric alternative for 
demonstrating initial and continuous compliance with the Hg emissions limit in proposed § 
63.1444. 
  
D. 
Reduced-Frequency Monitoring of Mercury Emissions 
 
Hg emissions at the Hayden smelter are already extremely low, as reflected in the 
derivation of the proposed MACT floor limit, for new sources, of 0.00097 lbs/hr.  87 Fed. Reg. 
at 1636/1.  Given such low rates, EPA should include in the final rulemaking a provision that, if 
demonstrations of continuous compliance show Hg emissions rates are below a certain 
percentage of the applicable numeric limit for a specific period time, then the frequency of the 
demonstration of continuous compliance can be reduced.  There is ample precedent for this 
approach, among the NESHAPs that concern Hg emissions.  See, e.g., 40 C.F.R. § 63.7515(b) 
(industrial boilers and process heaters); 40 C.F.R. § 63.10005(h) (low-emitting electric utility 
steam generating units). 
 
E. 
Combined Fugitive PM Emissions Limit at the Roofline 
 
Proposed 40 C.F.R. § 63.1444 would require fugitive PM emissions not to exceed 4.3 
lbs/hr “from the smelter building roofline vents,” 1.7 lbs/hr “from the converter department 
roofline vents,” and 1.6 lbs/hr “from the anode refining department roofline vents.”  This reflects 
an intention to limit fugitive PM mass emissions rates regardless of fugitive PM concentration.  
Nonetheless, such an approach to regulating roofline fugitive PM emissions makes no sense for 

  
 
 
 
Docket ID No. EPA-HQ-OAR-2020-0430 
April 26, 2022 
Page 5 of 15 
 
George A. Tsiolis 
Attorney at Law 
the Hayden smelter, since: (1) the majority of fugitive emissions at the roofline arise from 
secondary and transfer activities between the smelting, converting and anode-refining processes;2 
(2) the configuration of these three departments ensures that such fugitive PM emissions 
comingle substantially before exiting the roofline vents; (3) the extents of this comingling vary 
materially as a function of wind and heat conditions; and (4) determining empirically (using the 
methods specified in proposed § 63.1450(e)-(f)) whether any of the proposed three individual 
emissions rates has been exceeded would, as a result, be impossible.  FMI’s Miami smelter 
would experience similar conditions. 
 
In addition, the fugitive PM emission data that EPA used to develop the proposed 4.3 
lbs/hr and 1.7 lbs/hr limits were generated at the Miami smelter.  MACT Floor Analysis, Doc. 
55.  These proposed individual limits would not be correspondingly applicable to fugitive PM 
emissions from Hayden’s smelting furnace and converters because the smelting-furnace 
technology employed at Hayden yields a lower matte grade than the Miami smelting furnace, 
which means that significantly more of the smelting and converting process occurs in Hayden’s 
converter department than in Miami’s converter department. 
 
The final rulemaking should normalize these conditions by replacing the proposed three 
individual emissions rates with one combined mass emissions rate—which, unlike the proposed 
three individual emissions rates, could be practicably enforceable: 
 
You must not discharge to the atmosphere process fugitive gases 
from the smelter-building, converter-department, and anode 
refining-department roofline vents containing filterable particular 
matter emissions in excess of 7.6 lbs/hr as measured using the test 
methods specified in § 63.1450(e). 
 
The final rulemaking should, correspondingly, specify test methods that apply to all 
process fugitive PM emissions at the roofline, irrespective of their source.  At first blush, this 
would entail combining paragraphs (e) and (f) of proposed § 63.1450 into a single paragraph (e).  
However, see Comment F (below). 
 
 
 
 
2 The process gases are already well-captured and controlled as a result of the capture-and-control 
technologies and operational requirements imposed and implemented under the consent decree.  Doc. 18, 
¶¶ 8, 9, 13, 24, 26 and 27.  The result is that roofline PM emissions arise primarily from: (i) matte ladles, 
tapped from the smelting furnace, that are picked up from the matte tunnel exits and carried via crane to 
the converters; (ii) ladles of slag from the converters that are returned and poured into the smelting 
furnace; (iii) routine cleaning of the ladles; (iv) blister copper that is transferred via cranes to the anode 
furnaces; and (v) anode slag that is returned to the converters via crane. 

  
 
 
 
Docket ID No. EPA-HQ-OAR-2020-0430 
April 26, 2022 
Page 6 of 15 
 
George A. Tsiolis 
Attorney at Law 
F. 
Test Methods Proposed for Roofline Fugitive PM Emissions 
 
The test methods specified in paragraphs (e) and (f) of proposed § 63.1450 are not 
practicable for demonstrating compliance with any fugitive PM emissions-rate limit at the 
roofline. 
 
1. 
Method 1 cannot be applied for the purposes stated in subparagraphs 
(e)(1) and (f)(1) of proposed § 63.1450. 
 
a. 
As § 1.2 of Method 1 provides, “the method is applicable to gas 
streams flowing in ducts, stacks and flues.”  This is reflected in subparagraphs (e)(1)(ii)-(iii) and 
(f)(1)(ii)-(iii) of proposed § 63.1450, which require determinations of volumetric flow rate and 
dry molecular weight of the “stack gas.”  There is nothing in the rulemakings governing Method 
1 that indicates an intent that it be applicable to roofline fugitive emissions or that it can 
practicably be applied to roofline fugitive emissions.  Therefore, the proposed rulemaking’s 
assumption that Method 1 is applicable and can be practicably applied for the purposes stated in 
subparagraphs (e)(1) and (f)(1) of proposed § 63.1450 is without foundation. 
 
b. 
Method 1 provides two alternative procedures: a “simplified 
procedure,” and an “alternative procedure.”   The “simplified procedure,” according to § 1.2, 
“cannot be used when the measurement site is less than two stack or duct diameters or less than a 
half diameter upstream from a flow disturbance.”  Neither stack diameters nor duct diameters 
can be defined for the smelter facilities’ roofline vents, within the meaning and purposes of § 
1.2.  For this reason, as well, the “simplified procedure” cannot be practicably applied for the 
purposes stated in subparagraphs (e)(1) and (f)(1) of proposed § 63.1450. 
 
c. 
The “alternative procedure” is described in § 11.5 of Method 1.  It 
depends on the ability to develop representative pitch and yaw angles of the gas flow to be 
sampled, based on directional flow-sensing probe measurements of pitch and yaw angles at forty 
or more traverse points within the flow.  It is impossible to perform this procedure at the smelter 
facilities’ roofline vents because fugitive emissions at the vents occur at a variety of angles that 
are constantly changing due to ambient winds.  For this reason, as well, the “alternative 
procedure” cannot be practicably applied for the purposes stated in subparagraphs (e)(1) and 
(f)(1) of proposed § 63.1450. 
 
2. 
Method 5D cannot be applied for the purposes stated in subparagraphs 
(e)(1) and (f)(1) of proposed § 63.1450. 
 
a. 
As § 1.2.1 of Method 5D states, “this method is applicable for the 
determination of PM emissions from positive pressure fabric filters.”  There is nothing in the 
rulemakings governing Method 5D that indicates an intent that it be applicable to roofline 

  
 
 
 
Docket ID No. EPA-HQ-OAR-2020-0430 
April 26, 2022 
Page 7 of 15 
 
George A. Tsiolis 
Attorney at Law 
fugitive emissions or that it can practicably be applied to roofline fugitive emissions.  Therefore, 
the proposed rulemaking’s assumption that Method 5D is applicable and can be practicably 
applied for the purposes stated in subparagraphs (e)(1) and (f)(1) of proposed § 63.1450 is 
without foundation. 
 
b. 
Central to Method 5D is the requirement that the sample gas be 
withdrawn iso-kinetically from the source; i.e., the velocity of the gas withdrawn into the sample 
probe must more or less match the velocity of the gas stream at that sample-withdrawal point.  
This velocity-matching ensures that the concentrations of solids and droplets in the sample are 
representative of the concentrations of solids and droplets in the gas stream being sampled.  
However, due to the varying ambient winds and temperature conditions affecting fugitive 
emissions within the building, flows at the roofline vents are highly variable, and would be so for 
the duration of a sampling event, making the iso-kinetic sampling required by Method 5D 
practically impossible. 
 
3. 
Oregon Method 8 cannot be applied for the purposes stated in 
subparagraphs (e)(1) and (f)(1) of proposed § 63.1450. 
 
a. 
According to § 1.2. of Oregon DEQ’s Source Sampling Manuel 
(rev. November 2018), Oregon Method 8 is applicable to point (i.e., “stack”) sources, not 
fugitive emissions.  There is nothing in the public record that indicates an intent that Oregon 
Method 8 be applicable to roofline fugitive emissions or that it can practicably be applied to 
roofline fugitive emissions.  Therefore, the proposed rulemaking’s assumption that Oregon 
Method 8 is applicable and can be practicably applied for the purposes stated in subparagraphs 
(e)(1) and (f)(1) of proposed § 63.1450 is without foundation. 
 
b. 
Central to Oregon Method 8 is the requirement that the sample gas 
be withdrawn iso-kinetically from the source; i.e., the velocity of the gas withdrawn into the 
sample probe must more or less match the velocity of the gas stream at that sample-withdrawal 
point.  This velocity-matching ensures that the concentrations of solids and droplets in the 
sample are representative of the concentrations of solids and droplets in the gas stream being 
sampled.  However, due to the varying ambient winds and temperature conditions affecting 
fugitive emissions within the building, flows at the roofline vents are highly variable, and would 
be so for the duration of a sampling event, making the iso-kinetic sampling required by Oregon 
Method 8 practically impossible. 
 
 
 
4. 
In general, iso-kinetic test methods such as Method 5 and Oregon Method 
8 are applicable to forced-draft emissions sources with more or less constant exhaust-flow rates.  
In contrast, roofline fugitive emissions are not subject to a forced draft and occur at highly 
variable rates.  Indeed, during high wind events, the air pressure above the roofline can exceed 
the force of the upward draft of fugitive emissions, at some of the vents.  

  
 
 
 
Docket ID No. EPA-HQ-OAR-2020-0430 
April 26, 2022 
Page 8 of 15 
 
George A. Tsiolis 
Attorney at Law 
 
5. 
For the above reasons, if Subpart QQQ is going to be revised to include 
compliance monitoring of fugitive PM emissions at the roofline, then another test method needs 
to be considered.  See Comment G (below). 
 
G. 
Portable Air Samplers of Roofline Fugitive PM Emissions 
 
The consent decree that was entered in 2015 and currently governs Asarco’s operation of 
the Hayden smelter requires the performance of process fugitive emissions studies, pursuant to a 
protocol that is approved by EPA.  Doc. 18, ¶ 22.  One of the purposes of this requirement is to 
quantify process fugitive PM emissions “from 1) all processing steps from the copper 
concentrate dryers through the anode casting department, inclusive of those end points, and 2) 
the entire building(s) housing the smelting flash furnace, copper converter department, and anode 
furnaces.”  Pursuant to this requirement, Asarco on January 20, 2017 submitted to EPA a 
protocol for the conduct of the fugitive emissions studies (“FES Protocol” or “Protocol”).  EPA 
approved the Protocol on May 24. 2017.  Copies of the Protocol and EPA’s approval of the 
Protocol are provided as Attachment 1 to these comments. 
 
1. 
Within the FES Protocol is a determination that process fugitive PM 
emissions at the roofline shall be quantified via a sampling methodology that centers on: (a) the 
use of MiniVol portable air samplers at the roofline vents; and (b) collocating, with the samplers, 
temperature sensors and axial-flow sensors or pilot tubes to determine the volumes and rates of 
flow at the vents.  EPA’s approval of the Protocol constitutes a determination by EPA that this 
sampling method is appropriate for determining the rate of fugitive PM emissions at the roofline.  
The MiniVol sampler, in particular, is a low-flow sampler, which is well-suited to low, variable 
air flows at the roofline—unlike the iso-kinetic sampling methods specified in subparagraphs 
(e)(1) and (f)(1) of proposed § 63.1450. 
 
2. 
The MiniVol sampler, though jointly developed by EPA with the 
manufacturer, Airmetrics (http://www.airmetrics.com/company.html), is not part of an EPA-
certified reference method or equivalent method.  However, as stated in the FES Protocol, 
sampling results obtained using MiniVol samplers have been documented to closely approximate 
results obtained using EPA-certified reference-method samplers. 
 
3. 
Other relevant attributes of MiniVol samplers are that: (a) they can be run 
concurrently at several locations along the roofline, which better captures fluctuations in flows 
and emissions along the roofline; (b) they can be run for long times, allowing for the collection 
of samples representative of the variable process operations and heat and climactic conditions 
occurring within the building; (c) the samplers’ base-pump system can be kept outside of the 
roofline vents, using a sample line located between the sampler and the filter assembly, so that 

  
 
 
 
Docket ID No. EPA-HQ-OAR-2020-0430 
April 26, 2022 
Page 9 of 15 
 
George A. Tsiolis 
Attorney at Law 
the elevated temperature environment at the roofline does not pose a continuous safety problem; 
and (d) the samplers’ size makes them suitable for the confined spaces of the roofline vents. 
 
4. 
Based on the foregoing, including EPA’s approval of the FES protocol, 
the final rulemaking should include a provision that explicitly authorizes the use of MiniVol 
portable air samplers, together with appropriate temperature and flow sensors, to determine the 
rate of fugitive PM emissions at the roofline and whether those rates exceed the combined 
fugitive PM emissions-rate limit described in Comment E (above), “pursuant to a fugitive 
emissions monitoring protocol specific to the relevant smelter that is submitted to, and approved 
by, EPA OAR, OAQPS, Measurement Technology Group” or such other reviewing body that 
EPA may deem appropriate (such as the Arizona DEQ).  Asarco believes: (a) six months after 
the date of the final rulemaking’s publication in the Federal Register would be an appropriate 
deadline for submittal of the protocol for agency approval; and (b) two years after agency 
approval of the protocol would be an appropriate deadline for commencing measurements of the 
rate of fugitive PM emissions at the roofline to determine whether they exceed the fugitive PM 
emissions-rate limit.3 
 
H. 
Potential Lead Limit 
 
Asarco agrees with EPA’s determination not to propose a BTF lead emissions limit in 
addition to, or instead of, the fugitive PM emissions limit in proposed § 63.1444(i)(3).  87 Fed. 
Reg. at 1635/2-3.  Asarco adopts, as part of these comments, the reasoning underlying EPA’s 
determination.  Id.; MACT Floor Analysis, Doc. 55, § 4.7.  In addition, Asarco’s reasons in favor 
of a combined fugitive PM emissions-rate limit at the roofline, in Comment E (above), militate 
against a BTF lead emissions limit on process fugitive emissions arising specifically from the 
anode refining process. 
 
I. 
Applicability of Emissions Standards at All Times 
 
Proposed § 63.1448(a) provides that, on or after 180 days after publication of the final 
rulemaking in the Federal Register, “you must be in compliance with the emission limitations, 
work practice standards, and operation and maintenance requirements in this subpart at all 
times.” 
 
1. 
Proposed § 63.1448(a), if included in the final rulemaking, would violate 
42 U.S.C. § 7412(d)(3)(B), which requires the emissions standards promulgated under that 
 
 
3 Cf. Proposed § 63.1443(a)(1) (“For existing . . .smelting building roof vents, converter department roof 
vents, and anode refining department roof vents, you must comply with each emission limitation . . . in 
this subpart that applies to you no later than [INSERT DATE 2 YEARS AFTER DATE OF 
PUBLICATION OF THE FINAL RULE IN THE FEDERAL REGISTER].”). 

  
 
 
 
Docket ID No. EPA-HQ-OAR-2020-0430 
April 26, 2022 
Page 10 of 15 
 
George A. Tsiolis 
Attorney at Law 
subsection for existing sources in a category or subcategory to be based empirically or 
predictively on “the average emission limitation achieved by the best performing 5 sources (for 
which the Administrator has or could reasonably obtain emissions information) in the category 
or subcategory for categories or subcategories with fewer than 30 sources.”  This is because there 
is no indication in the public record that the numeric emissions limits in the current Subpart 
QQQ rules and proposed rules are based, at least in part, on emissions data from primary copper 
smelters that were gathered during periods of startup and shutdown.  See also Sierra Club v. 
EPA, 353 at 984 (“EPA complied with Section 7412(d)(3) by setting emission limits on the basis 
of the [pollutant] control that the best sources actually achieved.”). 
 
2. 
Until such time as EPA develops and promulgates MACT or BTF numeric 
emissions limits based on emissions data from the relevant primary copper smelting sources that 
include emissions data generated during periods of startup and shutdown, the MACT and BTF 
numeric emissions limits in the current Subpart QQQ rules and proposed rules cannot, as a 
matter of law, be applied to emissions that occur during periods of startup and shutdown.  This is 
not avoided by the several provisions in the proposal that would entail the conduct of 
performance tests and relative-accuracy testing of CEMS under “normal operating condition.” 
 
3. 
Nor does the decision in Sierra Club v. EPA, 551 F. 3d 1019 (D.C. Cir. 
2008), provide EPA carte blanche to sidestep the fundamental requirement of 42 U.S.C. § 
7412(d)(3)(B).  The 2-1 majority in Sierra Club took issue with what it perceived to be an 
abdication by EPA of its statutory obligation to promulgate NESHAPs that apply on a 
continuous basis, but it explicitly recognized that different numeric emissions limits and 
standards can apply to situations of startup and shutdown.  See 551 F.3d at 1027 (stating the 
definition of “emission limitation” and “emission standard” in 42 U.S.C. § 7602(k) “suggests 
that emissions reduction requirements ‘assure continuous emission reduction’ without 
necessarily continuously applying a single standard”).4 
 
4. 
If EPA is going to meet the requirement of 42 U.S.C. § 7412(d)(3)(B), in 
keeping with the majority’s decision in Sierra Club, then the only kinds of MACT or BTF 
numeric emissions limits it may apply to periods of startup and shutdown would be numeric 
limits promulgated based wholly or appropriately in part on relevant emissions data gathered 
during periods of startup and shutdown.   Proposed § 63.1448(a), as written, evades this basic 
requirement.  
 
 
 
4 The central holding of Sierra Club was that, “[b]ecause the general duty [to minimize emissions, at 40 
C.F.R. § 63.6(e)(1)(i)] is the only standard [in the rulemaking at bar] that applies during SSM events—
and accordingly no section 112 standard governs these events—the SSM exemption violates the CAA’s 
requirement that some section 112 standard apply continuously.” 551 F.3d at 1028 (emphasis added). 

  
 
 
 
Docket ID No. EPA-HQ-OAR-2020-0430 
April 26, 2022

George A. Tsiolis 
Attorney at Law 
5. 
Until EPA is able to promulgate, based on startup- and shutdown-
emissions data, Subpart QQQ numeric emissions limits that apply to periods of startup and 
shutdown, EPA should follow the logic of previous NESHAPs rulemakings, upheld in the courts, 
that imposed work practice standards applicable to periods of startup and shutdown instead of 
numeric emissions limits.  See Comment J.4 (below).  
 
J. 
Work Practice Standards Instead of Numeric Emissions Limits 
 
Under 42 U.S.C. § 7412(h)(1)-(2)(B), if EPA determines that it is not feasible to 
prescribe a numeric emissions limit because “the application of a measurement methodology to 
[the] particular class or source is not practicable due to technological or economic limitations,” 
then EPA may promulgate a “design, equipment, work practice, or operational standard, or 
combination thereof,” instead of a numeric emissions limit.  See, e.g., Sierra Club v. EPA, 884 
F.3d 1185, 1190 (D.C. Cir. 2018) (stating work practice standards can be thought of as a 
“statutory Plan B,” which EPA can resort to “when using numeric limits is ‘not feasible’”). 
 
1. 
In the mercury and air toxics standards rulemaking (“MATS 
Rulemaking”), for example, EPA proposed numeric emissions limits applicable to periods of 
startup and shutdown; received public comments on the lack, in the record, of adequate startup- 
and shutdown-emissions data and “concerns regarding the reliability of measurements obtained 
from EGUs during such periods”; and, in response, issued a final rule that eliminated the 
numeric emissions limits for periods of startup and shutdown and, in their stead, promulgated 
work practice standards applicable to the periods of startup and shutdown.  77 Fed. Reg. 9304, 
9380-81 (February 16, 2012).  On judicial review, the court remanded the rulemaking, not 
because it found fault with EPA’s determination to impose work practices standards instead of 
numeric emissions limits for periods of startup and shutdown, but rather on the grounds that the 
public had not had sufficient opportunity to comment on EPA’s determination “that the ‘best 
performing’ power plants cannot reliably measure emissions until four hours after they generate 
electricity.”  Chesapeake Climate Action Network v. EPA, 952 F.3d 310, 318-19 (D.C. Cir. 
2020).  In other words, the question on the merits, warranting remand, concerned the duration of 
startup after which numeric emissions limits should apply.  Neither the court’s opinion nor 
subsequent EPA rulemaking or litigation concerning the MATS Rulemaking (including the 
litigation of EPA’s May 2020 rollback of the MATS Rulemaking) has found fault with EPA’s 
basic determination that, in the absence of a reliable means of measuring emissions during 
startup and shutdown, work practice standards instead of numeric emission limits for periods of 
startup and shutdown are appropriate for those NESHAPs. 
 
2. 
There are other examples, in the NESHAPs, of EPA’s application of work 
practice standards instead of numeric emissions limits, when there are no reliable means of 
measuring emissions during startup and shutdown.  See, e.g., 76 Fed. Reg. 15608, 15613 (March 
21, 2011) (“Industrial Boilers Rulemaking”) (determining to apply work practice standards 

  
 
 
 
Docket ID No. EPA-HQ-OAR-2020-0430 
April 26, 2022 
Page 12 of 15 
 
George A. Tsiolis 
Attorney at Law 
instead of numeric emissions limits to startup and shutdown emissions because it was “not 
technically feasible” for boiler operators to conduct the emissions testing necessary to enforce 
numeric limits during startup and shutdown); Sierra Club v. EPA, 884 F.3d at 1199-1204 
(upholding that determination). 
 
3. 
As explained in Comment F (above), Method 1, Method 5D, and Oregon 
Method 8 are not appropriate for the purpose of measuring the rate of fugitive PM emissions at 
the roofline to determine whether they exceed a fugitive PM emissions-rate limit.  Also, as 
explained in Comment G.2 (above), the MiniVol sampler is not part of an EPA-certified 
reference method or equivalent method.   Given these considerations, the final rulemaking 
should provide that the roofline fugitive PM emissions-rate limit that is imposed in the final 
rulemaking shall not apply in advance of EPA’s approval of the fugitive emissions-monitoring 
protocol described in Comment G.4 above and a reasonable period of time needed to implement 
the approved protocol.  Here, Asarco reiterates its suggestion in Comment G.4, that: (a) six 
months after the date of the final rulemaking’s publication in the Federal Register would be an 
appropriate deadline for submittal of the protocol for agency approval; and (b) two years after 
agency approval of the protocol would be an appropriate deadline for commencing 
measurements of the rate of fugitive PM emissions at the roofline to determine whether they 
exceed the fugitive PM emissions-rate limit.  Correspondingly, the final rulemaking should 
provide that, during the pendency of the protocol’s implementation, only the work practice 
standards and operation and maintenance requirements of the revised Subpart QQQ rules shall 
apply to the process fugitive PM emissions.  This would be consistent with 42 U.S.C. § 
7412(h)(1)-(2)(B) and the approach EPA took in the MATS and Industrial Boilers rulemakings. 
 
4. 
In relation to Comment I (above), both the MATS Rulemaking and the 
Industrial Boilers Rulemaking based their determinations to apply work practice standards, 
instead of numeric emissions limits, to periods of startup and shutdown, not only on the lack of 
reliable means of measuring emissions during startup and shutdown, but also on the scarcity or 
unavailability of emissions data generated during periods of startup and shutdown which made it 
impracticable to develop defensible numeric emissions limits that apply to periods of startup and 
shutdown.  77 Fed. Reg. at 9381 (“Thus, we do not have sufficient data on emissions that occur 
during startup and shutdown on which to set emission standards.  We are therefore establishing 
work practice standards rather than numeric emissions standards for periods of startup and 
shutdown in the final rule.”; 952 F.3d at 318-19 (remanding on other grounds); 76 Fed. Reg. at 
15641-42 (“Numerous commenters raised concerns that insufficient data are currently available 
to establish emission standards for SSM events . . . EPA has considered these comments and has 
revised this final rule to incorporate a work practice standard for periods of startup and 
shutdown”); 884 F.3d at 1199-1204 (upholding that decision).  Likewise, the final Subpart QQQ 
rulemaking should provide that, based on the scarcity or unavailability in the rulemaking dockets 
of startup- and shutdown-emissions data from the relevant primary copper smelting sources, and 
until such data are gathered and appropriately used to promulgate Subpart QQQ numeric 

  
 
 
 
Docket ID No. EPA-HQ-OAR-2020-0430 
April 26, 2022 
Page 13 of 15 
 
George A. Tsiolis 
Attorney at Law 
emissions limits that apply to periods of startup and shutdown, only the work practice standards 
and operation and maintenance requirements of the revised Subpart QQQ rules shall apply to 
periods of startup and shutdown. 
 
5. 
Without limiting the scope of Comment J.4 (above), Asarco notes that: (a) 
during EPA’s development of the proposed rulemaking, EPA asked Asarco whether it was aware 
of any issues that the Hayden smelter might have with a requirement to be in compliance with 
Subpart QQQ standards “at all times”; and (b) Asarco responded that, if Subpart QQQ were to be 
revised accordingly, then, at a minimum, the numeric emissions limit applicable to the acid plant, 
at § 63.1444(b)(1) and (d)(5), would need to be revised, somehow, to account for acid plant 
emissions that occur during periods of acid plant startup and shutdown.  See Attachment 2.5  
Asarco’s response also indicated that it may be impossible to generate reliable measurements of 
nonsulfuric acid PM emissions from the acid plant during periods of acid plant startup and 
shutdown.  Id.  Asarco’s two attempts to achieve PS-11 correlation of a PM CEMS at the acid 
plant tail gas, pursuant to the terms of the Hayden smelter consent decree, Doc. 18, ¶¶ 14-15, 
were unsuccessful.  See Attachment 3.6  In the absence of a reliable means of measuring 
nonsulfuric acid PM emissions during acid-plant startup and shutdown, the final rulemaking 
should state that the 6.2 mg/dscm limit of § 63.1444(b)(1) and (d)(5) shall not apply to a primary 
copper smelter’s acid plant outside of the plant’s normal operating conditions; and that only the 
work practice standards and operation and maintenance requirements of the revised Subpart 
QQQ rules shall govern startup and shutdown emissions at the acid plant.7  This would be 
consistent with 42 U.S.C. § 7412(h)(1)-(2) and the approach EPA took in the MATS and 
Industrial Boilers rulemakings. 
 
K. 
Residual Risk 
 
Asarco adopts, as part of these comments, the reasoning of EPA’s residual risk 
assessment concerning the proposed rulemaking, and offers the following additional information 
for EPA’s consideration. 
 
In the proposed rulemaking, EPA states as follows: 
 
 
 
5 Asarco incorporates all of its statements in Attachment 2 as part of these comments. 
6 Asarco has committed to EPA to make a third attempt at PS-11 correlation of a PM CEMS at the acid 
plant tail gas, after Asarco resumes operation of the smelter. 
7 For purposes of this Comment J.5, Asarco believes an acid plant’s normal operating conditions (post-
startup and pre-shutdown) are defined by the period of time during which at least one acid plant blower is 
operating and the minimum catalyst-bed temperature required to sustain the plant’s conversion of SO2 to 
SO3 is maintained at all the catalyst beds without contributions of heat from a preheater or other external 
source of heat. 

  
 
 
 
Docket ID No. EPA-HQ-OAR-2020-0430 
April 26, 2022 
Page 14 of 15 
 
George A. Tsiolis 
Attorney at Law 
The acute arsenic HQ is based upon an REL, the acute REL 
represents a health-protective level of exposure, with effects not 
anticipated below those levels, even for repeated exposures; 
however, the level of exposure that would cause health effects is 
not specifically known.  As the exposure concentration increases 
above the acute REL, the potential for effects increases.  Based 
upon an acute HQ value of 2 for arsenic emissions based on the 
REL, and given the protective nature of the REL (as described 
previously in this preamble, in section III.C.3.c) and without any 
additional acute health benchmarks to apply to further characterize 
the potential for severe or reversible effects it is reasonable to 
assume that acute health risks from arsenic for this source category 
would be low. 
 
87 Fed. Reg. at 1643/2-3.  Asarco supports this assumption. 
 
The only health benchmark available for acute toxicity of arsenic is in the form of the 
REL, which is based on the most sensitive, relevant adverse health effect reported in the peer-
reviewed medical and toxicological literature.  The REL of 0.2 µg/m3, which is relied upon in the 
proposed rulemaking, is ascribed to arsenic by the California Office of Environmental Health 
and Hazard Assessment (“OEHHA”).  It is based on peer-reviewed studies that tracked the fetal 
weight of mice exposed to increasing concentrations of arsenic and, therefore, represents the 
most sensitive adverse health effect reported by any of the studies reviewed by OEHHA.  The 
absence, conversely, of AEGLs and ERPG levels for arsenic is adequately offset by the 
rulemaking’s reliance on the REL and ultra-conservative exposure assumptions employed by 
EPA in its acute screening assessment.  87 Fed. Reg. at 1631/2-3.

1.0 
INTRODUCTION 
Consent Decree No. CV-15-02206-PHX-DLR, entered by ASARCO LLC (Asarco) and 
the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA), provides that Asarco shall conduct 
fugitive emissions studies of the Hayden Operations Copper Smelter in Hayden, 
Arizona. The Consent Decree (CD) requires an initial study to commence within 6 
months of completion of the Converter Retrofit Project, which must be completed by 
December 1, 2018. A follow-up study is also required after 5 years following conclusion 
of the initial study (Study). Part of the Study requirement is preparation and submittal of 
a draft protocol for EPA review by June 30, 2016. That requirement was satisfied. This 
document revision reflects EPA comments on the draft protocol received December 5, 
2016 with this revision due February 6, 2017. This submittal is intended to fulfill that 
requirement. 
The proposed Study design follows the previous 1994-1995 six-month fugitive emissions 
study as referenced in the CD. The focus of the 1994-1995 study was to characterize 
fugitive sulfur dioxide (SO2) emissions, fugitive particulate matter (PM) emissions, and 
selected fugitive PM metal Hazardous Air Pollutants (HAPs) emitted from the flash 
furnace, converter aisle, and anode furnaces building structures. The CD-required Study 
will use similar methodologies as in the previous study updated to address facility 
modifications, additional technical requirements, and to take advantage of new 
technologies. Objectives of the Study are to: 
• 
Develop post-retrofit fugitive SO2, PM, and PM metal HAPs emission factors 
• 
Determine potential surrogate parametric correlations between pollutants   
• 
Establish post-retrofit baseline fugitive emission levels for future comparison 
• 
Assist facility operations in control and management of fugitive emissions 
The Study is designed to quantify fugitive emissions of SO2 and PM. The parameters of 
interest include: 
• 
Sulfur dioxide (SO2) 
• 
Mass flux determined using exit velocity (Ve) and exit temperature (Te) 
• 
Total suspended particulate matter (PMTSP) 
• 
Inhalable PM (PM10) 
• 
Respirable PM (PM2.5) 
• 
PM metal HAPs (PMmetals) for arsenic (As), antimony (Sb), cadmium (Cd), 
chromium (Cr), lead (Pb), manganese (Mn), nickel (Ni), selenium (Se), cobalt 
(Co), beryllium (Be), and mercury (Hg) 
• 
Prototype open path optical density (OPD) 
Characterization of fugitive emissions will be for all primary copper smelting processing 
steps from the copper concentrate dryers through the anode casting department, 
inclusive of those end points, and the entire building(s) housing the smelting flash 
furnace, converter department, and anode furnaces. It will include characterization of 
process start-ups, shutdowns, and malfunctions that happen to occur during the Study. 

Ms. Amy Veek 
Fugitive Emissions Study Protocol

Project Organization 
Asarco Hayden Operations personnel have the responsibility to ensure the Study 
satisfies the CD requirements. This includes selecting a qualified contractor as required 
by the CD. They have selected SLR International Corporation to prepare the Study 
protocol and conduct the Study (www.SLRConsulting.com). 
SLR is a privately held, global consulting and engineering company with 1200 
employees in 76 offices worldwide committed to providing environmental and 
engineering support to industry and government with a strong focus on providing expert 
air quality services. There are over 80 air quality engineers and scientists with a range of 
expertise across all air quality sub-discipline elements including: 
• 
Emissions Monitoring and Testing 
• 
Emissions Control Engineering 
• 
Ambient Air Quality Monitoring 
• 
Air Dispersion Modeling 
• 
Permitting and Compliance 
Study coordination and management will be provided from the SLR Fort Collins, 
Colorado Air Resources Laboratory with local support provided by a technician(s). The 
20-member Fort Collins lab is equipped with electronics and fabrication facility, titration 
and gravimetric laboratory, and a full complement of calibration and source testing 
equipment, tools, National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST)-traceable 
calibration standards and measurement support systems. 
SLR’s Study team has decades of experience working together on complex air 
emissions and impact studies at industrial facilities throughout the US. This includes 
operational support on the now decommissioned Phelps Dodge Hidalgo Smelter in New 
Mexico and the Freeport-McMoRan Miami Smelter in Arizona including their fugitive 
emissions study efforts similar to the CD-required Study. Figure 1-1 depicts the Study 
organization.
