2.0 
FACILITY PROCESS DESCRIPTION 
Figure 2-1 depicts a simplified flow diagram of the Hayden Operations primary copper 
(Cu) smelting process configuration after completion of the Converter Retrofit Project. It 
shows the potential sources of fugitive emissions.  
Figure 2-1 Asarco Hayden Operations Smelter 
Simplified Primary Smelting Process 
Post-Converter Aisle Retrofit Project 
 
 
The copper smelting process begins with furnace feed, a mixture of copper 
concentrate (~85%), silica flux (~10%) and recycled smelting byproducts (~5%), dried 
to < 1% moisture content in one of two concentrate dryers.  The dried feed is 
recovered in a product baghouse and subsequently introduced into the INCO Oxygen 
Flash Furnace along with 95% pure oxygen.  Combustible components of the feed 
(mainly sulfur) “flash” in the high temperature, high oxygen furnace atmosphere.  This 
exothermic reaction results in the feed mixture becoming a molten bath in the furnace 
that separates into a heavy, copper-containing matte layer and a lighter, ferro-silicate 
slag layer. The slag is “skimmed” from the top of the molten bath into a pot for 
disposal.  Copper matte (~50-60% Cu) is “tapped” from the bottom of the furnace 
through ventilated launders into 300 ft3 ladles (post retrofit capacity) for transfer into 
one of three new Peirce-Smith Converters. 
Copper matte is then converted to blister copper (~96-98% Cu) by injecting air and/or 
oxygen enriched air into the molten matte through tuyeres that are positioned below the 
surface of the molten bath when the converter is rotated to the “blowing” position. Silica 
flux is added to the bath to combine with iron and oxygen to produce slag, and the sulfur 

Ms. Amy Veek 
Fugitive Emissions Study Protocol 
 
January 2017 
 
9 
 
in the matte combusts to form SO2.  Cold materials consisting of scrap copper and 
reverts (ladle shells and converter aisle cleanup of spilled material) are added as 
appropriate to control converter temperature.  Slag produced by the converting process 
is returned to the flash furnace for additional copper recovery, and the SO2 created 
reports to the acid plant to become sulfuric acid.  The blister copper product is 
transferred by ladle to the anode furnaces. 
Blister copper from the converters is further refined in one of three anode furnaces by 
injecting air to oxidize any remaining sulfur and using natural gas to remove any excess 
oxygen. A natural gas fired burner in each vessel maintains proper temperature during 
refining and anode (99.5% Cu) casting. Refined anodes are shipped to the Asarco 
Copper Refinery in Amarillo, Texas. 
The Hayden Operations smelter is equipped with integrated air emissions control 
systems that are designed to capture SO2 and PM emissions at every step in the 
process. Dryer product baghouses and matte tapping-slag skimming ventilation gases 
are directed to a new vent gas baghouse for PM control. Flash furnace process gases 
are routed to the gas cleaning plant for PM removal and then on to the acid plant for SO2 
control. Converter off gases from blowing are collected in the converter primary 
ventilation system and also routed to the gas cleaning plant for PM removal and then on 
to the acid plant for SO2 control and production of sulfuric acid.  Emissions from 
converter secondary operations (transfer of matte and skimming of slag) are captured by 
the converter secondary hood ventilation system and routed to the secondary hood 
baghouse for PM control before exiting the annulus of the 1000’ stack. 
2.1 
Facility Modifications 
The facility will undergo major modifications over the next several years that will 
significantly reduce air emissions. A summary of changes relevant to potential fugitive 
emissions include: 
• 
The five existing converters will be replaced with three larger converters, making 
possible an operating schedule limited to a single converter blowing at any one 
time. This will better match the off gas volume from the converters to the gas 
processing capacity of the acid plant. 
• 
Better matching gas volumes from the converters with processing capacity of the 
acid plant, in combination with new, improved primary and secondary hoods, will 
reduce process gas spillage from the primary to secondary hood system during 
blowing operations. 
• 
A new tertiary ventilation system will capture other converter aisle emissions 
(e.g., ladle transfer). This modification also includes closing the roof monitors 
over the converter aisle, closing openings on the converter exterior wall, and 
additional sheeting to further isolate the converter aisle building from the flash 
furnace building. 
• 
Improvements will be made to the matte tapping and slag skimming ventilation 
system which will be routed to a new vent gas baghouse. This change will reduce 
fugitive emissions SO2 and particulate matter. 

Ms. Amy Veek 
Fugitive Emissions Study Protocol 
 
January 2017 
 
10 
 
2.2 
Process Operations 
Fundamental to the Study will be the ability to relate study measurements to facility 
operations and processes to determine various cause and effect relationships to 
observed concentrations at specific fugitive emission points. Pertinent facility process 
monitoring and emissions control operations are continuously monitored and recorded 
and will be available during the Study. This information includes but is not limited to: 
• 
Feed rates and natural gas firing rates to the concentrate dryers 
• 
Feed rate and oxygen rate to the flash furnace 
• 
Converter blast air and oxygen flow rates 
• 
Anode furnace air and natural gas rates 
• 
Ventilation system gas flow rates 
• 
Onsite dispersion meteorology data 
Also recorded and available to the Study are non-continuous/discrete indicators such as: 
• 
Ventilation systems damper positions 
• 
Batch process indicators (matte tapping, slag skimming, ladle transfers, etc.) 
• 
Integrated production totals at each stage 
 
 

Ms. Amy Veek 
Fugitive Emissions Study Protocol

3.0 
STUDY ISSUES AND STRATEGY 
Fugitive (Latin origin fugitīvus, meaning “fleeing away”) air emissions from a complex 
industrial process can potentially affect air quality at nearfield receptors downwind. This 
type of emission has escaped control and containment and therefore can potentially 
pose an acute and chronic exposure at nearby residences. This is the impetus for the 
Study. As discussed previously, the Hayden smelter facility will undergo significant 
modification in the next several years that will reduce all air emissions including these 
potential fugitive emissions. The purpose of the Study will be to demonstrate the 
reduction. 
3.1 
1994-1995 Study Results 
The CD requires Asarco conduct a 12-month fugitive emissions study of the Hayden 
primary smelter complex using similar or equivalent methodology as used in the 
previously conducted 1994-1995 study. That 6-month study attempted to characterize 
total fugitive air emissions of SO2, PM, and selected PM metals from the flash furnace, 
converter aisle, and anode furnaces. Results showed maximum average fugitive SO2 
emissions were from the converter aisle roof monitors with the anode furnaces showing 
the least amount of fugitive SO2. Conversely results also showed the anode furnaces 
area to have the highest average fugitive PM emissions.  
Table 3-1 provides a summary of relevant fugitive emission results in pounds per hour 
(lbs/hour) from the 1994-1995 fugitive emissions study. SO2 monitoring was for the 6-
month period September to February and the PM measurement were 5 months running 
October 1994 through February 1995. 
Table 3-1 Asarco Hayden Operations Smelter

The anode furnaces, and to a lesser extent the flash furnace, appeared to be the major 
sources of PM on a total mass emissions basis during the study. The higher anode PM 
was likely due to carbon soot from the reduction cycle and condensable metal fine 
particulate off the furnaces as suggested by the higher measured Pb, As, and Se 
concentrations. This study was conducted before the anode furnace ventilation and 
baghouse system was installed in 2012. The relatively high flash furnace PM was 
expected to be associated with material handling and transfer at the concentrate dryers 
and furnace. This particulate loading is likely of relatively larger aerodynamic diameter 
with higher densities and therefore would not travel far from the smelter and fallout due 
to nearfield gravitational settling. Review of the seasonal distribution of maximum 

Ms. Amy Veek 
Fugitive Emissions Study Protocol 
 
January 2017 
 
12 
 
measured fugitive emissions rates showed highest average fugitive emissions rates 
were measured during winter months and westerly winds. 
The 1994-1995 study included assessment of the nearfield dispersion meteorology to 
determine potential location(s) of maximum air quality impact from smelter fugitive 
emissions. This was to ensure that ongoing ambient monitoring was sufficient to 
characterize local source-receptor relationships. As a part of the CD, Asarco is required 
to maintain the relevant nearby dispersion meteorology and ambient monitoring stations 
that will support Study data analysis. 
3.2 
Study Design Issues 
A comprehensive and successful study design to accurately characterize fugitive 
emissions from an operational facility must solve significant technical and logistical 
issues. These issues span the range from selecting proper equipment that can withstand 
the harsh exposures found inside a primary copper smelter to ensuring safe access to 
Study measurement locations by personnel servicing that equipment. These issues 
reflect the challenges identifying relevant fugitive emission sources and continuously 
capturing representative data to quantify those emissions specific to the parameters 
designated in the CD. The following is a summary list of Study design issues: 
Technical Issues 
• 
Identifying all relevant SO2 and PM fugitive emission points for monitoring 
• 
Accurately characterizing total mass flux through each fugitive emission exit 
• 
Developing SO2 and PM surrogate parameter correlations for extrapolation 
• 
Determining selected particulate metals concentrations of fugitive PM emissions 
• 
Demonstrating an effective means to continuously measure optical density 
• 
Ensuring all Study measurements are traceable back to authoritative standards 
Logistical Issues  
• 
Gaining safe access to all relevant fugitive emission points for instrumenting 
• 
Installing utilities, instrument shelters, support structures for sampling systems 
• 
Selecting equipment and support systems that can withstand harsh exposure 
• 
Determining placement, plumbing runs, and installing each sampling array 
• 
Servicing sampling array network and instrumentation on a routine basis  
• 
Accessing data collection systems remotely in near-realtime 
3.3 
Study Plan Strategy 
The Study plan strategy includes multiple phases that help to address these technical 
and logistical issues and lead to a final Study design. Details of the monitoring phases of 
this strategy are presented in subsequent sections of this protocol. Phases of the Study 
include: 
• 
Facility Reconnaissance – This phase was performed April 14-15, 2016. It was 
a facility walkthrough, access assessment, process review, data evaluation, and 
preliminary siting and study design meeting. It addressed equipment siting 
access 
needs, 
facility 
safety, 
logistical 
requirements, 
planned 
facility 

Ms. Amy Veek 
Fugitive Emissions Study Protocol 
 
January 2017 
 
13 
 
modifications, and review of previous study results, lessons learned, and 
supporting ambient monitoring program design. 
  
• 
Siting Confirmation Survey – This will be performed after the Converter Retrofit 
Project is complete but before commencing the one-year Study. It will include a 
minimum 5-day survey of the smelter complex using continuous recording 
Forward Looking Infrared Thermal Imaging (FLIR) camera-technology. The 
purpose of this survey is to verify candidate sampling array locations and identify 
relevant transient fugitive emission paths for inclusion into each facility sampling 
array. Multiple day/night viewing locations will be used to gain a 360⁰ perspective 
of the entire facility. This effort will also include identifying any additional 
infrastructure preparation required for the Study. 
 
• 
Intensive Monitoring – This will be an initial 60 day period of the Study for 
determining potential parametric correlations that can be used to enhance 
understanding of the surrogate relationships between fugitive SO2, PM, and PM 
metals emissions and between PM monitoring methodologies. It will involve 
higher frequency of manual PMTSP, PM10, and PM2.5 sample collection and 
laboratory analysis. An expanded discussion of the technical strategy associated 
with the Intensive Monitoring period is detailed in sections 4.0 Monitoring System 
Description, 5.0 Monitoring System Configuration, and 6.0 Sampling System 
Operation. 
 
• 
Continuous Monitoring – This final phase will be the remaining 10 months of 
the Study and will be continuous monitoring for fugitive SO2 and PM emissions; 
and velocity and temperature measurements in support of mass flux 
determination. This portion of the Study will also include a demonstration project 
using continuous open path opacity monitoring methodology for optical density 
measurements. The open path opacity instrument siting will be aided by results 
from the Intensive Study.  
 
 
 

Ms. Amy Veek 
Fugitive Emissions Study Protocol 
 
January 2017

4.0 
MONITORING SYSTEM DESCRIPTION 
Fugitive air pollution emissions studies are generally unique from study to study and use 
a combination of source emissions monitoring methods, ambient measurements 
techniques, and site-specific strategies to accomplish study objectives. The Hayden 
Study will use similar or equivalent methodology as used in 1994-1995 study following 
EPA-established source measurement and ambient monitoring guidelines where 
applicable and good scientific judgement. These EPA guidance documents include: 
• 
Quality Assurance Handbook for Air Pollution Measurement Systems, Volume III 
Stationary Source Specific Methods, EPA-600/R-94/038c, September 1994 
 
• 
Code of Federal Regulations 40 CFR 60, Appendix A - Standards of 
Performance for New Stationary Sources, Reference Method 6C - Determination 
of Sulfur Dioxide Emissions from Stationary Sources (Instrument Analyzer 
Procedure) 
 
• 
40 CFR 60, Appendix B – Method 203 Determination of the Opacity of Emissions 
from Stationary Sources by Continuous Monitoring Systems (COMS) 
 
• 
40 CFR 60, Appendix B – Performance Specification 1 –Specifications and Test 
Procedures for Opacity Continuous Emission Monitoring Systems in Stationary 
Sources 
 
• 
Quality Assurance Handbook for Air Pollution Measurement Systems, Volume II - 
Ambient Air Quality Monitoring Program, EPA-454/B-13-003, May 2013 
 
• 
Code of Federal Regulations 40 CFR 53, Ambient Air Monitoring Reference and 
Equivalent Methods 
 
• 
Code of Federal Regulations 40 CFR 58 Appendix A – Quality Assurance 
Requirements for SLAMS, SPMs, and PSD Air Monitoring 
 
• 
Compendium of Methods for the Determination of Inorganic Compounds in 
Ambient Air, EPA/625/R-96/010a, June 1999. IO-3.3 - Determination of Metals in 
Particulate Matter Using X-Ray Fluorescence (XRF) Spectroscopy.  
These guidelines are intended to ensure data collection with known precision, accuracy, 
and bias while achieving the valid data capture (completeness) goals of the Study. 
Because the Study is a hybrid of both source and ambient monitoring, many 
conventional quality assurance requirements included in those guidelines simply do not 
apply. However, the Study design will ensure all measurements are traceable to the 
National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST) or other authoritative standard. 
4.1 
Parameterization 
The parameters of interest for the Study address the goal of characterizing fugitive air 
emissions from the Hayden Operations smelting process to develop appropriate 
emission factors and determine monitoring control effectiveness.  

Ms. Amy Veek 
Fugitive Emissions Study Protocol 
 
January 2017 
 
15 
 
These parameters include: 
• 
SO2 
• 
Particulate Matter as PMTSP, PM10, and PM2.5 
• 
PM metals for As, Sb, Cd, Cr, Pb, Mn, Ni, Se, Co, Be, and Hg 
• 
Mass flux as exit velocity (Ve) and exit temperature (Te) 
• 
Open path optical density (OPD) 
SO2 and PM are the primary fugitive emissions from the flash furnace, converter aisle, 
and anode furnaces. PM metals are associated with the PM emissions. Measurement of 
mass flux is fundamental to calculating total fugitive emissions in terms of lbs/hour as a 
function of measured concentrations and the amount of accompanying air flow exiting 
those vents and building openings. Continuous visible plume measurement is a 
challenge for fugitive sources of large size because conventional opacity monitors have 
limited range (<20 meters) across an optical path.  The converter aisle-anode building 
alone is over 190 meters (m) long. Identifying and instrumenting visible fugitive plume 
exits as candidate locations for demonstrating automated optical density measurement 
methodology will be a part of the Study. 
4.2 
Monitoring Systems 
Study measurement systems and support equipment that will be used to conduct the 
Study are described herein and presented in Table 4-1 and 4-2. They include both 
continuous monitoring instruments and sensors, and manual sample collection and 
associated analytical methods. Model numbers and quantities listed are simply 
estimates subject to change based on final Study design and commercially available 
technology. 
SO2 
Continuous measurement of fugitive SO2 emissions will be made using stack gas 
monitoring-type instruments generally following EPA reference method 6C (RM6C) 
where applicable. These higher concentration source monitors will be used for emission 
points in the sampling array including roof monitors above the flash furnace and 
converter aisle building that includes the anode furnaces. Separately, an ambient 
monitor will be used to measure expected lower concentration emissions in near-
ambient air adjacent to the anode furnaces roof monitors for comparison to PM 
emissions as part of the correlation study. All SO2 monitors will be located in 
environmentally-controlled shelters at ground level. Sampling lines will be plumbed into 
each shelter. The source SO2 monitors will be EPA-accepted, field proven, continuous 
emission monitors (CEM). The ambient monitor will be an EPA-certified equivalent 
method instrument. 
RM6C provides for determination of SO2 concentrations by continuously extracting gas 
samples and directing a portion to an SO2 analyzer to measure the concentration (parts 
per million by volume). The SO2 measurement systems will be altered from RM6C as 
they will be equipped with multiple probe, filter, and flow assemblies to allow for 
collection and averaging of concentrations simultaneously at multiple locations. Sample 
gas will be drawn from each process-specific sampling array point at a constant flow rate 
and delivered equally to a sample manifold. Residence time will be kept to less than 10 

Ms. Amy Veek 
Fugitive Emissions Study Protocol 
 
January 2017 
 
16 
 
seconds at 10 liters per minute through a ½-inch Teflon sample line. The mixed sample 
gas in the manifold will be directed to a single instrument for analysis. This configuration 
will provide the average measured concentration of the fugitive emission array. Each 
sampling array will also be equipped with a set of data logger activated solenoid valves 
at the termination of each sampling line prior to entry into the sample manifold. This will 
allow isolation and delivery of sample gas from a single exit or subset of sampling points 
in that array into that station SO2 analyzer. This will provide option for conducting 
detailed evaluation of the relative mass loading of SO2 from each point in each sampling 
array. 
The SO2 sampling instrumentation will also include interconnected, portable SO2 
monitors that will be used to characterize technician-accessible, potential transient 
fugitive emission openings in the facility such as doorways and wall openings. These 
battery powered instruments provide continuous data collection and include self-
contained data acquisition and digital communication capability. Although they are not 
EPA-certified equivalent monitors, they will be calibrated to an authoritative standard 
traceable to NIST. Data collected with these systems will be used to scale these 
potential transient fugitive emissions. 
PM 
Unlike SO2 monitoring, which is a relatively stable and mobile gas, accurate 
measurement of PM is problematic primarily because the aerodynamic nature of the 
pollutant requires the sampler inlet be immediately located nearby the collection filter 
media or instrument detection system. Therefore, each sampling system must be 
located at the fugitive emission point. Measurements of particulate matter in the Study 
will be made using manual samplers in the sampling array in order to provide the 
needed filter sample media for elemental analysis of metals.  
A strategy to employ continuous PM monitors to support an SO2/PM correlation 
assessment will also be a part of the PM sampling strategy. This will also include 
collocating manual samplers with the continuous PM monitors located on the external 
instrument platform outside the anode furnace roof monitors (see Figure 5-1) or on the 
flash furnace. 
The manual PM samplers will be portable and battery powered Minivols equipped with 
47mm filters and particle size-select inlets with cut points at 10 microns (µ) and 2.5µ 
aerodynamic diameter. These low flow samplers, jointly developed by EPA are not an 
EPA-certified reference or equivalent method. However, sampling results have been 
documented to closely approximate data from Federal Reference Method samplers. 
PMTSP/PM10/PM2.5 filter samples collected with these instruments will be integrated 24-
hour samples. 
Continuous PM monitors will be used to support the SO2/PM correlation assessment. 
They will be automated, portable, beta attenuation-based instruments equipped with 
particle size-select inlets with cut points at 10µ and 2.5µ. A 2.5µ instrument has been 
certified by EPA as Equivalent Method with 10µ instrument certification in process. 
These instruments do not require operation in an environmentally controlled shelter 
which makes them well suited to support the Study. 
 

Ms. Amy Veek 
Fugitive Emissions Study Protocol 
 
January 2017 
 
17 
 
 
PMmetals 
Particulate metals samples will be collected with the MiniVol samplers without a size 
select inlet onto 47mm Teflon filters for analytical analysis by a certified laboratory to 
determine As, Sb, Cd, Cr, Pb, Mn, Ni, Se, Co, Be, and Hg concentrations from PMTSP. 
The MiniVol PM sample without a size select inlet provides TSP capture which is the 
maximum particulate load captured by this sampling method. PMTSP samples will be 
analyzed gravimetrically (for total PM mass) and by x-ray fluorescence (XRF) 
spectroscopy for elemental analysis following EPA Inorganic Compendium Analytical 
Laboratory Method IO-3.3. 
Mass Flux 
Determining total air flow volume through each fugitive emission exit point monitored is 
fundamental to accurately calculating the emission rate at that sampling location. This 
requires knowing the area of the opening and the velocity out of it. This will be 
accomplished by instrumenting each opening in the sampling array with a temperature 
sensor and either an axial flow sensor or pitot tube depending on accessibility and 
degree of sensitivity required. Openings in the sampling array that can be equipped with 
structural support will be provided with an axial flow sensor composed of a helicoid 
carbon fiber thermoplastic 4 bladed propeller turning a tach generator. For fugitive 
emissions vents where structural support cannot be constructed, a Pitot tube-type 
sensor will be used. The temperature sensors will be simple base metal type-K 
thermocouples. 
Optical Density 
One of the challenges facing the Study is determining the feasibility of continuous 
monitoring of visible plumes from smelter fugitive emissions at or near their exits. Typical 
continuous opacity measurement systems are designed for in-stack applications and use 
a fixed length optical path that is generally less than 15 meters in length to measure total 
light extinction across a process vent or exhaust stack cross section. However, potential 
visible plumes from fugitive emissions from the smelter can originate from multiple 
locations at different heights that span much longer path lengths.  
Development of a strategy for conducting a demonstration of automated fugitive 
emissions opacity monitoring during the Study will rely on results of the Siting 
Confirmation Survey performed after the Converter Retrofit Project is completed. This 
survey will rely on the data collected from the FLIR camera that will help identify 
potential candidate locations of visible fugitive emissions where an open path opacity 
continuous monitoring system could be deployed. The objectives of this demonstration 
would be to determine if this technology is viable and would benefit smelter operations 
and fugitive emissions control. Multiple candidate locations will be surveyed and 
equipped to receive the open path opacity instrumentation that will be tied into the 
nearest adjacent monitoring stations. It is anticipated that multiple deployments of this 
open path opacity monitoring system will occur during the duration of the Study. 
 
 

Ms. Amy Veek 
Fugitive Emissions Study Protocol 
 
January 2017 
 
18 
 
Support Systems 
The support equipment needed to conduct the Study includes systems to house 
instrumentation, extract fugitive emissions, and ensure collection of valid data traceable 
to NIST or other authoritative standard. In addition a full complement of equipment 
spares and expendable supplies for one year of operation will be supplied to ensure 
maximum valid data collection. 
The SO2 monitors will be rack mounted and installed in 8’x8’x8’ environmentally-
controlled shelters equipped with HVAC systems, line voltage conditioners, digital data 
loggers, station computer, cell or radio telemetry link, and gas calibration systems. The 
shelters will be corrosion resistant and include workspace and facility for field operations 
personnel equipment, maintenance, and spare supplies. SO2 sample gas from each 
location will be brought to a central sample manifold and mixed equally and transported 
to the analyzer. Each sample line will be equipped with a replaceable filter inlet to protect 
the pumps, solenoid valves and individual flow controllers. The inlet lines will converge 
into a single manifold with a sample pump and series of valves to control instrument 
calibrations. Each shelter will be supplied with multi-stage gas regulators and EPA-
traceable SO2 calibration gas cylinders and zero air supply. The data collection platform 
will include a station-dedicated digital data logger, data communication link, and a 
station computer system.  
The manual PM samplers will each be supplied with battery charging systems and spare 
batteries. Included will be sample filter cassettes and sample storage containers. 
Sampler and probe hangers will be installed at all locations in the sampling array. 
The open path opacity monitoring systems will be equipped if needed with optional 
fiberglass weather covers for the transmitter and receiver to allow for both inside and 
potential outdoor deployment. 
The thermal imagining system that will be used during the Siting Confirmation Survey to 
identify potential relevant transient fugitive emission paths to be added to the sampling 
arrays will be a FLIR camera. This survey will be performed over multiple days after the 
Converter Retrofit Project is complete. The camera type that will be used is for 
automation and process control and can provide instantaneous and time lapsed images 
of thermal discontinuities typical of smelter fugitive emissions signatures.  
Site Preparation 
Study design will require substantial infrastructure development to provide routine 
access to all significant potential fugitive emission points in the smelter complex. This 
includes catwalk construction below the roof monitors in the anode-converter building, 
utility development at locations requiring electric power for continuous monitors, outside 
roof walkway expansion for placement of instrumentation, and various access changes 
to accommodate sensors and sampling line runs. These preparations will be in advance 
of the Study and where possible integrated into the planned smelter modifications. 
Additional site preparations may be required to accommodate modifications/additions to 
the sampling array and instrumentation once the Study is underway.

5.0 
MONITORING SYSTEM CONFIGURATION 
The Study sampling array will be supported by three instrument shelters; one at the flash 
furnace, one at the south end of the converter aisle, and one at the anode furnaces area. 
Each instrument shelter will be centrally located to the process-specific sampling array. 
Additionally, a fourth data collection platform will be installed on an outside platform 
above and adjacent to the anode furnaces roof monitors during the intensive data 
collection portion of the Study. Also as a part of the optical density demonstration 
component of the Study, an open-path opacity monitor will be self-contained with its own 
weather cover and data logger and telemetry for placement either inside or outside the 
smelter at select locations of highest potential fugitive particulate emissions detected by 
the sampling array.  
Based on review of preliminary construction plans, 19 roof monitor and building exit 
opening sampling points have been identified for the Study sampling array with an 
additional two to four sampling locations anticipated to be added to the flash furnace 
area. These additional flash furnace sampling locations will be below roof height at open 
vent areas above the furnace matte tapping and slag skimming process. Specific 
locations will be identified during the Siting Confirmation Survey phase of Study 
preparation as previously discussed. Figure 5-1 provides an illustration of the potential 
fugitive emissions source geometry and roof monitor and exits sampling array. 
Figure 5-1 Asarco Hayden Operations Smelter 
Fugitive Emissions Study 
Roof Monitor and Exits Sampling Array  
 
 
The SO2 sampling array configuration is designed to provide total potential SO2 fugitive 
emissions from each major copper smelter process source. The flash furnace process-
specific sampling array SO2 source instrument will measure total fugitive SO2 emissions 
from the furnace roof monitors which also vent the concentrate dryers. The flash furnace 
station will be equipped with a second SO2 source instrument to measure total SO2 
fugitive emissions from other non-roof monitor emission exits. The anode furnaces 
station roof monitor array will capture potential fugitive emissions from the anode 
furnaces and any potentially uncaptured converter aisle fugitives from that side of the 

Ms. Amy Veek 
 
 
Fugitive Emissions Study Protocol 
 
January 2017 
 
22 
 
building including any emissions escaping through the open north end building exit. The 
south end of the converter aisle will be equipped with a separate station for 
measurement of potential fugitive emissions through the single roof monitor and open 
south end building exit.  
PM monitoring will essentially use the same process-specific sampling array locations as 
for SO2 with varying configurations based on accessibility, SO2 observations, and data 
trends. PM monitoring will primarily be manual 24-hour integrated samples collected 
every 3 days during the 60 day Intensive Monitoring phase and every 6 days thereafter. 
The monitoring array will also be equipped with an external building SO2/PM instrument 
platform immediately east of the anode furnace area roof monitors near their exit height. 
This platform will be used to assist in determining potential surrogate relationship 
between fugitive SO2 emissions and PM emissions. Additionally, prevailing westerly 
daytime winds will increase the frequency of potential plume impingement at that 
platform location. The platform will be equipped with continuous PM monitors that do not 
require housing in an environmentally controlled shelter. The associated SO2 sample line 
run will be to an ambient SO2 analyzer in the anode furnaces station shelter immediately 
below the platform. 
The demonstration open path optical density monitoring system will be an open path 
continuous opacity monitor set up to transect  highest particulate concentration fugitive 
plume exits as determined during the Intensive Monitoring and Continuous Monitoring 
phases of the Study. It is anticipated that a single system will be deployed at multiple 
locations during the Study. The purpose of this demonstration is to determine the 
practicality of employing this technology to minimize smelter fugitive emissions by 
detecting process-related events and alerting operators in near-realtime. Because this 
technology will require infrastructure support, several candidate locations will be 
identified during the Siting Confirmation Survey so that site preparations can be 
completed before the start of the Study. Data from the open path continuous opacity 
monitoring systems will be continuously available in near-realtime to operations and 
archived for further analysis.  
 
 

Ms. Amy Veek

6.0 
SAMPLING SYSTEM OPERATION 
The Study design is a combination of fully automated continuous measurement systems 
and manual PM samplers. Once the sampling array and support systems are installed, 
tested, and become fully operational, Study network operations and maintenance will be 
supported by an onsite instrument technician(s) with ongoing and on-call assistance 
from project field operations, data management, and project management personnel. 
During the Intensive Monitoring portion of the Study, manual 24-hour integrated PM 
samples will be collected at three day intervals and then switched to an ever 6-day 
frequency during the Continuous Monitoring period following the EPA 6-day sampling 
schedule. Collocated PM sampling will be included during both periods, bi-weekly during 
the Intensive Monitoring period and monthly thereafter. A sampling day 47mm sample 
filter field blank will accompany each exposed filter batch for analysis. All other 
instrumentation will be collecting continuous data as presented in Table 3-1. 
During the intensive and continuous monitoring phases of the Study, the SO2 monitoring 
effort will include periodically isolating the potential relative mass loading of SO2 from 
each sampling point in each sampling array. There will be a data logger controlled 
solenoid valve on each sample line into each array station manifold. Each station data 
logger will be capable of being programmed to close all sample inlets into the array 
manifold and then cycle through sampling each inlet independently for a pre-determined 
duration and frequency on a pre-programmed basis. These programs will be configured 
based on sampling results, process activities, visual observations, or meteorological 
conditions. 
The network O&M schedule is presented in Table 6-1. It shows the frequency of 
expected routine servicing and preventative maintenance for each measurement 
system. These systems will be exposed to a relatively harsh operating environment and 
therefore the frequencies may change over time. All monitor calibrations and 
replacements will be NIST-traceable.

7.0 
QUALITY ASSURANCE PLAN 
Quality assurance (QA) procedures that will be used in the Study will generally follow 
EPA guidance as previously referenced where applicable. The O&M schedule listed in 
Table 6-1 provides the quality control steps that will be implemented for the Study. The 
Study is intended to provide unbiased, representative quantification of total smelter 
fugitive emissions of SO2 and PM with known accuracy and precision. This QA plan 
describes the procedures, control limits, data acceptance criteria, and quality control. 
These elements include: 
Representativeness – The Study sampling array and continuous sampling strategy will 
be chosen to characterize total potential fugitive air emissions from the flash furnace 
building, converter aisle, and anode furnaces during routine operations as well as start-
up, shut down, and process malfunctions that happen to occur during the study 
timeframe. All Study sampling points within the smelter structure will be given a unique 
identifier to ensure that all data collected are paired to a specific location within the 
facility. 
Comparability – SO2 and PM data collected by the sampling network will be comparable 
on an inter-network basis and with expected conditions based on process activities and 
meteorological events. Appropriate collocated and field blank/replicate PM samples will 
provide additional comparability. 
Completeness - Study valid data recovery on a parameter-specific basis is in excess of 
monthly minimums of 80% for all air quality and mass flux parameters. Preventative 
maintenance and complement of spare parts will be maintained to ensure goals are met. 
Accuracy – Study data accuracy is maintained in compliance with EPA requirements 
through strict routine system assessment, preventive service and maintenance, and a 
comprehensive calibration program. All calibration equipment will be maintained certified 
traceable to NIST or other authoritative standard where applicable. 
The QA objectives for the Study are listed in Table 7-1 with calculations to follow.

7.1 
Calibrations 
Instrument and sensor calibrations are required to reduce possible systematic bias and 
ensure collection of data with known accuracy and precision.  The onsite technician(s) 
and project field engineers will perform calibrations of the SO2 monitors on at least a 
quarterly basis or when automatic daily zero and span checks indicate recalibration is 
needed. The PM samplers will be calibration checked by the onsite technician(s) daily 
both before and after each sample is collected. The continuous PM monitors will be 
calibration checked every two weeks by the onsite technician(s). The axial flow 
measurement sensors will be replaced with rebuilt and recalibrated sensors monthly. 
The sampling array temperature sensors will be calibration checked on a quarterly basis.  
Operation of the demonstration optical density monitor will not strictly follow 40 CFR Part 
60 Appendix B Performance Specifications 1 because the application and operating 
conditions will not allow that level of documented precision and accuracy. However, 
QA/QC guidance contained therein will be applied where practical including periodic 
upscale and zero calibration checks.  
All calibration records will be reviewed by the Project Manager and Data Manager who 
have knowledge of all Study procedures but no direct involvement with actual 
calibrations. Calibration checks are conducted if any one of the following criteria is met: 
• 
On the schedule presented in Table 6-1 
• 
After service activity or corrective action that may alter instrument response 
• 
SO2 analyzer span deviates by more than ±10 percent from designated value 
• 
SO2 analyzer or sensor is physically moved 
• 
There is indication of potential malfunction in any data collection system 
• 
Prior to removal of any instrument or sensor that is still operational 
• 
Immediately following installation of a replacement instrument or sensor 
All calibrations conform to QA guidance referenced in Chapter 1. Calibrations will be 
performed according to applicable equipment SOPs as developed to meet requirements 
of EPA regulatory guidelines for traceability and documentation. Table 7-2 provides a list 
of calibration equipment that will be used during the Study.  All test equipment used for 
calibrations is maintained and calibrated on a regular basis.  Records providing 
traceability to the NIST or other authoritative standards for all equipment used to adjust 
monitoring systems will be maintained for disclosure. 
TABLE 7-2 
Asarco Hayden Fugitive Emissions Study  
Calibration Equipment

Documentation of all site visits will be provided through several forms. A sampling array 
network log and checklists will be maintained at each monitoring station detailing 
inspection, PM sampling, system calibrations, and repair activities. Corrective Action 
forms will be used to track any operational issues that may arise. Records and 
measurements taken during calibrations will be recorded on forms specifically designed 
for each respective instrument. Calibrations will also be documented digitally and with 
hardcopies. The Project Manager and Project Field Manager will be responsible for 
verifying project forms are properly completed and filed. All relevant project forms will be 
included with quarterly data reports and archived with project files. 
The continuous SO2 analyzers (source and ambient instruments) will be multi-point 
calibrated at least quarterly by the Project Monitoring Manager or a project field 
engineer. Multi-point calibrations consist of challenging the instruments with known 
concentrations at approximately 25, 55, and 90 percent of full scale. Linearity over the 
sampling range will be checked and adjustments made as needed to bring analyzer 
response within project accuracy goals. 
The portable SO2 instruments will be calibration checked at the beginning and end of 
each deployment and at least bi-weekly. These calibration checks will include zero and a 
SO2 level near the upper range of expected measured fugitive concentrations (≤20 ppm). 
Automatic zero/span checks will be performed daily.  No automatic adjustments occur; 
however, the information is used to detect sudden malfunctions or changes in calibration 
that may warrant unscheduled maintenance. Span check concentrations will be set at 
approximately 90 percent of the instrument full-scale response. Calibration, zero, and 
span gas will be introduced as close to the sample inlet locations as possible ensuring 
that the calibration gas passes through all filters, pumps, and flow meters and most of 
each samples line. 
SO2 monitor shelter temperatures will be continuously monitored and maintained 
between 15 and 35 °C to satisfy EPA certification specifications. The targets for 
maintaining shelter temperature will be set between 20 and 30°C.  Each station shelter 
temperature sensor will be calibration checked quarterly using an NIST-traceable 
thermometer. 
Each manual PM sampler will be supplied with a recharged battery and calibration 
checked with a certified PM flow calibration system prior to and after each sample is 
collected. Samples will be collected over a 24-hour period, midnight-to-midnight, ±60 
minutes. Approximately, 10% of the samples collected will have a collocated sample for 
precision assessment of the methodology from sample pre-weight through laboratory 
analysis. Collocated PM samples should maintain an average percent difference within 
±15%. The certified analytical laboratory that doing the sample analysis will be supplied 
with approximately 5% field blanks. Collocated collected filter samples and field blanks 
will be provided blind to the laboratory. 
 
 

Ms. Amy Veek 
 
 
Fugitive Emissions Study Protocol

8.0 
DATA MANAGEMENT AND REPORTING 
All Study data will be accessible in near-realtime via telecommunication interface to the 
Study data loggers with the exception of the manual PM samplers. Each Study network 
monitoring array station will be equipped with a digital data logger, station computer, and 
radio telemetry link that will allow data to be automatically polled and remotely 
downloaded into an offsite central data management system and smelter facility control 
room computer. The data communication system will also be designed to allow control 
and diagnostic evaluation of each air quality analyzer. All continuous air quality monitors 
will record and store its own data as back-up to the data loggers in the event of failure. 
Zero, span, and calibration data will be automatically captured, stored, and downloaded 
daily. As a quality control step a record of current sensor/sampling locations (identifiers) 
will be carefully documented by the onsite technician on a daily sampling log form and 
maintained to accompany all sampling and data collection efforts. 
Each data logger will sample all continuous monitors in their process-specific sampling 
array every second and compute and store 5-minute and hourly averages. The polling 
computers will automatically interrogate the Study network data loggers every hour. 
Every business day the onsite technician(s) and a project data management analyst will 
review the data and assess network performance. In the event an unresolved anomaly 
or measurement system failure is detected, notification will be made to the Monitoring 
Manager and Project Manager and a mitigation plan will be formulated to address the 
issue. All daily assessment and mitigation plans will be documented and follow-up 
verified. 
Original Study digital data records will be appended into complete monthly files and 
retained for reference. Separate working files will be made from the original appended 
files for validation. The data validation process will be conducted monthly and will 
include assessment of zero/span/calibration results, internetwork comparisons or 
observed data, review of smelter process records, and dispersion meteorological 
conditions recorded during the period. Edits to the database will be made that will 
include appropriate corrections and deletions. These will be documented for reference. 
Manually collected PM sample filters will be collected on an every 6-day basis (every 3 
days during Intensive Study) following the EPA sampling schedule for comparison to 
nearby ambient collected data. Each unexposed 47mm Teflon filter batch will be 
submitted to the analytical laboratory for serial numbering and gravimetric pre-weight. 
Chain-of-Custody forms will be prepared upon receipt of each unexposed batch that will 
follow each sample day batch. During each PM sample day the onsite technician (s) will 
record on a digital Chain-of-Custody template each filter serial number, sample array ID 
number, and all relevant sampling information (e.g., date, elapse time, pre- and post-
calibration flow, observed filter condition, etc.) using a portable computer tablet that will 
be downloaded into a station computer, stored, and printed out for transfer with the 
exposed filters to the laboratory for gravimetric and XRF analysis approximately every 
two weeks. Analytical results will be assessed and entered into the project data base 
upon receipt. 
Approximately two weeks following the end of each month, a Study progress report will 
be generated for distribution that will communicate preliminary network valid data 
retrieval statistics, significant events, QA results, and data summaries. Process related 
information will be incorporated into these progress reports where relevant. 

Ms. Amy Veek 
 
 
Fugitive Emissions Study Protocol 
 
January 2017 
 
30 
 
Approximately 30 days following the intensive 60-day data collection portion of the 
Study, a data report will be prepared that will provide detailed discussion of observed 
data trends relative to process events, surrogate parametric relationships, and any 
recommendations of potential changes that should be made for the remainder of the 
Study. 
At the conclusion of the Study, a final report will be prepared and submitted that will 
provide a detailed summary of results. This will include discussion of observed fugitive 
emissions to process activities, development of facility-specific fugitive emission factors 
with back-up computations, discussion of feasibility and results of demonstration 
monitoring strategies, recommendations for ongoing monitoring, and summary of all data 
collected. All original and validated data files will be transferred with the final report. 
 
 

Ms. Amy Veek 
 
 
Fugitive Emissions Study Protocol 
 
January 2017

9.0 
HEALTH AND SAFETY 
Conducting the Study will be potentially dangerous to field personnel because of the 
exposure to heights, hazardous gases, high temperatures, and moving process 
equipment all inherent in an operational primary copper smelter. Preparation of safe 
access to all monitoring points in the sampling array will be completed during the 
converter retrofit project and other associated facility modifications prior to installation of 
the network. Other preparations needed for the Study will be determined based on the 
Siting Confirmation Survey. 
All sampling points will be equipped with safety railing and anchor points for fall 
protection. Monitoring will not be conducted at facility locations that are not safely 
accessible. 
An important part of Study preparation will also be development and implementation of a 
strict health and safety (H&S) plan. This project H&S plan will integrate Asarco facility 
H&S requirements with Study-specific elements. It will address: 
• 
Minimum training and certification requirements 
• 
Emergency contact list 
• 
Incident reporting guidelines 
• 
Contaminants of Concern 
• 
Project hazards identification and risk assessment 
• 
Job safety analysis 
• 
Minimum personal protection equipment 
 
These elements will become part of the field operations project operating procedures.

Discussion between EPA and Asarco Regarding Achievability of Subpart QQQ

From: Amy Veek

Subject: RE: Asarco Hayden Smelter Modeling Inputs 
 
Hi Tonisha, 
 
I hope everything is good with you at this time. Additionally, I appreciate you 
giving Asarco the opportunity to voice any concerns regarding potential SSM 
issues. Pursuant to our conference call on April 8 and in further response to your 
March 31 e-mail (below this e-mail) I’ve outlined some SSM items to consider: 
 
1. If Subpart QQQ were to be revised to require that continuous monitoring data 
be used to demonstrate compliance with the nonsulfuric acid PM numeric 
standard applicable to smelting furnace process off-gas and converter system 
process off-gas, then Asarco believes the numeric standard presently at § 
63.1444(b)(1) and (d)(5) would need to be revised in order to account for 
those off-gas emissions that occur during periods of startup and 
shutdown.  That said, Asarco believes that it would be impossible to generate 
continuous monitoring data that could be used to demonstrate compliance 
with the nonsulfuric acid PM numeric standard applicable to smelting furnace 
process off-gas and converter system process off-gas. 
 
2. If Subpart QQQ were to be revised to require that continuous monitoring data 
be used to demonstrate compliance with the opacity standard applicable to 
visible emissions exiting the roof monitors or roof exhaust fans on the building 
housing the copper converter department, then Asarco believes the opacity 
standard presently at § 63.1444(d)(4)(i) would need to be revised in order to 
account for those visible emissions that occur during periods of: 
 
a. Startup and shutdown; 
 
b. Periods of non-blowing (that is, when air or oxygen-enriched air is not 
introduced into any of the batch copper converter furnace molten baths 
through tuyeres that are submerged below the level of the molten bath); 
and 
 
c. Periods during blowing when one or more of the interference events listed 
in § 63.1450(c)(7)(iii)(A) through (F) is occurring: 
 
(A) Charging of copper matte, reverts, or other materials to a batch copper 
converter; 
 

(B) Skimming slag or other molten materials from a batch copper 
converter; 
 
(C) Pouring of blister copper or other molten materials from a batch 
copper converter; 
 
(D) Return of slag or other molten materials to the flash smelting furnace 
or slag cleaning vessel; 
 
(E) Roll-out or roll-in of the batch copper converter; 
 
(F) Smoke and fumes generated inside the converter building by 
operation of the smelting furnace, the slag cleaning vessel (if used), 
anode refining and casting processes that drift into the copper 
converter department. 
 
That said, it is presently unclear whether it would be possible to generate 
continuous monitoring data that could be used to demonstrate compliance 
with the opacity standard applicable to visible emissions exiting the roof 
monitors or roof exhaust fans on the building housing the copper converter 
department. 
 
3. If Subpart QQQ were to be revised to incorporate standards other than the 
numeric and opacity standards that are presently imposed in Subpart QQQ, 
then the question of whether such standards could be achieved or compliance 
with such standards could be empirically demonstrated would depend on the 
standard in question and whether data could feasibly be generated to 
demonstrate the compliance. 
 
Attached for your convenience is a simplified gas flow diagram for the Hayden 
smelter.

--- COMMENT ID: EPA-HQ-OAR-2020-0430-0138 AND DOCKET ID: EPA-HQ-OAR-2020-0430 ---
Via Regulations.gov 
 
April 26, 2022

EPA Docket Center

1200 Pennsylvania Ave., NW 
Washington, DC  20460 
Re: 
A2C Comments on the Proposal Entitled “National Emission Standards for 
Hazardous Air Pollutants: Primary Copper Smelting Residual Risk and 
Technology Review and Primary Copper Smelting Area Source Technology 
Review” – Docket ID No. EPA-HQ-OAR-2020-0430 
To Whom It May Concern: 
The Air Advocacy Coalition (“A2C”) appreciates the opportunity to submit the following 
comments on the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency’s (“EPA” or “Agency”) proposal 
entitled “National Emission Standards for Hazardous Air Pollutants: Primary Copper Smelting 
Residual Risk and Technology Review and Primary Copper Smelting Area Source Technology 
Review,” published at 87 Fed. Reg. 1616 (Jan. 11, 2022) (“Proposal”).  The A2C is submitting 
these comments within the extended deadline of April 26, 2022.  87 Fed. Reg. 10325 (Feb. 24, 
2022). 
The A2C is an ad-hoc coalition that includes the American Chemistry Council, the 
American Coke and Coal Chemicals Institute, the American Fuel & Petrochemical 
Manufacturers, the American Iron and Steel Institute, the American Petroleum Institute, and the 
U.S. Chamber of Commerce.  The purpose of the A2C is to advocate for sensible and effective 
federal emissions standards under the Clean Air Act (“CAA”).  Companies represented by the 
members of the A2C own or operate primary copper smelters that will be subject to the 
forthcoming final rule.  A2C members also will be affected by the legal, regulatory, and policy 
precedents established by the final rule. 
The A2C has two comments on the Proposal.  These comments reinforce and amplify 
similar comments submitted individually by A2C members. 
1. 
EPA has not provided adequate justification for its proposed finding that risks due 
to HAP emissions from this source category are not acceptable. 
Based on the results of its risk assessment, EPA proposes to find that “the risks for this 
source category under the current MACT provisions are unacceptable.”  87 Fed. Reg. at 1642.  
EPA explains that its proposed finding “is largely based on the estimated exceedances of the lead 

U.S. Environmental Protection Agency 
April 26, 2022 
Page 2 
 
 
NAAQS … along with the maximum acute HQ of 7 for arsenic, which indicate there are 
significant risks of noncancer health effects for people near the facility.”  Id.  EPA notes that 
“[a]lso contributing to this proposed determination, although to a lesser extent, are the inhalation 
cancer MIRs due to arsenic, with an estimated MIR of 80-in-1 million for actual emissions and 
90-in-1 million for allowable emissions, which are approaching the presumptive level of 
unacceptability of 100-in-1 million.”  Id. 
EPA’s proposed finding that risks are unacceptable is inadequately justified for several 
reasons.  First, the additional emissions control measures proposed to be adopted under CAA 
§ 112(d)(2), (3), or (6) should be factored into the risk assessment before drawing conclusions 
about risk acceptability.  The Agency predicts that “post-control analysis indicated that with the 
addition of controls proposed in this rulemaking, the cancer risks will be reduced from an 
estimated maximum individual excess cancer risk at Freeport from 80-in-1 million to 30-in-1 
million, and noncancer risks will also be reduced significantly, substantially reducing risk among 
highly exposed individuals.”  Id. at 1644.  Except for additional fugitive dust measures proposed 
to be needed to achieve an ample margin of safety (and which EPA predicts will achieve little or 
no actual emissions reductions because they already are mostly being implemented by the 
affected facilities), id., all of the proposed additional emissions control measures are separately 
justified under CAA § 112(d)(2), (3), or (6). 
This means that, as appropriate, these control measures will be finalized on their own 
merits (i.e., without regard to the outcome of EPA’s risk assessment) and necessarily will reduce 
risks associated with emissions from this source category.  As a result, these additional proposed 
control measures reasonably should be factored into EPA’s risk assessment before making 
conclusions about risk acceptability.  This is particularly true for proposed CAA §§ 112(d)(2) 
and (3) control measures, which are “MACT” emissions controls that are required to be 
considered in residual risk assessments.  See CAA § 112(f)(2)(A) (residual risk review must be 
conducted after promulgation of standards under CAA § 112(d)). 
Notably, EPA has routinely taken into account concurrently adopted technology-based 
standards in risk assessments in prior RTRs.  See, e.g., 80 Fed. Reg. 62390, 62399 (Oct. 15, 
2015) (EPA finding that risks from the primary aluminum production source category are 
acceptable based on a risk assessment that takes account of additional control measures adopted 
due to EPA’s concurrent technology review.).  EPA provides no explanation as to why the same 
approach should not be taken here. 
Second, the predicted lead NAAQS exceedance “is estimated to occur only in a small 
area near Freeport.”  Id. at 1642.  Based on actual emissions, EPA predicts that “a single census 
block (about five people) has the potential to be exposed to lead concentrations greater than the 
lead NAAQS.”  87 Fed. Reg. at 1638.  Moreover, lead emissions from the Freeport facility will 
be significantly reduced by existing emissions control obligations (consent decrees, state control 
measures) and by the technology-based emissions control measures proposed in this rule.  Thus, 
the predicted NAAQS exceedance would, in the first instance, be limited.  Indeed, the possibility 
of an exceedance will be effectively eliminated by the myriad additional emissions controls that 
will be implemented by the facility for reasons unrelated to the current risk assessment.  It is thus 

U.S. Environmental Protection Agency 
April 26, 2022 
Page 3 
 
 
unreasonable for EPA to propose that the predicted lead NAAQS exceedances are a primary 
factor in its proposed finding that risks are unacceptable. 
Third, EPA’s proposal to give significant weight to the HQ of 7 attributable to arsenic 
emissions is inconsistent with findings in prior RTRs that such risk levels are acceptable.  For 
example, in the RTR for the primary aluminum production source category, EPA concluded that 
an HQ of 10 attributable to arsenic emissions represented an acceptable level of risk.  80 Fed. 
Reg. at 62399.  EPA provides no explanation in the Proposal as to why an HQ of 7 is a primary 
factor in proposing that risks are unacceptable when it previously concluded that an even higher 
HQ for the same HAP was not actionable.  This produces an arbitrary difference in outcomes. 
Fourth, EPA’s proposal that the cancer risks attributable to arsenic emissions are a factor 
supporting its proposed finding that risks are unacceptable is inconsistent with findings in prior 
RTRs that such risk levels are acceptable.  Based on allowable emissions, EPA’s risk assessment 
predicts a maximum individual cancer risk attributable to arsenic emissions from this source 
category to be 90 in-a-million, with 29,000 people predicted to have risks of greater than 1 in-a-
million and a cancer incidence of 0.003.  87 Fed. Reg. at 1637.  In contrast, in the RTR for 
primary aluminum production, EPA concluded that cancer risks due to arsenic emissions were 
acceptable.  The risk assessment in that rule predicted a maximum individual cancer risk of 70 
in-a-million, with 881,000 people predicted to have risks greater than 1 in-a-million and a cancer 
incidence of 0.06.  80 Fed. Reg. at 62398-9. 
So, the EPA predicts that the copper source category will have a slightly higher 
maximum cancer risk (90 vs. 70 in-a-million) but will have a significantly lower population 
exposed at levels above 1 in-a-million (well over an order of magnitude lower) and a 
significantly lower cancer incidence (again, well over an order of magnitude lower).  On balance, 
it is clear that the cancer risks attributable to arsenic emissions were substantially greater in the 
aluminum rule than in the current Proposal.  The relatively greater risks were deemed acceptable 
in the aluminum rule, yet contribute here to a proposed finding of unacceptability.  These 
disparate results are not explained in the Proposal and cause an arbitrary difference in outcome. 
2. 
Use of a revenue-based cost metric is unwarranted. 
In its consideration of costs in assessing “beyond the floor” (“BTF”) emissions standards, 
EPA uses the well-established metric of dollars per ton of pollutant removed.  However the 
Agency also asserts that it “also considers other factors in assessing the cost of the emission 
reduction as part of our BTF analysis, including, but not limited to, total capital costs, annual 
costs and costs compared to total revenues (e.g., costs to revenue ratios).”  87 Fed. Reg. at 1635.  
EPA estimates that the “annualized costs [of compliance with BTF standards] for Freeport are 
about 0.016 percent of the annual revenue of the facility’s ultimate parent company in 2019” and 
“[f]or the Asarco facility, the estimated annualized costs of the proposed rule (i.e., $95,000 in 
testing costs) were less than 0.01 percent of 2019 revenues for the facility’s ultimate parent 
company.”  Id. at 1651.  EPA concludes that these costs “are not economically significant.”  Id. 
at 1635. 

U.S. Environmental Protection Agency 
April 26, 2022 
Page 4 
 
 
EPA’s consideration of a revenue-based cost metric is unwarranted.  To begin, EPA cites 
NRDC v. EPA, 749 F. 3d 1055, 1060 (D.C. Cir. 2013) for the proposition that “Section 112 does 
not command EPA to use a particular form of cost analysis.”  EPA misreads the court’s opinion, 
which stated: “According to EPA, Section 112 does not command EPA to use a particular form 
of cost analysis.”  Id. at 1060-1 (emphasis added).  The complete quote makes it clear that the 
court was describing EPA’s argument and not embracing that argument as its own.  Contrary to 
EPA’s assertion in the Proposal, the court did not hold as a matter of law that “cost effectiveness 
is but one factor [that EPA may consider] to assess cost” in setting BTF standards.  87 Fed. Reg. 
at 1635. 
Indeed, this part of the NRDC opinion addressed the petitioners’ argument that “EPA 
misinterpreted the statute to allow it to consider cost-effectiveness when setting beyond-the-floor 
standards.”  NRDC at 1060.  The court concluded that EPA’s use of cost effectiveness was 
“reasonable” and, therefore, “reject[ed] petitioners’ argument that EPA was required to exclude 
consideration of cost-effectiveness.”  Id. at 1061.  In other words, the court expressly upheld 
EPA’s use of the cost effectiveness metric.  The court does not opine on whether or how EPA 
may consider other possible cost metrics.  Thus, NRDC does not support EPA’s contention that it 
may use a revenue-based cost metric here. 
More generally, the suggested revenue-based cost metric is flawed because it would 
produce arbitrary and inconsistent results.  For example, revenue is only one part of the 
economic picture for companies that own or operate affected sources.  A company with 
significant revenue might be unprofitable, or even non-viable financially, if its expenditures 
equal or exceed its revenue.  In contrast, a company with relatively small revenue might be 
highly profitable if it has little overhead.  Thus, revenue alone says nothing about the ability of a 
company to accommodate the costs of emissions controls. 
Similarly, companies often comprise multiple different business units that derive revenue 
in different ways.  Hypothetically, a company operating an affected copper smelter might also 
have chemical manufacturing, paper making, and financial services operations.  It makes no 
sense to assess the acceptability of a beyond-the-floor standard for copper smelting based on the 
revenue that has nothing to do with copper smelting.  Acceptability would depend more on 
happenstance (i.e., the make up of companies owning affected sources) rather than the financial 
viability of the affected operations. 
Additionally, beyond-the-floor determinations are conducted on a standard-by-standard 
basis.  That creates the possibility that individual beyond-the-floor determinations might be 
deemed acceptable as compared with company revenue when, in the aggregate, the collection of 
beyond-the-floor standards would not be supportable. 
Lastly, for all but the biggest emission control expenditures, there will be a significant 
mismatch between the cost of emissions controls for a given beyond-the-floor standard and the 
overall revenues of companies with affected facilities.  That means that the cost of beyond-the-
floor measures almost always will appear small and favorable as compared to revenues.  Such 

U.S. Environmental Protection Agency 
April 26, 2022 
Page 5 
 
 
comparisons provide little insight because they dilute the value of the cost assessment to the 
point that cost would rarely be a reason to forego emissions control alternatives. 
*  *  *  *  *  *  *  *  *  * 
Thank you again for the opportunity to submit these comments.  Please do not hesitate to 
contact me if you have questions or need more information. 
Sincerely, 
/s/ Keith Petka 
Keith Petka 
Senior Policy Manager 
American Petroleum Institute 
Chair – Air Advocacy Coalition

Administrator Regan, 
 
On behalf of Kennecott Utah Copper LLC (“KUC”), I am writing to request a 30-day 
extension of the deadline to comment on the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency’s 
(“EPA’s) proposed rule “National Emission Standards for Hazardous Air Pollutants: Primary 
Copper Smelting Residual Risk and Technology Review and Primary Copper Smelting Area 
Source Technology Review”.1  Comments on the proposed rule, which would modify 40 
CFR part 63, subparts QQQ and EEEEEE, are currently due on February 25, 2020. 
KUC appreciates the EPA’s work to-date to review and revise subparts QQQ and EEEEEE 
as contemplated by the Clean Air Act. KUC is supportive of the EPA’s efforts and 
anticipates that the travel restrictions caused by the ongoing Covid-19 pandemic has only 
heightened the difficult task of completing the residual risk and technology review for these 
source categories. As one of just three copper smelters located in the United States – and 
the only source subject to subpart EEEEEE – a comprehensive and complete 
understanding of KUC’s operations and constraints is fundamental to the technology 
reviews that EPA has proposed. Indeed, KUC’s smelter implements flash smelting and flash 
converting technology so it’s Utah-based facility operates with lower emissions, as such the 
operations at the KUC smelter are not necessarily mirrored by the operations at the other 
smelters.  
KUC is reviewing the proposed rules and the technical information that EPA has prepared 
and placed in the rulemaking docket and intends to provide EPA with comments on the 
 
1 See Docket ID No. EPA-HQ-OAR-2020-0430; 87 Fed. Reg. 1616, 1616/1 (Jan. 11, 2022).   

 
 
Page 2 of 2 
 
proposed revisions to subparts QQQ and EEEEEE. However, given the technical nature of 
the review, the extensive record that EPA has developed to-date, and limitations on staff 
availability given existing quarterly and annual reporting requirements as well as the 
planned full replacement of our Smelter continuous emissions monitoring systems in 
February 2022, KUC needs additional time to prepare comments on the proposed rules. For 
the reasons outlined in this letter, KUC requests a 30-day extension to the existing public 
comment period. 
Thank you for considering this request. Should you or EPA staff have any questions about 
this request, please contact Jenny Esker, KUC Principal Advisor, Air Quality, at (801) 569-
6494, or Jenny.Esker@riotinto.com.

National Tribal Air Association’s Comments on the U.S. EPA’s Proposed 
Primary Copper Smelter Residual Risk and Technology Review, Docket 
EPA-HQ-OA-2020-0430 
 
Dear Ms. Dawson: 
The National Tribal Air Association (NTAA) is pleased to submit this letter to 
provide comments on the Environmental Protection Agency’s (EPA’s) Proposed 
results of EPA’s Residual Risk and Technology Review (RTR) for the National 
Emission Standards for Hazardous Air Pollutants (NESHAP) for major source 
Primary Copper Smelters as required under the Clean Air Act (CAA) and the 
technology review for the Primary Copper Smelting area source NESHAP.   
The NTAA is a member-based organization with 154 principal member Tribes. 
The organization’s mission is to advance air quality management policies and 
programs, consistent with the needs, interests, and unique legal status of Indian 
Tribes. As such, the NTAA uses its resources to support the efforts of all federally 
recognized Tribes in protecting and improving the air quality within their 
respective jurisdictions. Although NTAA always seeks to represent consensus 
perspectives on any given issue, it is important to note that the views expressed by 
the NTAA may not be agreed upon by all Tribes. Further, it is also important to 
understand interactions with the organization does not substitute for government-
to government consultation, which can only be achieved through direct 
communications between the federal government and Indian Tribes. 
The NTAA agrees with the EPA’s proposal to determine the risk to be 
unacceptable, given the lead and arsenic emissions as well as the other HAP 
metals to the current regulations. The NTAA also agrees with the proposal for 
new standards for process fugitive emissions from anode refining roofline vents 
and work practices to minimize fugitive dust emissions, which will achieve 
acceptable risks and protect human health with an ample margin of safety.  
The NTAA is supportive of the proposal to establish emission standards for 
mercury emissions, separate of the PM standards that address emissions of other 
metal HAPs. Since mercury is emitted in the vapor stage, the NTAA agrees PM is 
not an appropriate surrogate for controls of mercury emissions.   

www.NTAATribalAir.org 
 
 
                                               National Tribal Air Association 
928.523.0526 office 
 
 
 
 
                                              P.O. Box 15004 
928.523.1266 fax  
 
 
 
 
                                Flagstaff, AZ 86011-5004  
 
 
The NTAA supports the proposal to include opacity limits for the Primary Copper smelters. It is 
appropriate to establish opacity limits (such as the opacity limits in the state air permits and CD) 
in the NESHAP in addition to the numeric PM MACT floor emissions limits. As EPA discussed 
in the proposal many of these opacity limits are already required in State permits, so there is no 
additional cost to the sources but by including opacity limits as part of the NESHAP, it 
establishes the national requirement as it provides a level-playing field across sources. 
Furthermore, as EPA discussed, these opacity limits provide for ongoing monitoring for upsets 
or other operational and compliance issues. The opacity limits should be in addition to the 
numeric PM MACT floor emissions limits as an additional layer of protection for the public.   
The NTAA believes that the EPA should establish direct lead limits, either in addition to, or 
instead of, the PM limit, because it is one of the risk drivers for this source category and would 
be appropriate to control for it directly.   
The NTAA is supportive of the ‘Beyond the Floor’ and ample margin of safety determinations 
for this source category, given the impacts of this source category on Native American and 
Hispanic populations. Providing protections beyond the floor will help reduce the risk to these 
disproportionately impacted populations.  
Specifically, the NTAA supports of this proposal: 
• BTF emissions limit for PM of 1.6 lbs/hr for anode refining process fugitive 
emissions at existing and new sources 
• Ample margin of safety determination the proposal to require facilities to develop and 
implement a robust fugitive dust plan, requiring, at a minimum, the specific work 
practices but also could include other practices identified by the facilities. 
Finally, the NTAA is disappointed with the apparent lack of attention to the impacts on the 
Tribes that are located near the two major sources; this includes the San Carlos Apache Tribe.  
The language you use in addressing EO 13175.   
This action does not have Tribal implications as specified in Executive Order 13175. 
Thus, Executive Order 13175 does not apply to this action. However, consistent with 
the EPA policy on coordination and consultation with Indian Tribes, the EPA will offer 
government-to-government consultation with Tribes as requested.  
We recognize that these sources are not on the reservations; however, and thus, the Tribe does 
not have jurisdiction over these sources, so under the terms of the EO by itself there are not 
“implications”.  However, the Agency’s should act on its own Indian Policy and the Tribal 
Treaty Rights policy in determining if consultation is required. It is disingenuous given the 
proximity to the reservation and its impact on nearby Tribal populations, the environment, and 
potential treaty right to not have proactively reached out and offered consultation to the impacted 
Tribes in Hayden and surrounding Gila Counties.   
The NTAA appreciates this opportunity to submit this pre-proposal comment and looks forward 
to further work with the EPA on this important issue. If you have any questions or seek

National Emission Standards for Hazardous Air Pollutants: Primary Copper Smelting

To whom it may concern: 
 
The Arizona Department of Environmental Quality (ADEQ) appreciates the opportunity to 
comment on the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency’s (EPA) proposed National Emissions 
Standard for Hazardous Air Pollutants (NESHAP) for primary copper smelting published in the 
Federal Register on January 11, 2022.  
 
EPA’s proposed rule established a deadline of February 25, 2022 for public comment.  ADEQ is 
requesting that EPA extend the comment period because: 1) the proposal is technically complex 
and will have a substantial impact on the copper smelters in Arizona; 2) the rule language was 
not made available until February 2, 2022; and 3) Executive Orders 12866 and 13563 suggest 
that a comment period should, at a minimum, provide for 60 days.   Therefore, ADEQ requests 
that EPA extend the public comment period by at least 30 days and notify the public of the 
extension as soon as possible and well in advance of the February 25 deadline so commenters 
may make full use of the additional time. 
 
First, EPA’s proposal is technically complex and has substantial economic impacts for the two 
copper smelters in Arizona.  Additionally, EPA’s proposed test and compliance methodology 
requires extensive evaluation for how ADEQ would implement this proposed rule.  ADEQ’s 
evaluation of the proposed rule is still on-going at this time. 
 
Second, the docket originally contained a document as “Proposed Regulation Edits for 40 CFR 
Part 63, Subpart QQQ” (Document 64).0F1  On February 1, 2022 (21 days after the opening of this 
docket), Document 90, an updated version of the proposed regulatory edits, was uploaded to the 
                                                 
1 86 FR 1,616, 1,619 (Jan. 11, 2022). 

Page 2 of 2 
 
docket.  Document 90 appears to have significant changes from the version in Document 64. 
These different versions necessitated additional review time to understand the full scope of 
EPA’s proposal.  Therefore, ADEQ requests EPA extend the comment period to allow additional 
time to review the proposed language of the regulations.  
 
Third, Executive Orders 12866 and 13563 suggest in most instances, a comment period should 
provide for a minimum of 60 days.  Executive Order 12866 provides that “. . .each agency 
should afford the public a meaningful opportunity to comment on any proposed regulation, 
which in most cases should include a comment period of not less than 60 days.”  Executive 
Order 13563 likewise directs agencies to “...afford the public a meaningful opportunity to 
comment through the Internet on any proposed regulation, with a comment period that should 
generally be at least 60 days.” These Executive Orders establish a general rule of providing a 
minimum of 60 days for public comment. The technically and legally complex issues the NPRM 
addresses illustrate why a minimum of 60 days must be allowed for the public to file comments 
in response to the rule. EPA’s proposed rule does not provide a justification for providing only 
45 days for public comment.   
 
ADEQ appreciates EPA’s need to have adequate time to consider public comments, conduct any 
additional analyses, develop the final rule package, and complete necessary agency and 
interagency review.  Additionally, ADEQ recognizes that EPA is operating under a court ordered 
deadline to complete this rulemaking.   However, these needs should also be balanced against the 
public's interest to have adequate time to consider and respond to EPA’s complex proposal.  
ADEQ is of the opinion that 45 days is an insufficient amount of time, especially given the 
complexity of these issues, and delays in providing the current version of the proposed 
regulatory text. 
 
If you have any questions, please contact Joseph Martini, Air Quality Improvement Planning 
Section, Air Quality Division, at 602-771-1089 or martini.joseph@azdeq.gov.  
 
Thank you for your consideration of ADEQ’s comments. 
 
Sincerely, 
 
 
 
Daniel Czecholinski 
Air Quality Division Director
