Re:  
National Emission Standards for Hazardous Air Pollutants: Primary Copper Smelting  
Residual Risk and Technology Review and Primary Copper Smelting Area Source Technology 
Review (87 FR 1,616) 
 
To whom it may concern: 
 
The Arizona Department of Environmental Quality (ADEQ) appreciates the opportunity to comment on 
the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency’s (EPA) proposed National Emissions Standard for Hazardous 
Air Pollutants (NESHAP) for primary copper smelting published in the Federal Register on January 11, 
2022.  Additionally, ADEQ appreciates EPA’s decision to expand the comment period. 
 
Under Arizona Revised Statutes (A.R.S) § 49-402(A)(2), ADEQ has original jurisdiction over the copper 
smelters located in Arizona, and therefore is the permitting authority for these two sources.  
Additionally, ADEQ has responsibility for compliance and enforcement. 
 
ADEQ has two concerns regarding two aspects of the proposed rule: 1) demonstrating compliance with 
the roofline limits might be practically impossible as EPA’s proposed methodology cannot be 
implemented at either the Miami, AZ or Hayden, AZ smelters; 2) EPA’s roofline limits were derived from 
data that was not collected using an EPA test method or using a reference monitor.  In addition to those 
concerns, ADEQ supports EPA’s decision to not include an opacity limit in the proposed rule. 
 
 
 
 

Page 2 of 6 
 
I. 
EPA’s proposed performance test method cannot be implemented at the smelters. 
A. EPA’s proposed rule requires isokinetic sampling, which is not possible at either 
smelter. 
 
ADEQ is concerned that EPA’s proposed rule presents difficulties demonstrating compliance with EPA’s 
proposed compliance testing methods for the copper smelters located in Miami, AZ and Hayden, AZ.   
Specifically, EPA’s proposed rule is based on Method 1 for selecting sample port locations.1  As will be 
discussed below, ADEQ believes that there are no locations along either  facilities’ rooflines that meet 
requirements of Method 1.  Therefore, ADEQ requests that EPA reconsider its compliance testing 
requirements in the proposed rule to permit alternatives that do not require isokinetic sampling. 
 
EPA’s proposed rule establishes three limits on emissions from existing facilities’ roofline: 
● 
For converter operations, EPA is proposing a PM emission limit of 1.7 lbs/hr for process 
fugitive roof vents.2 
● 
For smelting furnaces, EPA is proposing a PM emissions limit of 4.3 lbs/hr for process 
fugitive roof vents.3 
● 
For anode refining operations, EPA is proposing a limit of 1.6 lbs/hr for process roof 
vents.4 
 
EPA’s proposed rule establishes a fugitive emission limit from the smelter roofline vents.5 In order to 
demonstrate compliance with the roof vent filterable particulate matter emission limits in the proposed 
40 CFR § 63.1444, the facility must conduct a performance test to determine compliance with the limit.6  
40 CFR §§ 63.1450(e)-(f) establishes the performance test to determine compliance with the proposed 
40 CFR § 63.1444.  Both proposed §§ 63.1450(e)(1)(i) and 63.1450(f)(1)(i) require the use of Method 1 to 
select sampling port locations and number of traverse points. 
 
ADEQ is concerned that there are physical limitations for both facilities that prevent the use of Method 
1.  This section discusses these concerns for the smelter in Miami, AZ and then the smelter in Hayden, 
AZ.  As described in a technical report on test method roofline lead emissions, operational influences 
during testing, and effect on smelter reconfiguration, prepared by Trinity Consultants and Hatch for the 
FMI Miami smelter (Document 62), there are no locations that meet Method 1’s requirements.7  
 
                                                 
1 Regulations.gov, Proposed Regulation Edits for 40 CFR Part 63, Subpart QQQ: Primary Copper Smelting NESHAP Risk and Technology Review 
Proposal, Regulatory text with proposed edits in redline/strikeout, available at https://www.regulations.gov/document/EPA-HQ-OAR-2020-
0430-0090 (Document 90) (proposed 40 CFR §§ 63.1450(e)-(f)). 
2 87 FR 1,616, 1,635 (Jan. 11, 2022). 
3 Id.  
4 Id. 
5 Supra note 1 at 40 CFR §§ 63.1444(b)(2)(iii), (d)(7). 
6 Supra note 1 at 40 CFR §§ 63.1450(e)-(f). 
7 Regulations.gov, Freeport-McMoRan Miami, Inc. - Consent Order Docket No. A-10-18 Response to Items III.C and III.D, available at 
https://www.regulations.gov/document/EPA-HQ-OAR-2020-0430-0062 (Document 62). 

Page 3 of 6 
The Miami smelter’s Air Quality Permit, issued by ADEQ, requires annual roofline fugitive testing for PM, 
PM10, PM2.5, and Pb, as well as a roofline monitoring system for SO2.8  In order to meet the PM testing 
requirement for the ADEQ permit, FMI Miami conducted a feasibility study on different test methods at 
the roofline.9  This report examined several test methods (Methods 1, 14, 5 and 29, 201A and 202, 5D 
and 29).10   
 
This report discussed the difficulties of implementing Method 1, which involves the placement of a 
sample probe into the exhaust stream where sampling is obtained isokinetically.11  This report 
concluded that the roofline vents at the Miami smelter cannot meet the minimum methods of Method 
1, including either the simplified (section 1.2) procedure or alternative procedure (11.5).12  
 
If Method 1 cannot be utilized effectively at the two facilities subject to the major source rule, ADEQ is 
concerned the rule is not practical to implement or enforce. 
 
B. Test methods that rely on Method 1  
 
This section discusses the impacts of the facilities  rooflines on EPA’s proposed methodology.  As part of 
its compliance testing methodology, the proposed rule requires the use of additional test methods.13  
These methods rely on sampling obtained via Method 1.   
However, regarding the Miami smelter, Document 62 examined several of these methods.  For example, 
Document 62 EPA Method 5D found that the application of this test method for fugitive emissions at the 
roofline is problematic, as the FMI Miami roofline does not meet the criteria of Method 1.14   EPA 
Method 5D requires isokinetic sampling that is designed to sample numerous sample points (twenty-
four or greater) across the entirety of a positive pressure fabric filter outlet, which meets EPA Method 1 
criteria for isokinetic sampling.  However, as described above, since the roofline does not meet Method 
1, ADEQ believes that the Miami smelter will not be able to meet this requirement.  This same issue 
appears to be the same for EPA’s proposed use of Oregon 8.15 
Additionally, EPA Method 29 for lead emissions needs a separate sample system essentially doubling the 
number of samples to be collected under isokinetic conditions. Because quality assurance objectives for 
representativeness require isokinetic conditions; this is not a feasible method for sampling rooflines, as 
isokinetic conditions are not possible. 
                                                 
8 Id. 
9 Id. 
10 Id. at 10 - 13. 
11 Id.; see also 40 CFR Part 60, Appendix A, Method 1. 
12 See supra note 7. 
13 Supra note 1 at 40 § CFR 63.1450(e)(1)(ii)-(iv), (f)(1)(ii)-(iv). 
14 Id. 
15 Supra note 1 at 40 CFR § 63.1450(e)(1)(v), (f)(1)(v). 

Page 4 of 6 
Further, determination of gas flow angles in the roofline vents is impractical due to variability in the 
smelting operations and ambient wind conditions.16 For example, during wind gusts, the air pressure on 
one side of the roofline may be greater than the air pressure under the roofline causing additional air to 
flow into the roofline from above.  In contrast, on the opposite side  of the roofline, the reverse effect 
could occur and causing air to flow out of the roofline. Because wind gusts  affect both the flow rate and 
direction at the roofline vents, obtaining accurate gas flow traversing angles requires consistently calm 
wind conditions, which is not likely to occur outside of a controlled laboratory environment. To 
compensate for the variability in wind conditions, , the sampling protocol would require constant 
instantaneous adjustments to the sample probes (during test runs of 12 hours or more) to meet the 
isokinetic test requirements in EPA Method 5.17 Such instantaneous adjustments may not be practicable 
to implement during sampling .  
Also, Method 5 sampling protocol incorporates a requirement for adequate sample locations to account 
for variations in the flows along the roofline.  This necessitates a large number of sampling staff to be 
located in a dangerous, high temperature environment for extended periods of 12 hours or more.18 
Temperatures at the roofline can reach 140°F and poses a significant safety concern for the testing 
personnel. 
After assessing several other test methods, the study conducted by FMI Miami concluded that using 
MiniVol portable air samplers, manufactured by Airmetrics, is the most representative sampling method 
for the roofline emissions application.19 The MiniVol is not a Federal Reference Method (FRM) sampler, 
but provides results that closely approximate data from FRM samplers to obtain representative 
concentrations of particulate matter without the need for isokinetic sampling . Airmetrics, in 
conjunction with the EPA, developed the patented low-flow technology found in the MiniVol to address 
the need for portable air sampling.20 The portable air samplers can be run concurrently at several 
locations along the roofline.  This approach offers several benefits:  (1)  fluctuations in flows and 
emissions along the roofline are better managed, (2) Sampling is  not dependent on linear air flow, so 
constant adjustments are not required, and  (3) Sampling can occur for for longer periods of time, which 
provides a  more representative sample of the process operations occurring in the smelter buildings.  
Use of this sampling protocol will require the collection of velocity and temperature measurements 
using the existing roofline monitoring system equipment. As an added benefit, the portable air samplers 
also are capable of speciating samples of PM, PM10, and PM2.5.21  
During required annual compliance testing, the Miami smelter concurrently utilizes 5 samplers at the 
Anode Building roofline, 3 samplers at the IsaSmelt Furnace Building roofline, 2 samplers at the 
Converter Building roofline, and 2 samplers at the Electric Furnace Building roofline. 22 
                                                 
16 See supra note 7.

Page 5 of 6 
Similarly, the roofline vents at the Asarco copper smelter located in Hayden, AZ do not meet the 
requirements of Method 1 to obtain samples isokinetically. Asarco utilized MiniVol samplers during its 
Fugitive Emissions Study as part of the 2015 consent decree with EPA.   
Considering the test methods that rely on Method 1, ADEQ is concerned about the feasibility of 
requiring Method 1 for roofline compliance testing.  
C. A standard based on  data from  MiniVol samplers should reflect the practical 
challenges meeting Method 1. 
In a related point, ADEQ is concerned that the proposed roofline lead limit is based on data collected 
using samplers that are not designated as a Federal Reference Method (FRM). Use of non-FRM sampler 
data could create a standard that is not achievable, if tested using a FRM.  It is unclear from EPA’s 
proposed rule how to address a potential discrepancy between a standard based on non-FRM and 
testing using a FRM.  
As discussed above, the roofline PM data from the FMI Miami facility was obtained from MiniVol 
samplers.23 As described in Document 62, FMI utilized MiniVol samplers in May/June 2018 at the Miami 
Smelter.  It appears that this data was utilized by EPA to develop its roofline emissions limits.24   EPA 
used the MiniVol data for its proposed PM roofline limit.25 However, as discussed above, this data was 
collected using a method other than Method 5 due to the issues surrounding the FMI Miami facility 
roofline.  Therefore, EPA’s rule is based on data that does not meet its own methodology.   
EPA’s proposed PM limit was established using data that was collected using a method other than 
Method 5.26  As the permitting agency, ADEQ has three concerns with EPA’s rule: First, it is not clear 
from EPA’s rule that a Method 5 test conducted at the same time would have produced the same result 
as the alternative method used to obtain the data the rule is based on. Second, it is unknown whether 
this standard is achievable, as determined by the proposed test methodology. Third, as described above, 
neither rooflines meet Method 1 and there are no locations to obtain samples under this method. 
As there are only two existing copper smelters that are major sources of HAPs in the United States, 
ADEQ believes that the rule should provide for an effective method of implementation at both facilities.  
As currently structured, the rule does not do so.  This concern could potentially be addressed by 
reconsidering the proposed requirements for roofline compliance testing. 
III. ADEQ supports EPA’s decision to not set an opacity limit. 
In its proposal, EPA solicited comments on whether to set an opacity limit in the NESHAPs rule.27  As 
discussed in EPA’s April 5, 2021 memorandum entitled “Opacity Standards for Major Primary Copper 
Smelting Facilities,” EPA decided not to propose opacity limits due to variability in opacity limits in air 
                                                 
23 Regulations.gov, Memorandum: MACT Floor Analysis for the Primary Copper Smelting Source Category (Nov. 27, 2020), Section 3 available at 
https://www.regulations.gov/document/EPA-HQ-OAR-2020-0430-0055 (Document 55) (“The data collected for the fugitive roofline vents was 
not collected using an EPA method or using reference monitors.”). 
24 Id.  
25 See supra note 22, 3. 
26 Id. 
27 86 FR 1,616, 1,634 (Jan. 11, 2022). 

Page 6 of 6 
permits and the consent decree for Asarco.28  ADEQ agrees with EPA’s decision to not propose an 
opacity limit, as it is not clear that an opacity standard represents either the maximum achievable 
control technology (MACT) floor or beyond-the-floor (BTF) standard.  ADEQ believes that the limits 
established by existing operating permits, or consent decree, are sufficient.   
IV. Conclusion. 
This issue is one of concern for ADEQ, as the department has original jurisdiction for sources that pertain 
to the smelting of metal ore.29 ADEQ will be involved in the issuance of any title V permits, and 
compliance with these permits for the two copper smelters in Arizona.   Therefore, ADEQ requests EPA 
reconsider its rule to address these concerns. 
If you have any questions, please contact Joseph Martini, Air Quality Improvement Planning Section, Air 
Quality Division, at 602-771-1089 or martini.joseph@azdeq.gov.  
 
Thank you for your consideration of ADEQ’s comments.

National Tribal Air Association’s Comments on the U.S. EPA’s Proposed 
Primary Copper Smelter Residual Risk and Technology Review, Docket 
EPA-HQ-OA-2020-0430 
 
Dear Ms. Dawson: 
The National Tribal Air Association (NTAA) is pleased to submit this letter to 
provide comments on the Environmental Protection Agency’s (EPA’s) Proposed 
results of EPA’s Residual Risk and Technology Review (RTR) for the National 
Emission Standards for Hazardous Air Pollutants (NESHAP) for major source 
Primary Copper Smelters as required under the Clean Air Act (CAA) and the 
technology review for the Primary Copper Smelting area source NESHAP.   
The NTAA is a member-based organization with 154 principal member Tribes. 
The organization’s mission is to advance air quality management policies and 
programs, consistent with the needs, interests, and unique legal status of Indian 
Tribes. As such, the NTAA uses its resources to support the efforts of all federally 
recognized Tribes in protecting and improving the air quality within their 
respective jurisdictions. Although NTAA always seeks to represent consensus 
perspectives on any given issue, it is important to note that the views expressed by 
the NTAA may not be agreed upon by all Tribes. Further, it is also important to 
understand interactions with the organization does not substitute for government-
to government consultation, which can only be achieved through direct 
communications between the federal government and Indian Tribes. 
The NTAA agrees with the EPA’s proposal to determine the risk to be 
unacceptable, given the lead and arsenic emissions as well as the other HAP 
metals to the current regulations. The NTAA also agrees with the proposal for 
new standards for process fugitive emissions from anode refining roofline vents 
and work practices to minimize fugitive dust emissions, which will achieve 
acceptable risks and protect human health with an ample margin of safety.  
The NTAA is supportive of the proposal to establish emission standards for 
mercury emissions, separate of the PM standards that address emissions of other 
metal HAPs. Since mercury is emitted in the vapor stage, the NTAA agrees PM is 
not an appropriate surrogate for controls of mercury emissions.   

www.NTAATribalAir.org 
 
 
                                               National Tribal Air Association 
928.523.0526 office 
 
 
 
 
                                              P.O. Box 15004 
928.523.1266 fax  
 
 
 
 
                                Flagstaff, AZ 86011-5004  
 
 
The NTAA supports the proposal to include opacity limits for the Primary Copper smelters. It is 
appropriate to establish opacity limits (such as the opacity limits in the state air permits and CD) 
in the NESHAP in addition to the numeric PM MACT floor emissions limits. As EPA discussed 
in the proposal many of these opacity limits are already required in State permits, so there is no 
additional cost to the sources but by including opacity limits as part of the NESHAP, it 
establishes the national requirement as it provides a level-playing field across sources. 
Furthermore, as EPA discussed, these opacity limits provide for ongoing monitoring for upsets 
or other operational and compliance issues. The opacity limits should be in addition to the 
numeric PM MACT floor emissions limits as an additional layer of protection for the public.   
The NTAA believes that the EPA should establish direct lead limits, either in addition to, or 
instead of, the PM limit, because it is one of the risk drivers for this source category and would 
be appropriate to control for it directly.   
The NTAA is supportive of the ‘Beyond the Floor’ and ample margin of safety determinations 
for this source category, given the impacts of this source category on Native American and 
Hispanic populations. Providing protections beyond the floor will help reduce the risk to these 
disproportionately impacted populations.  
Specifically, the NTAA supports of this proposal: 
• BTF emissions limit for PM of 1.6 lbs/hr for anode refining process fugitive 
emissions at existing and new sources 
• Ample margin of safety determination the proposal to require facilities to develop and 
implement a robust fugitive dust plan, requiring, at a minimum, the specific work 
practices but also could include other practices identified by the facilities. 
Finally, the NTAA is disappointed with the apparent lack of attention to the impacts on the 
Tribes that are located near the two major sources; this includes the San Carlos Apache Tribe.  
The language you use in addressing EO 13175.   
This action does not have Tribal implications as specified in Executive Order 13175. 
Thus, Executive Order 13175 does not apply to this action. However, consistent with 
the EPA policy on coordination and consultation with Indian Tribes, the EPA will offer 
government-to-government consultation with Tribes as requested.  
We recognize that these sources are not on the reservations; however, and thus, the Tribe does 
not have jurisdiction over these sources, so under the terms of the EO by itself there are not 
“implications”.  However, the Agency’s should act on its own Indian Policy and the Tribal 
Treaty Rights policy in determining if consultation is required. It is disingenuous given the 
proximity to the reservation and its impact on nearby Tribal populations, the environment, and 
potential treaty right to not have proactively reached out and offered consultation to the impacted 
Tribes in Hayden and surrounding Gila Counties.   
The NTAA appreciates this opportunity to submit this pre-proposal comment and looks forward 
to further work with the EPA on this important issue. If you have any questions or seek

= FREEPORT-McMoRAN

VIA ELECTRONIC SUBMISSION 
The Honorable Michael S. Regan

Re: | Comments by Freeport-McMoRan Miami Inc. on the Proposed Primary Copper Smelting 
NESHAP Rule Revisions (Docket ID No. EPA-HQ-OAR-2020-0430) 
Dear Administrator Regan: 
Freeport-McMoRan Miami Inc. (“Freeport” or “FMMI”) currently owns and operates a 
copper smelter located in Miami, Gila County, Arizona (the “Miami Smelter’). The Miami Smelter 
is one of only two sources regulated under the Environmental Protection Agency’s (“EPA”) 
National Emission Standards for Hazardous Air Pollutants (“NESHAP”) for Primary Copper 
Smelting, 40 CFR part 63, subpart QQQ (the “proposed NESHAP revisions”). The proposed 
NESHAP revisions will substantially change the air emission limitations applicable to the Miami 
Smelter as well 
as how the facility must demonstrate compliance with the rule limitations. 
Complying with these proposed changes would impose substantial additional capital and operating 
costs on FMMI. 
FMML isa subsidiary of Freeport-McMoRan Inc. (FCX), one of the world's largest publicly 
traded copper producers. The FCX portfolio of assets includes the Grasberg minerals district in 
Indonesia, one of the world's largest copper and gold deposits; and significant mining operations 
in North America and South America, including the large-scale Morenci minerals district in 
Arizona and the Cerro Verde operation in Peru. Freeport has a long history of robust sustainability 
programs and reporting. In 2021, FCX issued its 20th Annual Sustainability Report and its second 
annual Climate Report. Since 2005, FCX has reported 
its sustainability performance under 
standards of the Global Reporting Initiative (GRI) and now also reports on its performance in 
alignment with standards of the Sustainability Accounting Standards Board (SASB). Freeport is 
also 
committed 
to 
aligning 
its 
strategy 
and 
disclosures 
in 
the 
coming 
years 
with 
the 
recommendations of the Task Force on Climate-related Financial Disclosures (TCFD). In addition, 
FCX is continuously striving to embrace evolving stakeholder expectations and to implement best 
practices. 
FCX recognizes that mining and metal processing impacts the natural environment. Our 
goal is to conduct our mining and processing operations in a manner that minimizes adverse 
impacts on the environment and supports protection of the ecosystems through responsible 
environmental stewardship. To that end, FCX understands that environmental protection and 
stewardship are the keys to ensuring the long-term viability of our business, including maintaining 
the necessary support from our host communities and governments. Our policy objectives include 
minimizing environmental impacts using risk management strategies based on valid data and 
sound science.

FCX is committed to validating all of our copper producing sites, including our mines and 
smelting and refining facilities, with the Copper Mark, a comprehensive assurance framework 
designed to demonstrate the copper industry’s responsible production practices. To achieve the 
Copper Mark, 
each 
site 
is required 
to complete 
an 
external assurance process 
to 
assess 
conformance with 32 environmental, social and governance requirements. To date, a total of seven 
sites, including the Miami Smelter, have been validated. 
With that environmental commitment in mind, FMMI nevertheless believes that some of 
the specific proposed NESHAP revisions are not appropriate, and accordingly require revision. 
FMMI’s comments on the Proposed NESHAP Revisions are organized as follows. First, this letter 
describes in summary fashion the primary substantive concerns raised by the proposed revisions 
including a narrative explanation and suggested resolution for those concerns. 
Second, Table 
1 
attached to this letter sets out FMMI’s specific comments and suggested revisions to the actual 
regulatory language text. Importantly, Table 
1 addresses not only the primary substantive concerns 
discussed in this letter but also additional comments and suggested revisions to the regulation text 
to correct apparent errors, resolve internal inconsistencies and provide greater clarity. The third 
part of FMMI’s comments is a set of Attachments A thru L that contain the underlying analysis 
supporting the comments raised in this letter and Table 1. 
i 
The MACT floor PM limits for the Anode Refining Department should be the same 
as the existing (unchanged) limits for similar affected sources managing molten 
material. 
EPA has proposed a MACT-floor based emission limit for the Anode Refining Department 
based upon testing 
at the Asarco smelter (the other primary copper smelter subject 
to this 
NESHAP). But there is already an existing technology-based limit for similar emission points in 
the current NESHAP that 
is applicable and more appropriate than the Asarco-based 
limit. 
Specifically, in the existing NESHAP (and unchanged with the Proposed NESHAP Revisions) 
capture and control systems for process gases from vessels managing molten material, including 
the concentrate dryers, smelting vessels, slag cleaning vessels, and copper converter departments, 
must meet a concentration-based filterable particulate matter (“fPM”) emission limitation of 23 
mg/dscm. (see 40 CFR §§ 63.1444(a)(2), 63.1444(b)(2)(ii), 63.1444(c)(3)(ii), and 63.1444(d)(6)).! 
This is a consistent technology-based limit that is currently applied to multiple gas streams similar 
to those from the anode furnace, and there is no technical justification for deviating from this 
established standard for the anode refining department. For the FMMI facility, this concentration- 
based limit would apply to the aisle scrubber stack, which discharges captured and controlled gases 
originating from both the anode refining department and copper converter department, with each 
affected source subject to the same 23 mg/dscm limit. Accordingly, EPA should set the MACT 
floor for the anode refining department based on a concentration limitation of 23 mg/dscm. 
Even if there was a justification to treat the anode furnace differently from every other 
molten material vessel (which there is not), EPA does not have sufficient data to set a mass rate 
limit for the anode refining process vent MACT floor. The data EPA used in the Proposed 
' The major furnace left out of the current rule standards is the anode furnace. But EPA addressed the anode furnace 
vent stream in the Asarco 2015 consent decree and applied the same 23 mg/dscm emission limitation. 
See EPA-HQ- 
OAR-2020-0430-0018 (Asarco consent decree) at 418 (p. 27)(implicitly acknowledging that this is a similar vent 
stream).

NESHAP Revisions is not representative of the covered sources. Indeed, all of the anode refining 
point source fPM test data used by EPA in the calculation of the MACT floor emission rates came 
from the Asarco smelter. See 87 Fed. Reg. 1632 (noting that EPA had anode refining point source 
test data for only one source). As a result, the MACT floor Upper Prediction Limit (“UPL”) 
calculation is based on a 
single facility. The UPL does not reflect the operating performance of 
FMMP’s anode refining baghouse and does not reflect the “best 5 sources” (or all available sources 
when as here there are less than 5 total) as prescribed by EPA procedure for source categories 
containing less than 30 sources. 
Direct data regarding the performance of the anode furnace baghouse using EPA test 
methods is not available at FMMI due to the configuration of this facility. Emissions from the 
FMMI anode baghouse are routed to the aisle scrubber where it is combined with additional vent 
streams. The aisle scrubber then provides additional emissions control for the combined vent 
stream (including the anode baghouse effluent) before it is emitted from the aisle scrubber stack. 
The current vent ducting configuration does not permit intermediate testing of the discrete anode 
baghouse effluent that meets the location requirements of the EPA sampling methods, and as a 
result, there is no available testing of this discrete vent using EPA-approved methods.” Moreover, 
testing at this location would not account for any additional pollution control accomplished in the 
aisle scrubber. 
The only available emissions data specifically capturing FMMI’s anode refining baghouse 
are from testing that was conducted in November 2018 in connection with assessing baghouse 
performance. 
These measurements were focused on the baghouse performance for engineering 
estimations and the sample location did not comply with EPA method requirements. The FMMI 
anode refining baghouse does not discharge directly to the environment (it is routed to the aisle 
scrubber), and these internal process test results were not provided when responding to EPA’s 
Information Collection Request in connection with this rulemaking. Regardless, incorporation of 
these results into EPA’s MACT floor analysis provides 
a more complete data set and should 
strengthen EPA’s understanding of the anode furnace baghouse emissions. Accordingly, a copy of 
the 
test report prepared 
by SLR International Corporation 
is included 
as Attachment 
B.? 
Importantly, if FMMI performance test data are considered, the variability in the data set increases 
(standard deviation of 2.2 mg/dscm including the FMMI data, and 1.6 mg/dscm with only the 
Asarco data). Recalculation of the 99% Upper Prediction Limit (“UPL”) with the FMMI anode 
refining process vent data included yields a UPL of 7.3 mg/dscm. 
Please see UPL calculations 
included as Attachment C. Again, FMMI is not suggesting that this performance data should be 
used 
as 
a basis for an anode-only MACT floor, 
as noted above the existing 23 mg/dscm 
concentration-based limit should be used. But the performance data does illustrate that a more 
complete data collection would be necessary to support an anode-only MACT floor calculation 
that is representative of the covered sources. 
? An explanation of the testing limitations of the anode baghouse effluent ductwork configuration prepared by FMMI’s 
consultant, Ramboll, is included as Attachment A. 
3 As noted above, these SLR measurements do not meet all applicable regulatory performance test requirements. For

In summary, the MACT floor UPL calculation derived by EPA comes from an extremely 
limited data set 
at 
a single facility, clearly does not represent the “best 
5 sources,” and 
is

A complete data collection to support 
a robust MACT floor calculation would require

configuration at the FMMI facility requires this measurement as part of a combined vent stream at 
the aisle scrubber. Thus, this data collection would require an evaluation using the combined gas 
stream procedures outlined in 40 CFR § 63.1446. The necessary underlying data for this complex 
process does not currently exist and is unnecessary given the uniform concentration-based limit 
applied to all similar vessels under the primary copper smelting NESHAP. Accordingly, FMMI 
requests that EPA adopt a MACT limit for the anode refining department of 23 mg/dscm, which 
is the same as the current limit for four other similar fPM-containing gas streams from molten

IL. 
EPA’s Approach to the Process Fugitive Emissions Currently Exiting from Roofline 
Vents Require Several Revisions. 
A. 
EPA’s residual risk analysis overstates acute non-cancer risks and does not 
support the need for additional controls on the roofline vents to meet the 
agency’s “acceptability” criteria. 
EPA has proposed additional emission controls for the anode furnace roofline vent based 
on its conclusion that current emissions do not meet its “acceptable risk” criteria under EPA’s RTR 
process. See 87 Fed. Reg. 1643. Specifically, the agency concluded (i) its acute, non-cancer risk 
screening assessment for arsenic yielded 
a hazard quotient (“HQ”) of 7, and (ii) emissions 
modeling predicted very limited exceedances of the three-month average primary National 
Ambient Air Quality Standard (“NAAQS”) 
for lead. 
Jd. Review of the available scientific 
literature, site data, and EPA precedent suggests that EPA’s risk analysis overstates these risks and 
the current emissions meet EPA’s “acceptable risk” thresholds. Accordingly, control of the anode 
furnace roofline vents must be justified, if at all, under EPA’s “ample margin of safety analysis” 
or its “reasonable further progress” technology-based standard. 
1. 
The acute non-cancer threshold for arsenic employed by EPA in its risk 
assessment is overly conservative and not supported by 
a balanced 
assessment of the scientific literature. 
EPA’s RTR for acute non-cancer arsenic risk relies on an arsenic inhalation toxicity value 
that does not reflect the latest scientific evidence for acute exposures. Specifically, EPA only cites 
a reference exposure level (“REL”) developed by the California Office of Environmental Health 
Hazard Assessment (“OEHHA”) in 2008 for acute (1 hour) exposures for arsenic (0.2 ug/m3). 
The California REL, however, 
is based 
on 
a poorly documented 
and outdated 
study by 
Nagymajtenyi et al. (1985) that focuses on potential developmental effects. But the study suffers 
from several substantial weaknesses including the failure to adequately distinguish maternal 
toxicity 
from 
the 
allegedly observed 
developmental 
effects. 
Indeed, 
EPA 
has previously 
acknowledged the “uncertainty and conservative nature” of the basis for the acute HQ for arsenic 
based on the Nagymaitenyi study. See Integrated Iron and Steel Manufacturing NESHAP, 85 Fed. 
4

Reg. 42074, 42083 (Jul. 13, 2020) (acknowledging that there are “data gaps and uncertainties in 
acute reference values”). Several other regulatory authorities have rejected the Nagymaitenyi study 
as a basis for deriving toxicity values, and have instead relied on a 1999 study (Holson et al. 1999) 
conducted according to Good Laboratory Practices (“GLP’’) with associated better documentation 
and study design. 
Relying on the Holson 
et al. (1999) study, the Texas Commission on Environmental 
Quality (“TCEQ”) derived an acute reference value (ReV) of 9.9 ug/m3 and an acute effects 
screening level (“ESL”) of 3 ug/m3 for short-term 
(1 
hour) exposures (as compared 
to the 
California REL of 0.2 ug/m3). TCEQ describes ESLs as “‘chemical concentrations in air that are 
safe [that] protect human health in the general public, including children, the elderly, pregnant 
women, and people with pre-existing health conditions.”* The TCEQ 1-hour ESL for arsenic of 3 
ug/m3 
is based on good science, protective 
of public health, 
and suitable 
for use 
as 
the 
“acceptability” threshold for the agency’s RTR review. Importantly, if a non-cancer 1-hour arsenic 
threshold of 3 ug/m3 
is used, the HQ for arsenic drops to 0.5--well below 
| (meeting EPA’s 
“acceptable risk” threshold). 
A description of the basis for the OEHHA REL, the newer developmental studies, and 
regulatory reviews by the USEPA 
and National Advisory Committee for Acute Exposure 
Guideline Levels for Hazardous Substances (NAC/AEGL Committee) and TCEQ prepared by 
FMMI’s consultant, Ramboll is included as Attachment D. 
In addition, EPA’s risk evaluations for acute non-cancer arsenic risks in prior NESHAPs 
do not support its determination here that an HQ of 7 results in unacceptable risks. For example, 
in the prior NESHAP for Primary Aluminum Reduction Plants, EPA considered an acute non- 
cancer HQ for arsenic as high as 10 to be acceptable. See 80 Fed. Reg. 62390, 62398 (Oct. 15, 
2015) (finding that the “prebake subcategories are acceptable” notwithstanding an HQ of 10 based 
on arsenic emissions). The HQ of 7 estimated by EPA for the primary copper smelting NESHAP 
falls under the HQ previously approved for Primary Aluminum Reduction Plants. 
In summary, 
modeled short term emission impacts of arsenic for the primary copper smelting NESHAP do not 
support EPA’s conclusion that acute non-cancer risks are unacceptable under the RTR metrics. 
2. 
Based 
on 
a comprehensive 
analysis 
of both 
the monitoring 
and 
modeling data, there is no compelling evidence that current emissions 
threaten an exceedance of the lead NAAQS. 
EPA’s 3-month average lead modeling based upon estimates of actual emissions from the 
FMMI facility identified a maximum 3-month average for lead of 0.17 ug/m3 as compared to the 
primary NAAQS of 0.15 ug/m3, or a modeled value that exceeds the NAAQS by only 13%. See 
87 Fed. Reg. 1638. Moreover, that modeled exceedance applies only to a single census block or 
about 5 people in total. /d. The modeling results from similar runs using allowable emissions are 
somewhat higher, but still include maximum modeled values that only moderately exceed the lead 
NAAQS ina 
very limited area. /d. Again, these are modeled values based on emissions estimates 
+ TCEQ Fact Sheet General Information About Regulatory Guidelines (2010), available at 
https://www.tceq. texas. gov/assets/public/implementation/tox/dsd/facts/general.pdf 
5

impacting a very small area adjacent to the Miami smelter, and as is discussed below, the available 
ambient air monitoring data does not support an actual exceedance of the NAAQS. 
EPA acknowledges in the preamble that the lead NAAQS contains multiple conservative 
assumptions regarding risks from lead exposure and “includes an adequate margin of safety to be 
protective of all sub-populations 
at risk, especially children.” 
Jd. 
Similarly, 
in 2016, EPA 
reaffirmed its conclusion that the current lead NAAQS is conservative and health protective. 81 
Fed. Reg. 71906 (Oct. 18, 2016). As part of its analysis, the agency highlighted the uncertainty in 
drawing conclusions regarding potential health impacts from ambient air exposures at or close to 
the NAAQS level. Jd. at 71926 (recognizing the “uncertainty in characterizing the relationship of 
effects on IQ with blood Pb levels below those represented in the evidence base and also in 
projecting the magnitude of blood Pb response to ambient air Pb concentrations at and below the 
level of the current standard.”) Review of the most recent scientific literature on low dose lead 
exposures in children reinforces the very conservative nature of the current NAAQS. At the very 
low doses associated with the lead NAAQS, it is very difficult to isolate the impacts of the lead 
because of “confounding” where the measured association between an exposure variable and an 
outcome is distorted by an effect of a third variable. As discussed in the Ramboll toxicity memo 
included as Attachment D, 
a recent publication by Wilson and Wilson (2016) suggests that 
uncontrolled confounding by parental and household factors with several of the cohorts incorrectly 
support the association between health effects and low blood lead levels. They indicate that “while 
the effects of higher levels of lead exposure are not disputed, overestimation of health effects at 
low lead exposure has significant implications for policy-makers endeavoring to protect public 
health through cost-effective regulations.” Another study by Van Landingham et al. (2020) reaches 
a very similar conclusion. See Attachment D. 
FMMI supports EPA’s overall goal of reducing lead exposures of those children at greatest 
risk 
of adverse 
health 
effects, 
but 
the most 
recent 
science 
shows 
that 
ambient 
air 
lead 
concentrations generally equivalent to the existing lead NAAQS are fully health protective. As 
noted above, EPA has based its conclusion of unacceptable risk on modeled impacts in a small 
area that are roughly equivalent to lead NAAQS. This result contrasts sharply with other RTR 
NESHAP 
revisions where 
failure 
of the “acceptable 
risk” 
criteria hinged upon modeled 
exceedances of the lead NAAQS where those modeled exceedances prior to additional control 
were frequently 10 times or more the lead NAAQS. See e.g., National Emissions Standards for 
Hazardous Air Pollutants from Secondary Lead Smelting; Final Rules, 77 Fed. Reg. 556, 562-563 
& Table 4 (Jan. 5, 2012). 
In addition, as EPA notes in the preamble, there is ambient monitoring data for lead that is 
available for a monitor in close proximity to the FMMI facility (approximately 1400 meters away). 
See 
87 Fed. Reg. 
1640. This data reinforces the point that lead impacts do not cause an 
“unacceptable risk.” Specifically, EPA states that the annual average lead concentration at this 
monitoring site during 2019 was 0.022 ug/m3 or less than 15% of the NAAQS. 
EPA points to 
potentially higher results predicted based upon modeling, but again the modeling based on actual 
emissions only suggests a single NAAQS exceedance of 13% perhaps impacting 5 people. 
Moreover, in recent years FMMI installed facility-wide improvements to its air emissions 
control systems to ensure compliance with revised SO standards, but those modifications also 
improved metal HAP controls. All of these systems were operational by 2018. A review of a more 
6

complete set of the ambient monitoring data from this post-modification period reinforces the 
conclusion that there is no compelling evidence that there is 
a substantial likelihood of lead 
NAAQS exceedances in the vicinity of the current FMMI facility. For example, the annual average 
lead concentration at the monitor location for 2020 was 0.014 ug/m3, and the annual average 
concentration for 2021 was 0.019 ug/m3, both values well below the already low 2019 data cited 
by EPA in the preamble and approximately an order of magnitude below the NAAQS. Moreover, 
during the entirety of the last two years of data consisting of 119 discrete daily average readings, 
there is not a single daily mean lead concentration that exceeds the 3-month average lead NAAQS. 
Indeed, there are only three daily average readings over that 2-year period that even equal or 
exceed 50% of the 3-month average NAAQS. Finally, this measured drop 
in ambient lead 
concentrations has been accompanied by an increase in the overall production rate at the smelter. 
A 
plot 
of the 
rolling 3-month lead 
concentration average versus 
facility production 
rate 
demonstrates that lead ambient concentrations have dropped (particularly in the last two years) as 
production has increased. See plot below:

E,

EPA established the structure of the current RTR process as part of the Benzene NESHAP. 
In that rulemaking, EPA stated that it was not imposing “a rigid line for acceptability,” but rather 
an assessment that weighs a series of health measures and factors including “the number of persons 
exposed [at elevated risk]” and “estimation uncertainties associated with the risk measures.” 54 
Fed. Reg. 38044, 30845 (Sept. 
14, 
1989). Both metrics 
in this case caution against 
a rigid 
application of the NAAQS based on modeled results 
to conclude current emissions 
fail the 
“acceptable risk” criteria. As discussed above, the modeled NAAQS exceedances near the FMMI 
facility are small and isolated, and ambient monitoring data suggests those exceedances do not 
actually occur. 
EPA should conclude that risks based on current lead and arsenic emissions are acceptable. 
B. 
Regardless of whether additional controls are imposed, regulation of process 
fugitive emissions exiting from the roofline vents should be addressed via a 
single combined emission limit. 
The FMMI smelter building is a single structure that includes a smelting vessel (IsaSmelt 
furnace or “ISA”), slag cleaning vessel (electric furnace or “ELF”), a copper converter department, 
and an anode refining department. 
While there is some geographical separation between these 
affected sources, the explicit distinction drawn in the proposed rule between roofline vents based 
on their relative proximity to these affected sources is artificial because there is open air flow 
between these sources. In addition, there are related support processes between the affected sources 
such as matte ladle transfers and slag returns between the furnaces and the converters. Similarly, 
there are blister ladle transfers, anode slag returns, and ladle cleaning operations between the 
converters and anodes furnaces. Process fugitive emissions will therefore vary across the roofline 
vents based on the operating and transfer status of various processes. 
Accordingly, from an air 
quality regulatory perspective, it does not make sense to establish separate and distinct limits on 
individual roofline vents. 
Based upon the physical configuration and comingling of process fugitive emissions from 
affected sources and associated support processes, FMMI believes that process fugitive emissions 
exiting from the roofline vents should be regulated via a single combined emission limit that would 
be equal to the sum of the relevant UPLs less any emission reductions imposed by EPA’s residual 
risk and MACT technology review. To the extent that FMMI installs additional capture and control 
systems with discrete stacks to reduce existing process fugitive emissions (utilizing either existing 
or new control devices), the fPM emissions exiting such stacks would be subject to a 23 mg/dscm 
limit. 
See 
40 
CFR 
§§ 
63.1444(a)(2), 
63.1444(b)(2)(ii), 
63.1444(c)(3)(ii), 
63.1444(d)(6), 
63.1444(i)(1), and 63.1444(j)(2) as proposed in Table 1. 
This regulatory approach will allow FMMI to assess all of the affected sources contributing 
to process fugitive emissions exiting from the roofline vents and evaluate the most efficient 
combination of additional capture and control systems and potentially other process modifications 
to meet the new combined emission limit at the least cost and greatest online reliability. 
These 
issues and potential capture and control configurations are discussed in more detail in an Analysis 
of the Cost and Feasibility of Possible Control Strategies for Process Fugitive Emissions Exiting 
the Roofline Vents prepared by FMMI’s consultant, Gas Cleaning Technologies (“GCT”), 
included as Attachment F.

Because the process fugitive emissions currently exiting from the roofline vents are the 
primary drivers for EPA’s risk assessment conclusions, FMMI conducted a modeling exercise to 
demonstrate that the particular roofline vent where the emissions escape does not substantially 
change the magnitude of the offsite impact. 
Specifically, FMMI ran three modelling runs where 
each run modeled the entirety of the combined process fugitive emissions through one of the three 
roofline vents as modeled by EPA (e.g., converter, anode, and average of ISA and ELF).° The 
modeling confirms that the critical offsite impact areas are largely unchanged between the 
modeling runs. 
See Attachment G. Thus, establishing a combined emission limitation for the 
collective process fugitive emissions exiting all of the roofline vents instead of specific emission 
limitations for each roofline does not significantly impact the risk analysis. 
Cc; 
EPA’s preamble discussion of the FMMI process related to the process fugitive 
emissions 
exiting 
the 
roofline 
vents 
contains 
two 
important 
misunderstandings. 
1. 
EPA improperly combined and averaged the emissions exiting the ISA 
and ELF roofline vents. 
EPA’s MACT floor analysis of the roofline emissions data improperly averages the 
emission test results for two vessels that have distinct operations. The proposed rule Preamble 
states that FMMI has two smelting vessels (see 87 Fed. Reg. 1633) implicitly suggesting these two 
vessels have the same operating function, but this is not correct. 
FMMI operates an IsaSmelt 
furnace (ISA), which is the smelting vessel. In FMMI’s configuration, the electric furnace (ELF) 
receives material from the ISA and is a slag cleaning or slag settling vessel. While the two vessels 
refer to matte and slag tapping operations, each vessel (e.g., the ISA and ELF) has 
a distinct 
operating function and is considered a separate affected source under Subpart QQQ. Roofline 
sampling results are available for each independent operation. Rather than averaging the data for 
these two independent operations, the sum of the individual UPLs of those emission rates and the 
UPLs for the converter and anode roof line vents should be combined (e.g., four roofline locations) 
to create a single emission limit for all of the process fugitive emissions exiting the roofline vents. 
2. 
EPA erroneously describes the FMMI converters as having “primary 
and secondary capture systems and controls, but no tertiary controls.” 
FMMI’s Hoboken converters use a side-flue intake capture system and 
the roofline canopy system is properly described as a tertiary capture 
system. 
In its preamble discussion with regard to converters, EPA states that “Freeport has primary 
and secondary capture and controls, but no tertiary controls.” 
87 Fed. Reg. 1616, 1634 (Jan. 11, 
2022). To clarify, the Hoboken converters at FMMI’s copper converter department use a side-flue 
capture system. Only copper converter departments using Peirce-Smith converters have primary 
and secondary capture systems for purposes of Subpart QQQ. While these terms are not defined 
under Subpart QQQ, 40 C.F.R. § 63.1444(d)(2) requires that the capture system design for copper 
> Note that FMMI modeled the ISA and ELF as a single averaged emission point for this analysis in order to duplicate 
EPA’s modeling approach, but these two roof vents are separate emission points. FMMI has developed its combined 
roof vent UPL emissions calculations treating them as separate emission points. See Attachments F and H for 
additional discussion.

converter departments using Peirce-Smith converters “must include use of a primary hood that 
covers the entire mouth of the converter vessel when the copper converter is positioned for 
blowing” and “[a]dditional hoods (e.g., secondary hoods) or other capture devices 
. 
. 
. as needed 
to achieve the opacity limit in [40 C.F.R. 
§ 63.1444(d)(4)].” 
In contrast, for copper converter 
departments using Hoboken converters, 40 C.F.R. 
§ 63.1444(d)(3) specifies that “the capture 
system must collect all process off-gas vented during blowing through the side-flue intake on each 
converter vessel.” 
40 C.F.R. 
§ 63.1444(d)(5) then specifies 
a 6.2 mg/dscm non-sulfuric acid 
particulate matter limit for process off-gases that are discharged from “any Pierce-Smith converter 
primary hood capture system or Hoboken converter side-flue intake capture system.” The EPA 
has thus recognized in the existing primary copper smelter NESHAP the differences between the 
capture systems for Hoboken and Peirce-Smith converters. 
The initial Subpart QQQ rulemaking history confirms this understanding as the 4% opacity 
limit for copper converter department building roof monitors at 40 C.F.R. § 63. 1444(d)(4)(i) and 
the 23 mg/dscm particulate matter limit for secondary capture systems at 40 C.F.R. § 63.1444(d)(6) 
was derived based on the fixed or sliding secondary hoods and an air curtain secondary hood used 
to capture secondary or fugitive emissions that escape the primary capture system at smelters using 
Peirce-Smith converters. 
See EPA, National Emission Standards for Hazardous Air Pollutants 
(NESHAP) for Primary Copper Smelters — Background Information for Promulgated Standards, 
EPA-453/R-02-0003, at 2-18 and 3-6 (hereinafter “Background Information Document”). Out of 
the six smelters considered during the Subpart QQQ rulemaking, five used Pierce-Smith converters 
with a primary hood and a secondary hood or air curtain as the secondary capture system. See id. 
at Tables 3-1 and A-1. Secondary hoods are technically infeasible for Hoboken converters because 
of the way that off-gas exits these vessels. Therefore, no secondary (or tertiary) capture systems 
or controls were identified or required for the Phelps Dodge (now FMMI) copper converter 
department using Hoboken converters. As EPA concluded, “[b]ased on the opacity data and other 
information available to us for this smelter, we believe that the Phelps Dodge Miami smelter can 
meet the standards under the final rule without having to install additional air pollution control 
equipment.” Jd. at 6-2. 
While FMMI subsequently installed 
a new roofline canopy system in 2017 as part of 
facility-wide improvements to ensure the Miami area’s compliance with revised standards for 
sulfur dioxide (SO2), this system does not constitute a “secondary capture system” as described 
above for Peirce-Smith converters under Subpart QQQ. Instead, this system is akin to the El Paso 
smelter’s building evacuation system (which evacuated 
the entire converter building 
to 
a 
particulate control device) that EPA characterized as a tertiary capture system during the Subpart 
QQQ rulemaking. 
See 67 Fed. Reg. 40,478, 40,484 (June 12, 2002); Background Information 
Document, at Table A-1. While a building evacuation system would help ensure compliance with 
the 4% opacity standard, EPA did not establish any specific requirements for such 
a system. 
Indeed, EPA expressly considered and rejected a converter building evacuation system in the 
development of the Subpart QQQ opacity standard. 
See 67 Fed. Reg. 
at 40,486. 
As result, 
characterization of FMMI’s roofline canopy system (which is designed to capture process fugitive 
emissions from various sources that are then routed to a SO2 scrubber) as a tertiary capture system 
would be of no consequence under current Subpart QQQ requirements. 
10

D. 
The “Ample Margin of Safety” second step of the RTR process does not justify 
additional controls for non-cancer impacts of arsenic and lead. 
Unlike with cancer risks, EPA has not established risk-based thresholds under its “ample 
margin of safety” analysis for non-cancer impacts of arsenic and lead. As noted above, a protective 
ambient air threshold for arsenic that 
is supported by 
the available scientific literature on 
developmental health risks from arsenic inhalation exposures results in an HQ well below 1.° 
Similarly, EPA has described the lead NAAQS as “including an adequate margin of safety to be 
protective of all sub-populations at risk, especially children” (87 Fed. Reg. 1638), and there is no 
compelling evidence that existing FMMI lead emissions threaten a NAAQS exceedance. 
Accordingly, additional controls on the process fugitive emissions exiting the roofline 
vents must be justified (if at all) under the beyond the floor (“BTF”) MACT standard. 
E. 
The justification for additional controls of lead and arsenic is marginal when 
cost is considered under the BTF MACT standard. 
In the very limited time available between the publication of the proposed NESHAP 
revisions and the closure of the comment period, FMMI has done what it can to evaluate potential 
strategies to control the anode furnace roof line fugitives. 
As EPA notes in the rule preamble, 
there are multiple potential strategies to accomplish this control including (i) direct control of the 
roof vent, (ii) secondary hood controls within the building to capture emissions before they reach 
the roof vent, and (iii) some combination of both approaches. FMMI commissioned Gas Cleaning 
Technologies (“GCT”), 
an engineering company with extensive experience controlling {PM 
emissions in smelting facilities, to develop possible conceptual scopes and to provide a cost 
estimate for each. In all cases, a baghouse was used to remove the (PM. A copy of GCT’s analysis 
is included as Attachment F. 
In summary, both capital costs and operating costs for the proposed emission controls far 
exceed the estimates 
in EPA’s analysis. EPA estimated 
a capital cost of $5,887,000 and an 
annualized cost of $1,558,000 or a cost effectiveness value of $367,000 per ton of HAP reduction. 
GCT identified several problems with EPA’s cost estimate including under-sizing of flow volume 
required for dust capture, failure to provide for corrosion protection with a lime injection system, 
the need for a multi-compartment baghouse, and the cost associated with retrofitting such a large 
system into the limited space available. Taking these factors into account, GCT estimated that the 
capital cost of a system to control anode process fugitive emissions would be between $24,200,000 
and $65,600,000 (depending upon the design assumptions) and an annualized cost of between 
$4,653,000 and $11,594,000. 
These more realistic estimates yield a cost effectiveness range of 
$1,095,0000 to $2,728,000 per ton of HAP reduction. Even at the low end of this range, the cost 
effectiveness value is at the extreme upper end of the range that EPA has historically concluded 
justify additional controls for metal HAPs, and at the upper end would clearly be not cost effective 
based upon established EPA metrics. Moreover, FMMI has no confidence that the reduction of 4.5 
tons per year of metal HAPs based upon a 90% removal efficiency of the anode furnace roof line 
  
° FMMI notes that EPA concluded that an HQ of 2 using the California REL did not justify additional controls under 
its ample margin of safety analysis. See 87 Fed. Reg. 1643. 
11

fugitives is possible at the lower end of the cost range. The lower cost estimate is based exclusively 
on the use of secondary hooding over process operations. 
But it is not clear if the necessary level 
of gas capture is possible with secondary hooding, or if alternatively, some amount of canopy 
capture at the roofline may be required. Roofline canopy systems are much more expensive, and 
if necessary, will drive the costs higher than the cost effectiveness threshold. FMMI cannot answer 
these process scope questions without additional investigation and engineering that was not 
possible within the limited comment period. 
GCT’s engineering evaluation does suggest, however, that significant cost savings are 
possible if the 4.5 tons per year of metal HAP reductions are achieved based upon a combined 
limit for the process fugitive emissions exiting the roofline vents. This regulatory approach allows 
FMMI to design a system that maximizes the cost effectiveness of this additional control. For 
example, a combined limit makes it more likely that FMMI can utilize excess capacity in the anode 
furnace baghouse to control some portion of the process fugitive emissions. 
Use of the anode 
furnace baghouse has the added benefit that the vent gas is routed to the aisle scrubber, which 
provides SO> control.’ GCT states in its engineering analysis of the various approaches to reducing 
emissions of metal HAPs exiting from the roofline vents that it expects that a combined limit 
approach will prove to have the lowest cost of control for any of the available options. 
See 
Attachment F. 
As noted above, FMMI urges EPA to adopt this combined limit approach to the process 
fugitive emissions exiting the roofline vents both because this approach reflects the nature of the 
emissions and because this would be the most cost-effective way 
to achieve the proposed 
emissions reductions. FMMI’s consultant, Ramboll, calculated an UPL-based combined emission 
limit for fPM of 6.5 lbs/hr. 
This combined limit is based upon the same 90% reduction in the 
anode roofline emissions as proposed by EPA and is accordingly functionally equivalent to EPA’s 
proposal. Ramboll used the same statistical procedure that EPA used to calculate the proposed 
facility-wide mercury limit based upon a 90% reduction in the aisle scrubber. See Ramboll’s UPL 
Calculation for the Combined Limit for Process Fugitive Emissions Exiting the Roofline Vents, 
included as Attachment H. 
F. 
Even if additional controls on process fugitive emissions exiting the roofline 
vents are warranted, EPA should permit the three-year compliance period 
applicable under 42 U.S.C. § 7412(i)(3). 
Congress established two different timeframes for compliance with NESHAP revisions: 
the default period of 3 years, 42 U.S.C. § 7412(i)(3), and a shorter 2-year compliance period for 
revisions necessary to protect public health. /d. 
at 
§ 7412(f(4)(B). 
The proposed NESHAP 
revisions include a two-year compliance period for existing anode refining department roofline 
vents subject to new limits assuming the adoption of additional controls based on EPA’s Section 
7412(f) finding that the risks from arsenic and lead are unacceptable. However, as demonstrated 
above, the appropriate inquiry for additional controls for the anode refining department is under 
7 Similarly, a combined emission limit approach will provide more opportunities for process and feed modifications 
that have the potential to reduce process fugitive emissions. See EPA preamble discussion at 87 Fed. Reg. 1635 
12

Section 7412(d)(2) and (3). As a result, in the event that EPA concludes additional control of these 
process fugitive emissions is appropriate then a three-year compliance period should apply. 
There are also significant practical reasons for 
a three-year compliance period. 
As 
described above the design and engineering of the most cost efficient and reliable combination of 
control options to achieve the targeted emission reductions is complex. It involves multiple time 
consuming steps to (i) assess the emission sources, (ii) evaluate the potential control options for 
effectiveness, reliability and cost, (iii) design engineering of the final scope, (iv) procurement in a 
current environment where supply issues are common, (v) construction, and finally (vi) startup. It 
will be challenging to accomplish this in three years, much less two. Please see the GCT Analysis 
of Roofline Vent Control Strategies included as Attachment F for additional discussion of these 
issues. 
Ill. 
The proposed mercury emissions limitations are not justified based on either BTF 
MACT considerations or RTR risk analysis, and if implemented will not work in 
anything approaching a cost effective manner. 
A. 
EPA has not demonstrated a risk-based justification for additional mercury 
controls, and as a result, any additional mercury controls must be justified 
based on a BITF MACT technology-based standard. 
In the proposed NESHAP revisions, EPA calculated for the first time a facility wide MACT 
floor for mercury emissions of 0.01 lbs/hr, and then proposed further reductions from the MACT 
floor based on a BTF analysis to 0.0043 Ibs/hr.’ See 87 Fed. Reg. 1636. EPA’s technology-based 
analysis identified activated carbon injection and a polishing baghouse as available technology 
that is capable of achieving this emission reduction. Nevertheless, in justification for this new 
standard, EPA states: “EPA is proposing this BTF limit for mercury because mercury is a highly 
toxic, persistent and bioaccumulative HAP. 
. 
. .” Jd. But EPA conducted a risk analysis as part of 
this rulemaking that specifically addressed mercury emissions. /d. at 1629. As part of this analysis, 
EPA did not identify any mercury emissions that did not meet its “acceptable risk” threshold or as 
a basis for an “ample margin of safety” analysis. /d. 
at 1642-44. Thus, to the extent that new 
mercury emissions controls are appropriate, they must be justified based on the technology-based 
BTF analysis that includes consideration of both cost and feasibility. 
B. 
The proposed new mercury controls (¢.g., activated carbon injection in the 
acid plant tail gas with a polishing baghouse) are not used by any facility in 
this source category and are unsuited for the process conditions at issue. 
FMM is not aware of any primary copper smelters that successfully utilize an activated 
carbon injection into the acid plant tail gas followed by a polishing baghouse to remove mercury. 
EPA has not identified such a facility in its preamble and the only relevant citation to the docket 
is EPA’s “Estimated Costs for Beyond-the-Floor Controls for Mercury Emissions from Primary 
Copper Smelting Facilities” (Docket ID No. EPA-HQ-OAR-2020-0430-0059). 
But this analysis 
S EPA also proposed a mercury limit for new converter facilities of 0.00097 Ibs/hr of mercury based upon emissions 
measured from the Asarco facility. 
But this is an inappropriate basis to set a new facility emission limit because the 
Asarco facility feedstock simply does not contain mercury, and as a result, mercury controls are unnecessary. 
13

addresses only cost, and simply assumes a 90% mercury control efficiency of the acid plant tail 
gas. The document does not contain a citation or other explanation regarding how EPA derived 
that control efficiency for this unique application. Similarly, EPA’s Technology Review for the 
Primary Copper Smelting Source Category (Docket ID No. EPA-HQ-OAR-2020-0430-0033) did 
not identify activated carbon and a polishing baghouse as available technology to control mercury 
emissions from the acid plant tail gas. 
Review of chemistry and physical conditions of the acid plant tail gas and the mechanism 
for mercury removal with activated carbon identifies multiple problems with EPA’s choice of 
technology and assumption regarding its efficiency. First, the acid plant tail gas is a wet gas stream. 
Condensation of water on the activated carbon will effectively block the pores of the carbon and 
severely restrict its ability to adsorb mercury (the vast majority of adsorption sites are within the 
pores). Second, the tail gas contains residual SO2 and SO; in higher concentrations than the trace 
mercury content, and these compounds will compete for adsorption sites and inhibit mercury 
removal. 
Finally, the tail gas mercury concentration is very low (<10 ug/dscm) and activated 
carbon systems are generally not efficient at removing mercury at these very low concentrations. 
All of these factors have been shown in other applications to significantly reduce mercury removal 
efficiency. FMMI retained GCT (an engineering consultant with specialized expertise in air 
emissions abatement projects like this) to evaluate the potential effectiveness of an activated 
carbon system to remove mercury from the acid plant tail gas. GCT’s conclusion was that even 
an optimized activated carbon system was likely to achieve only a removal efficiency of 50 to 
75%. See GCT Mercury Analysis at Attachment I. 
In addition, a tail gas mercury removal system poses significant material of construction 
and reliability problems. Condensation of the tail gas results in an acidic condensate that can 
corrode duct work, the baghouse, and the ID fan as well as blinding the filter bags and impeding 
flow. These conditions reduce system reliability, increase maintenance costs, and ultimately 
adversely impact mercury removal. 
As discussed by GCT, management of the acidic gas causes 
increased production costs associated with more expensive materials of construction and the 
addition of a gas treatment system. 
In summary, activated carbon injection with 
a polishing 
baghouse on the acid tail gas is not a proven technology in this application, and engineering 
analysis suggests this technology would result in poor mercury removal efficiency, poor reliability, 
and excessive maintenance costs. 
Please see the GCT Analysis of the Cost and Feasibility of 
Possible Control Strategies for Mercury Emissions from the Tail Gas Scrubber Vent included as 
Attachment 
I for additional discussion of these issues. 
Cc. 
Additional controls of mercury on the acid tail gas are not justified when cost 
and feasibility are considered under the BTF MACT standard. 
An activated carbon system will be much more expensive than EPA has estimated, and will 
have 
a much higher cost effectiveness per pound of mercury removed. FMMI’s analysis of 
activated carbon control of mercury emissions from the acid plant tail gas demonstrates that (i) 
this technology is unlikely to meet the efficiency assumptions supporting the proposed NESHAP 
revisions (resulting in fewer pounds of mercury removed), and (ii) both capital costs and operating 
14

costs for the proposed emission controls far exceed the estimates in EPA’s analysis. Specifically, 
the capital cost to install an activated carbon system to remove mercury from the acid plant tail 
gas would equal $16,450,000. Combined with expected operating costs, the activated carbon 
system is expected to have an annualized cost of $4,432,000. 
Moreover, it is very unlikely that 
the activated carbon system could achieve the proposed 90% mercury removal rate given the 
process conditions and the very low inlet mercury concentrations. Assuming a more realistic 50- 
75% removal efficiency, the cost effectiveness value 
is $202,600 
to $303,900 per pound of 
mercury removed. 
These values are close to an order of magnitude higher than the EPA’s cost 
effectiveness threshold of $27,500 per pound. 
Even if the system is capable of a 90% removal rate (which for the reasons set out above 
is unlikely), the cost effectiveness value would be $168,800 per pound of mercury removed. See 
GCT Mercury Analysis at Attachment I. Even this value based on an unrealistic control efficiency 
is more than 6 times higher than the acceptable cost of control for mercury. 
Moreover, it is higher 
than the cost effectiveness determinations in other recent NESHAP decisions where additional 
mercury 
controls 
were 
considered 
not 
cost 
effective. 
See 
e.g., 
Integrated 
Iron 
& 
Steel 
Manufacturing NESHAP ($136,000 per Ibs. of Hg not cost effective) (84 Fed. Reg. 42704, 42732 
(Aug. 16, 2019)); Ferroalloys — SiMn Production ($109,000 per Ibs. of Hg not cost effective) (79 
Fed. Reg. 60238, 60263 (Oct. 6, 2014)); Gold Mine Ore Processing ($100,000 per Ibs. of Hg not 
cost effective) (75 Fed. Reg. 22470, 22483 (Apr. 28, 2010)). 
GCT also considered other potential mercury removal technologies. For example, GCT 
evaluated the application of the Boliden Norzink process (“BN Process”). 
Based on GCT’s 
analysis, the BN Process (like activated carbon) performs poorly at low mercury concentrations 
with estimated removal efficiencies of 50 to 85%. The cost of control, even assuming an unlikely 
90% removal efficiency, would be $148,000 per pound of mercury removed. 
Other mercury 
control technologies considered by GCT were incompatible with the acid plant tail gas process 
conditions 
and/or 
had 
insufficient 
control 
efficiencies 
at 
the 
already 
low 
inlet 
mercury 
concentrations present in the acid plant tail gas. See GCT Mercury Analysis at Attachment I. 
In summary, a cost-effective technology that is capable of removing 90% of the mercury 
in the acid plant tail gas simply does not exist, and as a result, EPA’s proposed mercury controls 
are not justifiable as additional beyond the floor controls. 
Dz. 
EPA’s calculation of mercury MACT floor does not include all of the relevant 
mercury emissions data and underreports the UPL. 
EPA’s calculation of the MACT floor for mercury is based upon a very small dataset 
collected using a sampling methodology that did not include the complete EPA Method 29 trains 
designed to capture mercury emissions (only one potassium permanganate impinger was used). 
Thus, the sample results likely under-report mercury emissions to some unknown degree resulting 
in an artificially low UPL. Moreover, additional sample runs at the FMMI facility are available for 
2020 and 2021 (albeit with the incomplete permanganate trains) that were not included in EPA’s 
15

UPL analysis, but nevertheless show substantially more variability in the acid plant tail gas.° For 
these reasons, the current data set is not sufficiently robust without additional validation to support 
the creation of a MACT floor. 
EPA calculated the MACT floor emission limit for facility-wide mercury emissions using 
a very limited test data set for the FMMI and Asarco facilities. Specifically, Appendix A of EPA’s 
MACT floor memorandum!” lists total facility mercury data for just five test runs, two for Asarco 
in 2019 (sum of five sampling locations) and three for FMMI in 2018 (sum of all three stacks 
tested). FMMI subsequently conducted additional tests in 2020 and 2021 in which all three stacks 
were tested, producing six additional test runs for total facility emissions. The 2020 (previously 
provided to EPA) and 2021 test reports are provided as appendices to Attachment J. Adding these 
results to EPA’s data set results in a slightly more robust data set (11 test runs, still statistically 
small) and would raise the UPL by 80%, from 0.01 
(as reported by EPA in the MACT floor 
memorandum) to 0.018 lb/hr. This clearly demonstrates that the limited data set used by EPA to 
establish the proposed facility-wide mercury emissions limit 
is too limited 
to represent an 
achievable MACT floor emission limit. Additional details are provided in Ramboll’s analysis of 
the Limitations of the EPA Data Set for Calculating a MACT Floor for Mercury included as 
Attachment J. 
FMMI did not measure mercury emissions to demonstrate performance relative to any 
established mercury emissions limits. Instead, the test was designed for compliance testing for 
lead. Testing for other metals, including mercury, was included for emissions estimating purposes. 
The tests generally followed EPA Method 29, but the sampling train only used one impinger 
containing potassium permanganate absorbing solution instead of the two impingers specified by 
EPA methods (see Attachment K, Method 29 Modifications for FMMI Mercury Emissions Tests). 
The potassium permanganate solution captures elemental mercury, much of which passes through 
the nitric acid/hydrogen peroxide absorbing solution in the impingers upstream. Two impingers in 
series with each absorbing solution are used to assure complete mercury capture within the sample 
train. As a result of this modification, an unknown portion of the mercury in the stack gas sample 
may not have been captured within the sampling train. Therefore, the test results, and the resultant 
UPL, are biased low by an unknown amount. Consequently, it is unlikely the facility-wide mercury 
emission limit could be achieved when the full Method 29 sampling train is used with the same 
degree of confidence and coverage assumed when calculating the UPL. Additional details are 
provided in Attachment K. 
For these reasons, the data set upon which the UPL is based is insufficient to support the 
calculated MACT 
floor, and additional 
testing and analysis 
of the fluctuations 
in mercury 
emissions is necessary before a representative MACT floor can be determined. 
° Additional uncertainty in the data set comes from known fluctuations 
in the mercury content of the copper 
concentrate. As acknowledged by EPA in the preamble, mercury emissions are largely driven by the mercury content 
in concentrate and other feedstocks. See 87 Fed. Reg. 1636. This means not only that mercury levels can differ from 
facility to facility but also for the same facility over time, as these facilities each use ore from multiple mines, and the 
ore composition at a given mine can fluctuate over time as different sections of the orebody are mined.

0055.
