IV. 
Many of the proposed new work practice standards have no application to operations 
at the FMMI smelter and are unlikely to improve control of metal HAPs. 
The new work practice standards addressing fugitive dust sources included in the Proposed 
NESHAP Revisions (40 CFR §§ 63.1445(b)(1) and (7) & 63.1445(e)) were taken from the 2015 
Asarco Consent Decree. These specific provisions were negotiated between that facility and 
ADEQ for control of dust emissions 
at that particular facility. FMMI and Asarco smelting 
operations are not configured identically. For example, FMMI feed materials are delivered directly 
to covered storage areas and transferred primarily by conveyor belt directly into bins that feed the 
smelter. In the infrequent event that in-plant haulage of concentrates does occur, a large end dump 
truck is utilized to efficiently move feed materials 
in fewer loads. As 
a result, tarping these 
infrequent in-plant transfers is both impractical and unsafe. Further, the speed limit requirements 
are overly prescriptive and do not account for the variability in the type and frequency of traffic 
on particular roads. Similarly, there is no indication that daily sweeping or 6-week applications of 
chemical dust suppressants is necessary at the FMMI facility. Proposed 40 CFR § 63.1445(b)(7) 
is simply overly prescriptive and does not adequately compensate for significant differences 
between how the different smelters manage feedstocks and other solid materials. 
The management of fugitive dust sources of the type addressed by these work practice 
standards depend on how feedstocks are stored, the type of loading and unloading systems used, 
the layout of the facility and distances travelled, and other facility-specific details. These are site- 
specific issues that require site-specific solutions. The most efficient and effective way to address 
these fugitive dust concerns is through the site’s fugitive dust control plan that, pursuant to 
§ 
63.1445(c), must identify the control measures used to control fugitive dust emissions from each 
source addressed in the plan “as applicable and appropriate for your site conditions.” Furthermore, 
pursuant to 
§ 63.1445(e), that fugitive dust control plan must be approved by the delegated 
authority every five years. FMMI therefore requests that the overly prescriptive (and duplicative) 
requirements in § 63.1445(b)(1) and (7) be eliminated, and the agency utilize the requirements of 
§ 63.1445(c) and (e) to ensure that each facility implements applicable and appropriate measures 
to control fugitive dust emissions via a site-specific fugitive dust control plan. 
V. 
Compliance demonstration, performance test methods, and operating parameter 
limit establishment. 
A. 
Performance Test Methods 
FMMI engaged Ramboll to evaluate the specific performance test methods and procedures 
set forth in the Proposed NESHAP Revisions for purposes of demonstrating compliance with new 
emissions limits. Testing the vent streams addressed by these revisions will create unique 
challenges. For example, as noted above, the anode baghouse must be analyzed as a combined 
process stream at the aisle scrubber, but data collection of the inputs to the combined gas stream 
assessment process under 40 CFR § 63.1446 will be a challenge. Similarly, it is not possible to 
collect test data from the roofline vents using EPA Methods 
1, 2/2F/2G, 3/3A/3B, 4 and 5D (or 
listed alternatives) as proposed by EPA. For this reason, FMMI utilized a sampling methodology 
and test protocol negotiated with the Arizona Department of Environmental Quality (*ADEQ”) 
specific 
to 
this unique situation when measuring emissions 
exiting roofline vents. FMMI 
recognizes that the proposed NESHAP revisions contain provisions for the use of alternate 
17

methods (see e.g., 
§ 63.1450(a)(2)). In this instance, “alternate” methods are the only option 
because those methods in the proposal are technically infeasible and FMMI stresses that the only 
way to provide regulatory certainty and facilitate compliance assurance is if an alternate, and 
realistic, test method 
is developed as part of the NESHAP rule revisions. Moreover, FMMI 
believes that the appropriate test method for the roofline vents should be based on the existing 
methodology developed with ADEQ. Importantly, this is the same methodology that generated the 
emissions data upon which the rule revisions are based. 
Additional discussion of these issues, 
including 
a preliminary outline of a proposed test methodology, 
is contained in the Ramboll 
assessment that is included as Attachment L. 
B. 
Operating Parameter Limit Establishment 
EPA’s 
November 
4, 
2021 
redline 
revisions 
include 
requirements 
at 
40 
CFR 
§ 
63.1450(d)(3) for the establishment (and re-establishment) of operating parameter limits for 
mercury emission control devices during each performance test based on the lowest or highest 
hourly average value of the relevant parameter measured in any of the three hour-long test runs. 
While the prescribed approach for establishing operating parameter limits is modeled after existing 
language in Subpart QQQ for establishing operating parameter limits for venturi wet scrubbers 
and other control devices 
for particulate matter, 
the approach for such control devices 
is 
fundamentally flawed because it does not allow for the establishment of a limit (range or maximum 
or minimum value) that reflects the designed and expected range of operating parameter values 
that are indicative of proper control device operation and performance. 
Because the current approach requires the operating parameter limit to be set at an hourly 
average value (even if the lowest or highest measured value during 
a performance test), the 
operating parameter level will inherently be above or below that value approximately 50% of the 
time the control device is operating. As a result, maintaining the operating parameter value above 
or below an hourly average limit established during a single performance test is not necessarily a 
representative and reliable indicator of the control device’s operation and performance. 
For 
example, consider a scrubber with 
a manufacturer’s recommended operating specification for 
pressure drop between 3.0 in. WC and 6.0 in. WC. Further consider that hourly average pressure 
drop values measured during each run of a performance test were relatively consistent at 4.5 in. 
WC, 4.6 in. WC, and 4.7 
in. WC. 
If the lowest measured hourly average was selected, the 
operating parameter limit for pressure drop would be no lower than 4.5 in. WC. 
As a result, 
subsequent 
operations 
at 
hourly average pressure 
drop 
values 
within 
the manufacturer’s 
recommended 
operating 
range, 
but 
below 
the 
4.5 
in. WC 
limit, 
would 
be 
considered 
noncompliance. 
Similarly, the scrubber could be operating in compliance with the operating 
parameter limit 
(i.e., no lower than 4.5 
in. WC), but 
at levels above 
the manufacturer’s 
recommended 
operating 
range 
that 
could 
be 
reflective 
of operational problems 
or 
poor 
performance of the scrubber. Accordingly, the overly simplistic and rigid approach to establishing 
operating parameter limits based solely on hourly average values measured during 
a single 
performance test does not adequately account for the expected range of pressure drop values under 
a control device’s design. 
FMMI therefore requests that EPA revise the Subpart QQQ requirements for establishing 
operating parameter limits for venturi scrubbers and other control devices to allow for other 
information to be considered in the selection of a limit (a range or single maximum or minimum 
18

value) that is indicative of proper operation and performance of the control device. This approach 
has been adopted under other NESHAP regulations (see e.g., 40 CFR § 63.1366(b)(3)(ii)(B) under 
Subpart MMM) where performance test data is supplemented by engineering assessments and/or 
manufacturer’s recommendations. 
Similar to the existing Subpart QQQ provisions for selecting 
operating parameters 
“as appropriate 
for 
the 
control 
device design, 
that 
can 
be 
used 
as 
representative and reliable indicators 
of the control device operation,” the selection of the 
associated operating parameter limit could require written documentation to support the selection. 
Such documentation could include manufacturer’s specifications 
or recommendations, EPA 
technical guidance, generally available air pollution control engineering manuals, source-specific 
engineering assessments, and/or observations of operating parameter values measured via a series 
of performance tests. This approach would be similar to the criteria for designing an appropriate 
range 
or designated 
condition 
for 
a selected performance indicator under the Compliance 
Assurance Monitoring Rule at 40 CFR § 64.3(a)(3) and would allow for the selection of a more 
representative single maximum or minimum value (e.g., minimum scrubber liquid flow rate) or a 
range (€.g., minimum to maximum pressure drop across a scrubber) as appropriate. 
Suggested revisions to the requirements for establishing operating parameter limits for 
venturi wet scrubbers and other control devices at 40 CFR §§ 63.1450(a)(4) and 63.1450(a)(5) are 
provided in Table 
1 
for EPA’s consideration. Suggested conforming revisions to reflect these 
changes are also provided for the associated provisions at 40 C.F.R. §§ 63.1444(g), 63.1444(h), 
63.1446(d), 63.1446(e), 63.1450(e)(4) and 
(5), 63.1451(g), 63.1451(k)(3), 63.1453(a)(1)(ii), 
63.1453(d)(1), and 63.1453(e)(1). 
Note that for purposes of supporting 
a selected operating 
parameter limit, the CAM Rule (at 40 C.F.R. 
§ 64.3(c)(1)) states that “[e]mission testing is not 
required to be conducted over the entire indicator range or range of potential emissions.” Similar 
language is provided under the NESHAP Subpart MMM example referenced above at 40 CFR § 
63.1336(b)(3)(i)(B). Furthermore, the CAM Rule recognizes that performance testing may be 
unnecessary where an indicator range is appropriately based on engineering assessments or other 
data (see 40 C.F.R. § 64.3(d)(2)). This understanding is incorporated in the suggested revisions for 
EPA’s consideration. 
Finally, there are a number of other ambiguities and practical impediments to the Proposed 
NESHAP 
Revisions addressing compliance demonstration 
and 
related 
issues. 
FMMI has 
addressed these issues in Table | (attached) including suggested regulatory language to resolve the 
particular issue. 
VI. 
— EPA’s comparison of the anticipated cost of the proposed NESHAP revisions to the 
revenues of FMMI’s ultimate parent FCX is inapposite, and does not provide any 
meaningful information on the economic impact of the revisions on FMMI. 
As is explained at the beginning of this letter, FCX is FMMI’s ultimate parent company 
with other subsidiaries operating copper and molybdenum mines, copper smelters and refineries, 
and other processing facilities throughout the world. FMMI accounts for only a modest part of 
both the value and the revenues of the much larger FCX group of companies. 
To assess the 
economic impact of the proposed NESHAP revisions on FMMI’s business and ability to continue 
to operate—the cost of the revisions must be measured against FMMI’s economic and other 
performance metrics. FMMI’s continued operation depends on its individual performance—not 
the revenues of its parent from other operations. EPA’s assumption that “parent companies [will] 
19

shift resources across their operations to address regulatory compliance needs” does not reflect 
modern business decision making. See 87 Fed. Reg. 1651 (essentially arguing the parents will 
subsidize underperforming subsidiaries). 
FMMI is not a publicly traded company and does not have publicly available audited 
financial statements. Nevertheless, there is no doubt that the proposed NESHAP revisions will 
cause significant financial challenges for FMMI. As discussed above, EPA’s cost estimates 
uniformly underestimate the expected cost of compliance. The ultimate outcome, of course, 
depends on the final provisions of the revised NESHAP, but to suggest that this rule has only minor 
economic consequences for FMM1I is plainly incorrect. Indeed, if it becomes uneconomical for 
FMMI to process copper concentrates and the facility shuts down, then the United States would 
be nearly completely dependent on non-domestic copper smelting capacity. Nearly all of the 
smelting production of this critical metal would be overseas. Moreover, copper concentrates from 
the U.S. mines would then likely be sent overseas with the associated increase in transportation 
related carbon dioxide and other emissions. 
VII. 
Enforcement discretion 
for malfunction 
events 
following removal 
of the SSM 
exemption in Sections 63.6(f)(1) and 63.6(h)(1) 
FMMI believes that EPA should include in the rule itself the conditions under which it 
would exercise its enforcement discretion in response to malfunctions. 
It is true that judicial 
precedents 
and 
the Agency’s 
policies 
prohibit automatic 
or discretionary exemptions 
and 
affirmative defenses in section 112 standards. See, e.g., NRDC v. EPA, 749 F.3d 1055 (D.C. Cir. 
2014); Sierra Club 
v, EPA, 551 F.3d 1019 (D.C. Cir. 2008). See also, “EPA’s SSM Policy 
Applicable to SIPs,” 80 Fed. Reg. 33840 (June 12, 2015) (“2015 SSM Policy”). Nevertheless, the 
2015 SSM Policy expressly allows state implementation plans (“SIPs”) to include regulatory 
provisions describing the circumstances under which a state will exercise enforcement discretion 
to address noncompliance. See 
80 Fed. Reg. 
at 33981. Because SIPs are state rules, these 
enforcement discretion provisions may apply only to the exercise of enforcement discretion by the 
state, and cannot purport to bind the exercise of enforcement discretion by EPA or citizen 
plaintiffs. See id. 
Courts have approved 
a similar formulation 
in section 
112 rules under which EPA 
announced that its own enforcement discretion would be exercised in consideration of various 
factors. See, e.g., United States Sugar Corp. 
v. EPA, 830 F.3d 579, 607-608 (D.C. Cir. 2016) 
(accepting EPA’s statement in the preamble to the major boilers rule as to how it would exercise 
case-by-case 
enforcement 
discretion 
in 
a 
way 
that 
would 
not 
prohibit 
citizen 
plaintiff 
enforcement). In the present proceeding, EPA reiterated its enforcement discretion policy in the 
preamble to the proposed rule. 
In the unlikely event that a source fails to comply with the applicable CAA section 
112(d) standards as a result of a malfunction event, the EPA would determine an 
appropriate response based on, among other things, the good faith efforts of the 
source to minimize emissions during malfunction periods, including preventative 
and corrective actions, as well as root cause analyses to ascertain and rectify excess 
emissions. The EPA would also consider whether the source's failure to comply 
with 
the CAA section 
112(d) 
standard was, 
in 
fact, 
sudden, 
infrequent, 
not 
20

reasonably preventable, and was not instead caused, in part, by poor maintenance 
or careless operation. 40 CFR 63.2 (Definition of malfunction). 
87 Fed. Reg. 1616, 1647 (Jan. 11, 2022). 
FMMI recommends that EPA blend the approaches from its 2015 SSM Policy and its past 
section 112 rulemaking practice, and include enforcement discretion conditions in the final rule 
text 
itself. Especially given that the proposal replaces the requirement to prepare 
a written 
malfunction response plan with a fairly generic good air pollution control practices provision in 
proposed section 63.1447(a), FMMI believes 
that articulating EPA enforcement discretion 
conditions in the rule itself will result in a more informed facility-level response in case of a 
malfunction. The following elements from the 2015 SSM Policy could be included in the final rule 
as factors EPA would consider in exercising its enforcement discretion: 
(1) To the maximum extent practicable the 
air pollution control equipment, process 
equipment or processes were maintained and operated in a manner consistent with good 
practice for minimizing emissions. 
(2) Repairs were made in an expeditious fashion when the operator knew or should have 
known that applicable emission limitations were being exceeded. Off-shift labor and 
overtime were utilized, to the extent practicable, to ensure that such repairs were made as 
expeditiously as practicable. 
(3) The amount and duration of the excess emissions (including any bypass) were 
minimized to the maximum extent practicable during periods of such emissions. 
(4) All possible steps were taken to minimize the impact of the excess emissions on ambient 
air quality. 
(5) The excess emissions are not part of a recurring pattern indicative of inadequate design, 
operation or maintenance. 
Alternatively, FMMI requests that EPA include this more detailed statement of how it 
would exercise its enforcement discretion in the preamble to the final rule. 
VIII. 
FMMI responses for miscellaneous areas where EPA has requested comments 
A. 
An additional opacity limit or lead limit on the roofline vents is not necessary. 
As part of EPA’s discussion regarding emission limits on process fugitive emissions 
exiting the roofline vents, the agency solicited comments on whether opacity limits should also be 
included as part of the MACT floor. See 87 Fed. Reg. 1634. FMMI agrees with EPA’s conclusion 
that additional opacity limits are unnecessary. The addition of opacity limits will not result in the 
reduction of facility emissions, and will not impact the risk analysis that is driving the agency’s 
RTR analysis. As noted above, the off-site ambient monitoring data for lead demonstrates that the 
FMMI facility does a good job of consistently managing its metal HAP emissions. Process fugitive 
emissions will be adequately controlled for metal HAPs via fPM limits. 
21

EPA’s November 4, 2021 
redline revisions also suggested opacity 
as 
a site-specific 
operating parameter limit for roof vents. 
As explained in Table 1, operating parameter limits are 
not appropriate for the smelter building roofline vents because they are not control devices or the 
underlying affected sources. Roof vents (i.e., emission points) do not warrant the establishment of 
operating parameter limits and associated operating parameter monitoring as specified for control 
devices to provide a reasonable assurance that the control device is being operated and maintained 
properly 
(i.e., 
limits 
that 
are “representative and 
reliable indicators 
of the control 
device 
performance”). As proposed by FMML, site-specific operating parameter limits on control devices 
used to control process fugitive emissions that otherwise might escape the smelter building roofline 
vents would be required. 
EPA also solicited comments regarding whether 
a lead limit should be added to (or 
substituted for) the fPM limit on the roofline vents. See 87 Fed. Reg. 1635. Again, FMMI agrees 
with EPA’s conclusion that {PM is the most appropriate surrogate for metal HAPs in this service. 
For reasons that are similar to the opacity discussion above, there is no justification for adding an 
additional control parameter when fPM is adequate. 
B. 
Beyond-the-floor 
controls 
on 
the anode 
refining 
point 
sources 
are 
not 
necessary. 
FMMI agrees with EPA’s conclusion that beyond-the-floor reductions in metal HAPs are 
not cost effective and should not be included in the NESHAP rule revisions. 
See 87 Fed. Reg. 
1633. These emissions are already controlled with a state-of-the-art baghouse. 
Additional fPM 
controls would be extremely expensive and result in only very minimal emissions reductions. As 
EPA has indicated in the preamble, the cost effectiveness of additional {PM controls (estimated at 
$8.7 million per ton of metal HAP reduced) is at least two orders of magnitude higher than prior 
thresholds for cost effectiveness for this type of pollutant. 
CG 
Additional controls for {PM on the aisle scrubber are not necessary under an 
ample margin of safety analysis. 
EPA solicited comments regarding whether additional metal HAP reductions to the aisle 
scrubber were justified under an ample margin of safety analysis. See 87 Fed. Reg. 1643. FMMI 
agrees with EPA’s conclusion that additional metal HAP limits on the aisle scrubber are not 
necessary. 
As set out above, FMMI proposes that the aisle scrubber would be subject to 
a 
concentration-based fPM limit of 23 mg/dscm similar to other vents processing emissions from 
the vessels managing molten material. Moreover, existing MACT floor emissions from the aisle 
scrubber do not significantly contribute to the estimated risks from metal HAPs. 
As the agency 
found, any PM control on the aisle scrubber would result 
in “high estimated capital costs, 
uncertainties, and moderate risk reductions.” Jd. Accordingly, FMMI does not believe that 
additional {PM limitations on the aisle scrubber beyond the 23 mg/dscm are necessary or 
appropriate.
